Quantum K K -theory for flag varieties
Sybille Rosset

Abstract




Summary

We present here an overview of the different results presented in this thesis. Chapters
2
and
3
use some of the results of Chapter
1
, but are otherwise independent and each of them may be read separately.

0.0.1
Chapter
1
: Gromov-Witten varieties of partial flag varieties.

Let
n\geq 0
, let
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{M}\}
be a set of
M
integers satisfying
0<j_{1}<\dots<j_{M}<n
. Consider the flag variety
X=Fl_{J}
parametrizing the flags of vector spaces of type

V_{j_{1}}\subset V_{j_{2}}\subset\dots\subset V_{j_{M}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n},

where
V_{j_{k}}
is a vector subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of dimension
j_{k}
. For an element
{\mathbf{d}}
in the semi-group
E(X)\subset H_{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
of effective classes of curves, we denote by
{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
the space of maps
(f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\})
with
r
marked points
p_{i}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
satisfying
f_{*}[{\mathbb{P}}^{1}]={\mathbf{d}}
. Associating to a map the image of its marked points yields an evaluation morphism
ev_{\mathbf{d}}:{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X^{r}
.
For
r=3
and
X
a cominuscule variety, and in particular for
X
a Grassmannian, Chaput-Perrin proved that the general fiber of the evaluation map is a rational variety
[
CP11a
]
. For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, Pandharipande computed the genus of the general fiber of the evaluation map
ev_{d}:{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2},d)\rightarrow({\mathbb{P}}^{2})^{r}
when this fiber has dimension
1

[
Pan97
]
, genus which turns out to be positive for
d\geq 3
. For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, examples of values of
d
such that the general fiber of
ev_{d}:{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},d)\rightarrow({\mathbb{P}}^{1})^{r}
is not a rationally connected variety have been obtained using techniques from quantum
K
-theory
[
IMT14
]
. One might wonder what happens for a variety of Picard rank greater than one. Chapter
1
provides a step in that direction by studying the geometry of the general fiber of the evaluation map
ev_{\mathbf{d}}:{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X^{r}
for
X
a flag variety, and more generally the geometry of Gromov-Witten varieties of flag varieties.

Definition 0.0.1.1
.

Let
X_{1}
,
\dots
,
X_{r}
be Schubert varieties of the flag variety
X
, let
\mathbf{d}
be an element in
E(X)
. For any element
g=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
, we call
Gromov-Witten variety of degree
\mathbf{d}
associated with the varieties
X_{i}
and with
g
the subscheme

{\mathcal{W}}_{X;X_{1},\dots,X_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}X_{1})\cap\dots\cap ev_{r}^{-1}(g_{r}X_{r})

of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})
.

Note that the Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{X;X_{1},\dots,X_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}
parametrizes morphisms
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
representing the class
{\mathbf{d}}
whose image has a non empty intersection with the translates
g_{i}\cdot X_{i}
of the Schubert varieties
X_{i}
. When these varieties are zero dimensional, their number of points is a Gromov-Witten invariant. More generally, the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of their adherence in the space
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})
is a correlator in quantum
K
-theory
[
Giv00
]
.

Let
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
be a set of
m
integers satisfying
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}<n
, such that
I
is contained in
J
. We denote by
\pi:X=Fl_{J}\rightarrow X^{\prime}=Fl_{I}
the forgeftul map.
Fix a class
\lambda=[l]\in H_{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
of a curve
l
whose projection by the forgetful map
\pi
is a point. We observe in Chapter
1
a relation between the general fiber of the evaluation map

ev_{[C]+\lambda}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,[C]+\lambda)\rightarrow X^{r}

and the general fiber of the map
ev_{[\pi(C)]}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X^{\prime},[\pi(C)])\to(X^{\prime})^{r}
.

Theorem
.

(Theorem
1.1.2.1
)
Suppose the collection
(I,J,[C]+\lambda)
is a stabilized collection in the sense of Definition
1.1.1.1
. Then :

—

For any Schubert varieties
X_{1}
,
\dots
,
X_{r}
of
X
, for
g
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{J};X_{1},\dots,X_{r}}^{g,[C]+\lambda}
of
Fl_{J}
is a rationally connected fibration above the Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};\pi(X_{1}),\dots,\pi(X_{r})}^{g,[\pi(C)]}
of
Fl_{I}
.

—

For
x
general in
(Fl_{J})^{r}
, the fiber
ev_{[C]+\lambda}^{-1}(x)
is a tower of unirational fibrations over the fiber
ev_{[\pi(C)]}^{-1}(\pi(x))
.

We called a variety
W
a
rationally connected fibration
(respectively unirational fibration) above
W^{\prime}
if there exists a dominant map
W\rightarrow W^{\prime}
sending each irreducible component of
W
onto a different irreducible component of
W^{\prime}
, and whose general fiber is a rationally connected variety (respectively unirational). A variety
W
is a
tower of unirational fibrations
above a variety
W^{\prime}
if there exists a sequence of morphisms

W_{1}=W\rightarrow W_{2}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow W_{l}=W^{\prime}

such that for
1\leq i<l
, the variety
W_{i}
is a unirational fibration above
W_{i+1}
.

0.0.2
Chapter
2
: A comparison formula between genus
0
correlators of flag varieties.

Fix integers
1\leq n_{1}<\dots<n_{m}<n
. We consider the flag variety
X
parametrizing flags of vector spaces

V_{n_{1}}\subset\dots\subset V_{n_{m}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{.}

satisfying
{\rm dim}V_{n_{i}}=n_{i}
. Let
T
be a torus in
{\rm GL}_{n}
acting on
X
by left multiplication. Let
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be an effective class of curve in
H_{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
. We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
the coarse moduli space parametrizing genus
0
stable maps representing the class
{\mathbf{d}}
. Assocating to a stable map with
r
marked points the image of its
i
-th marked point induces an evaluation map
ev_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X
. Let
E_{1}
,
\dots
,
E_{r}
be
T
-equivariant fiber bundles over
X
.
In quantum
K
-theory,
T
-equivariant correlators are defined by

\langle\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\rangle_{T,(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})}^{X}:=\chi_{T}\left(ev_{1}^{*}E_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes ev_{r}^{*}E_{r}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}\right),

(1)

where we denote by
\chi_{T}
the
T
-equivariant Euler-Poincaré characteristic
[
Giv00
,
Lee04
]
.
For
1\leq k\leq m
, we denote by
X_{\widehat{k}}
the flag variety obtained from
X
by forgetting the vector space
V_{n_{k}}
, and by

\pi_{\widehat{k}}:X\rightarrow X_{\widehat{k}}

the forgetful map. In order to facilitate notations, we set
d_{0}=0=d_{m+1}
. Chapter
2
yields the following equality between correlators-cf. Theorem
2.1.3.1
bis.

Theorem
.

Suppose :

—

\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
,

—

d_{k-1}\leq\lfloor\frac{d_{k+1}}{n_{k+1}}\rfloor
,

—

d_{k}\geq r(n_{k}-n_{k-1})+d_{k-1}+(n_{k}-n_{k-1})(\lfloor\frac{d_{k+1}-d_{k-1}}{n_{k+1}-n_{k-1}}\rfloor+1)
.

Then for any
T
-equivariant fiber bundles
E_{1}
,
\dots
,
E_{r}
over
X_{\widehat{k}}
, the correlators of
X
and
X_{\widehat{k}}
associated with the degree
\mathbf{d}
and the fiber bundles
E_{i}
are equal :

\langle(\pi_{\widehat{k}})^{*}E_{1},\dots,(\pi_{\widehat{k}})^{*}E_{r}\rangle_{T,(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})}^{X}=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{r}\rangle_{T,(d_{1},\dots,d_{k-1},d_{k+1},\dots,d_{m})}^{X_{\widehat{k}}}.

Note that for
3
-pointed Gromov-Witten invariants, a result of Peterson/Woodward allows to deduce all invariants with
3
marked points of generalized flag varieties
G/P
from
3
-pointed Gromov-Witten invariants of
G/B

[
Woo05
]
.

0.0.3
Chapter
3
: Schubert calculus for the incidence variety
Fl_{1,n-1}
.

We study in Chapter
3
different variations of modern Schubert calculus for the incidence variety
X=Fl_{1,n-1}
parametrizing pairs
(p,H)
where
p
is a point in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
and
H
is a hyperplane in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
containing the point
p
. Note that
X
can be written as
G/P
, where
G={\rm GL}_{n}
,
T
is the set of diagonal matrices,
B
is the set of upper triangular matrices and
P
is the parabolic subgroup of
G
satisfying
B\subset P
associated with the roots
\{\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}\}
. The smallest length representative
w
of an element in
W/W_{P}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}/\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}
is a permutation
w_{i,j}
satisfying
w(1)=i
,
w(n)=j
, and such that for
1<k<n-1

w(k)<w(k+1)
. For
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
i\neq j
, we call
X(i,j):=X(w_{i,j})
the Schubert variety of
Fl_{1,n-1}
associated with the element
w_{i,j}
in
W^{P}\simeq W/W_{P}
.

Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
.
For
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
i\neq j
, the natural embedding
i:X(i,j)\hookrightarrow X
defines a sheaf
i_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X(i,j)}
, which is a coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Fl_{1,n-1}}
-module. We denote by
{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j}:=[i_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X(i,j)}]
the class of
i_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X(i,j)}
in the Grothendieck group
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
of coherent sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Fl_{1,n-1}}
-module. Schubert classes
({\mathcal{O}}_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq n,\>i\neq j}
form a basis of
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
, and the product

{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p}=\sum_{i=0}^{{\rm dim}Fl_{1,n-1}}(-1)^{i}[Tor_{i}^{Fl_{1,n-1}}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{X(i,j)},{\mathcal{O}}_{X(k,p)}\right)]

defines a structure of associative and commutative ring
(K(Fl_{1,n-1}),\cdot)
with identity element
[{\mathcal{O}}_{Fl_{1,n-1}}]
. We set
{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j}=0
if
i<1
or
j>n
.

Proposition

(Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
-cf. Proposition
3.4.1.1
)
.

Let
1\leq i,j,k,p\leq n
, where
i\neq j
and
k\neq p
. Then

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{k,p}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{i,j}&=\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n,j+p-1}\>&\mathrm{if}\>i+k-n\geq j+p\>\mathrm{or}\>i<j\>\mathrm{or}\>k<p;\\
\mathcal{O}_{k,p}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{i,j}&=\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n-1,j+p-1}+\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n,j+p}-\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n-1,j+p}\>&\mathrm{else}.\end{aligned}\right.

Chevalley formula and positive algorithm in
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
.
We denote by
l_{1}:=X(2,1)
et
l_{2}:=X(1,2)
the two one-dimensional Schubert varieties of
Fl_{1,n-1}
. Their classes
[l_{1}]
and
[l_{2}]
in the singular homology of
Fl_{1,n-1}
generate
H_{2}(Fl_{1,n-1},{\mathbb{Z}})
. The
small quantum
K
-ring

QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})=(K(Fl_{1,n-1}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}[Q_{1},Q_{2}],\star)
of
Fl_{1,n-1}
is a deformation of the ring
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
by the Novikov variables
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
associated with the classes
[l_{1}]
and
[l_{2}]

[
Lee04
]
. This deformation depends on the correlators
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p},{\mathcal{O}}_{s,t}\rangle_{d_{1}[l_{1}]+d_{2}[l_{2}]}
defined by (
). Considering the limit
Q_{1},Q_{2}\rightarrow 0
yields the
K
-ring
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
.

We name
h_{1}:=X(n-1,1)
and
h_{2}:=X(n,2)
the two Schubert varieties of
Fl_{1,n-1}
of codimension
1
.

Proposition

(Chevalley formula in
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
-cf. Proposition
3.7.0.1
)
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{1}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}Q_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{n-1,n}+Q_{1}Q_{2}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]-{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{1}}\right)&\mathrm{if}\>k=1,p=n\\
Q_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{n,p}&\mathrm{if}\>k=1,\>p<n\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{1,2}+Q_{1}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]-{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{1}}\right)&\mathrm{if}\>k=2,p=1\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{k-1,p}&\mathrm{if}\>k>1,\>k\neq p+1\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{p-1,p}+{\mathcal{O}}_{p,p+1}-{\mathcal{O}}_{p-1,p+1}&\mathrm{if}\>1<p<n-1,\>k=p+1\\
\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{2}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}Q_{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{1,2}+Q_{1}Q_{2}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]-{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{2}}\right)&\mathrm{if}\>k=1,p=n\\
Q_{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{k,1}&\mathrm{if}\>k>1,\>p=n\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{n-1,n}+Q_{2}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]-{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{2}}\right)&\mathrm{if}\>k=n,p=n-1\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p+1}&\mathrm{if}\>p<n,\>k\neq p+1\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{p,p+1}+{\mathcal{O}}_{p-1,p}-{\mathcal{O}}_{p-1,p+1}&\mathrm{if}\>1<k<n-1,\>k=p+1\\
\end{array}\right.

Note that for
X
a cominuscule variety, a Chevalley formula in small quantum
K
-theory is given by Buch-Chaput-Mihalcea-Perrin
[
BCMP16
]
.

Since
({\mathcal{O}}_{w})_{w\in W^{P}}
is a basis of
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
, for any elements
u
and
v
in
W^{P}
, there is a unique decomposition

{\mathcal{O}}_{u}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}=\sum_{d_{1},\>d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{w\in W^{P}}a_{u,v}^{w;d_{1},d_{2}}Q_{1}^{d_{1}}Q_{2}^{d_{2}}{\mathcal{O}}_{w}.

We call the polynomials
\sum_{d_{1},\>d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}a_{u,v}^{w;d_{1},d_{2}}Q_{1}^{d_{1}}Q_{2}^{d_{2}}

Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
.
We call the ring
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})

positive
if for any elements
u
and
v
in
W^{P}
,
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
in
\mathbb{N}
the sign of the coefficient
a_{u,v}^{w;d_{1},d_{2}}
is given by the following inequality.

(-1)^{{\rm codim}X(w)-{\rm codim}X(u)-{\rm codim}X(v)+\int_{d_{1}[l_{1}]+d_{2}[l_{2}]}c_{1}(T_{X})}a_{u,v}^{w;d_{1},d_{2}}\geq 0.

Note that for
d_{1}=0=d_{2}
, this inequality is satisfied according to M. Brion’s result on positivity in the Grothendieck groups of generalized flag varieties
[
Bri02
]
.
Finally, we call an algorithm
positive
if at each iteration the computation performed is a sum of coefficients having the same sign.

Proposition
.

The algorithm given Subsection
3.8.1
is a positive algorithm computing Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
.

Corollary
.

(Cf. Proposition
3.8.2.1
)
The small quantum
K
-theory ring
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
is positive.

Conjecture
.

The closed formula described in Subsection
3.8.3
yields Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
QK_{s}(Fl_{1,n-1})
.

Definitions and notations

0.1
Space of stable maps
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
.

Definitions.
Let
Y
be a complex projective smooth variety. Let
C
be a connecetd reduced nodal curve of genus zero equipped with
r
distinct non singular marked points.
A morphism
C\rightarrow Y
from
C
to the variety
Y
is called a
stable map
if each irreducible component of
C
sent onto a point contains at least three points that are nodal or marked. Let
\gamma
be an element in
H_{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
corresponding to an effective class of curve. We say the morphism
\varphi:C\rightarrow Y

represents the class
\gamma
if
\varphi_{*}[C]=\gamma\in H_{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Let
S
be a complex scheme. A family of quasi-stable maps with
r
marked points above
S
is given by

—

A flat and projective morphism
\pi:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow S
equipped with
r
sections
p_{i}:S\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, such that each geometrical fiber is a reduced nodal projective connected curve of genus zero
C_{s}
and such that the marked points
p_{i}(s)
are distinct and non singular.

—

A morphism
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Y
.

The
space
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
of genus
0
stable maps to
Y
representing
\gamma
is the coarse moduli space parametrizing families of stable maps with
r
marked points above
S
from genus
0
curves to
Y
representing the class
\gamma
, such that the geometric fiber
\mathcal{C}_{s}
above any point
s
in
S
defines a stable map
\mathcal{C}_{s}\rightarrow Y

[
FP96
]
.
For a homogeneous variety
Y
, the space
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
is an irreducible rational projective variety, ofe dimension the expected dimension and with quotient singularities
[
FP96
,
KP01
]
. Furthermore, the subvariety
{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
parametrizing maps
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow Y
is a dense open subset of
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
. A general point in
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
thus corresponds to a map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow Y
representing the class
\gamma
.

Note that a general point in
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
is not necessarily associated with an injective map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to Y
. This is immediate if
Y={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
; indeed, a general point in
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},d)
is associated with a degree
d
map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. This phenomenon may also be observed in higher dimensions. Consider for example the full flag variety
Y=Fl_{1,2}={\rm GL}_{3}/B
parametrizing pairs
(p,l)
where
p
is a point in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
l
is a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
taht contains
p
. Fix a line
l
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Denote by
\gamma
the class of the curve
C\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset Fl_{1,2}
containing all pairs
(p,l)
, where
p\subset l
and by
\pi:Fl_{1,2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
the map forgetting the line
l^{\prime}
in a pair
(p,l^{\prime})
. Then the restriction of
\pi
to the curve
C
is an isomorphism onto its image, which is the line
l\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Denote by
\pi^{\prime}:Fl_{1,2}\rightarrow{\rm Gr}(2,3)
the map induced by forgetting the point
p
in a pair
(p,l)
. An irreducible curve
C^{\prime}
in
Fl_{1,2}
of class
2\gamma
satisfies
\pi_{*}[C^{\prime}]=2
in
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
. Furthermore its image
\pi(C^{\prime})
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is included in the line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
defined by the image
\pi^{\prime}(C^{\prime})\in{\rm Gr}(2,3)
. Then the projection
\pi
induces a degree
2
morphism between the curve
C^{\prime}
and its image
\pi(C^{\prime})
, which contradicts the fact that the restriction of
\pi
to
C^{\prime}
is an injection. There thus is no irreducible curve of class
2\gamma
in
Fl_{1,2}
, and a general point in
{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{1,2},2\gamma)\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{1,2},2\gamma)
corresponds to a degree
2
map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow Fl_{1,2}
.

G
-action on
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
.
Let
G
be a complex algebraic group,
Y
be a smooth projective
G
-variety (in this thesis
G
will be the group
{\rm GL}_{n}
of invertible matrices and
Y
will be a flag variety parametrizing flags of vector spaces included in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
). Let
\gamma
be an element of
H_{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
corresponding to an effective class of curve. We consider the natural transformation of functors associating to a morphism
\phi:S\rightarrow G
and to a family of quasi-stable maps
(\pi:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow S,\mu:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Y)
above
S
with
r
marked points representing the class
\gamma
the following family :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\cdot\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where we denote by
G\cdot\mu:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Y
the morphism
\mathcal{C}\ni c\rightarrow\Phi\circ\pi(c)\cdot\mu(c)\in Y
translating curves
\mu(\mathcal{C}_{s})
by the element in
G
image of
s
by
\Phi
.
This natural transformation of functors induces an action
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)\times G\rightarrow\overline{{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)}
, associating to an element
g
in
G
and to a map
\mu:C\rightarrow Y
the map

g\cdot\mu:C\ni p\rightarrow g\cdot\mu(p)\in Y.

0.2
Flag varieties

We recall here some classical definitions and properties of flag varieties.
Let
n>0
. We consider a collection
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
of
m
non-negative integers
i_{k}
satisfying
i_{0}:=0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}<i_{m+1}:=n
. We denote by
Fl_{I}
the variety parametrizing flags of vector spaces

V_{i_{1}}\subset V_{i_{2}}\subset\dots\subset V_{i_{m}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n},

where
V_{i_{k}}
is a vector subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of dimension
i_{k}
. Note that the variety
Fl_{I}
represents the functor
Sch\to Sets
associating to a scheme
S
the following set :

\{\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\dots\subset\mathcal{F}_{m}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{S}^{\oplus n}\},

where the fiber bundles over
S

\mathcal{F}_{k}
have rank
i_{k}
and the quotient sheaves
\mathcal{F}_{k}/\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
are rank
(i_{k}-i_{k-1})
fiber bundles. Furthermore, we consider the complex algebraic group
G={\rm GL}_{n}
, the maximal torus
T
of
G
given by the set of diagonal matrices and the Borel subgroup
B\subset G
given by the set of upper triangular matrices. We denote by
W\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}
the Weyl group of
G={\rm GL}_{n}
, and
\Delta={\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}
the set of simple roots positive with respect to
B
. We denote by
\Delta(I)=\{\alpha_{1},\dots\alpha_{i_{1}-1},\dots,\alpha_{i_{m}-1}\}
the subset of
\Delta
associated with
I
, and by
W_{I}\simeq\prod_{k=1}^{m+1}\mathfrak{S}_{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}
the subgroup of
W
generated by reflexions
s_{\alpha}
associated with elements
\alpha
in
\Delta(I)
. Finally, we denote by
P_{I}:=BW_{I}B
the parabolic subgroup associated with
\Delta(I)
. Note that the block diagonal matrix whose
k
-th block is
GL_{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}
is a Levi sugroup
L_{I}
associated with
P_{I}
. We have the following identification :
Fl_{I}\simeq G/P_{I}
.

Let
1\leq k<m
. Composing the map forgetting all vector spaces except from the
k
-th one with the Plücker embedding
{\rm Gr}(i_{k},n)\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{\binom{n}{i_{k}}-1}
induces a map
\varphi:Fl_{I}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{\binom{n}{i_{k}}-1}
. Let
l_{i}
be a curve on
Fl_{I}
sent onto a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{\binom{n}{i_{k}}-1}
by
\varphi
, and such that the restriction of
\varphi
to
l_{i}
is an isomorphism. The classes
[l_{i}]
generate the free abelian group
H_{2}(Fl_{I},{\mathbb{Z}})
, and we may thus identify an effective class of curve
{\mathbf{d}}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}d_{k}[l_{k}]
in
E(Fl_{I})\subset H_{2}(Fl_{I},{\mathbb{Z}})
with
m
positive integers
(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
.

0.3
Algebraic
K
-theory

0.3.1
Equivariant algebraic
K
-theory.

We recall here some classical definitions and properties of the equivariant
K
-theory of a variety ; complete proofs can be found in
[
C+09
]
, chapter 5. Let
H
be a complex linear algebraic group, let
X
be an irreducible variety with an
H
-action. Let
E
be a vector bundle on
X
. We call
E
an
H
-equivariant vector bundle
if there exists an
H
-action
\Phi:H\times E\rightarrow E
satisfying

–

The map
E\rightarrow X
commutes with
H
-actions.

–

For any
x
in
X
and
g
in
G
, the map
\Phi(g,\bullet):E_{x}\rightarrow E_{g\cdot x}
is a linear map of vector spaces,

where we denote by
E_{x}
the fiber over a point
x
in
X
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on
X
. Denote by
a:H\times X\rightarrow X
the action of
H
on
X
, by
p:H\times X\rightarrow X
the projection map and by
p_{2\>3}:H\times H\times X\rightarrow H\times X
the projection along the first factor
H
. We call
\mathcal{F}
an
H
-equivariant coherent sheaf
if there exists an
H
-action
\Phi:H\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
satisfying

–

There exists an isomorphism
I:a^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow p^{*}\mathcal{F}
.

–

{p_{2\>3}}^{*}I\circ({\rm id}_{H}\times a)^{*}I=(m\times{\rm id}_{X})^{*}I
of sheaves on
H\times X
.

–

There is an identification
I_{1\times X}={\rm id}:\mathcal{F}=a^{*}\mathcal{F}_{|1\times X}\rightarrow p^{*}\mathcal{F}_{|1\times X}=\mathcal{F}
.

Denote by
K_{H}(X)
the Grothendieck group of
H
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
X
and by
K^{H}(X)
the Grothendieck group of
H
-equivariant vector bundles on
X
. The tensor product of equivariant vector bundles defines a ring structure on
K^{H}(X)
. Moreover, the map
K^{H}(X)\otimes K_{H}(X)\rightarrow K_{H}(X)
defined by the tensor product

[E]\otimes[\mathcal{F}]\rightarrow[E\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}]

makes
K_{H}(X)
a
K^{H}(X)
-module. When
X
is non singular, the map
[E]\rightarrow[E\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]
yields an isomorphism
K_{H}(X)\simeq K^{H}(X)
. Indeed, any equivariant sheaf then has a finite resolution by equivariant vector bundles.

Furthermore, for any equivariant proper morphism of irreducible varieties with an
H
-action
f:X\rightarrow Y
, there is a pushforward map
f_{*}:K_{H}(X)\rightarrow K_{H}(Y)
defined by :

[\mathcal{F}]\rightarrow\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}[R^{i}f_{*}(\mathcal{F})].

Indeed, if
\mathcal{F}
is an
H
-equivariant sheaf on
X
, the Godement resolution implies that
R^{i}f_{*}(\mathcal{F})
will also be an
H
-equivariant sheaf on
Y
.

In particular, if
X
is an irreducible projective
H
-variety, the equivariant pushforward
K_{H}(X)\rightarrow K_{H}(point)
is called
equivariant Euler characteristic
, and denoted by
\chi_{H}
.

Moreover, an equivariant morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
induces a pullback map
f^{*}:K^{H}(Y)\rightarrow K^{H}(X)
:

[E]\rightarrow[f^{*}(E)].

Consider an equivariant proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of irreducible varieties with an
H
-action. Let
E
be an equivariant vector bundle on
Y
, and
\mathcal{F}
be an equivariant coherent sheaf on
X
.
Note that the following projection formula is satisfied in
K_{H}(Y)
:

f_{*}(f^{*}[E]\otimes[\mathcal{F}])=[E]\otimes f_{*}[\mathcal{F}].

0.3.2
Algebraic K-theory of
G/P
.

We recall here some classical definitions and properties of the Grothendieck ring of coherent sheaves on a variety.
Denote by
K_{\circ}(X)
the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves on
X
and by
K^{\circ}(X)
the Grothendieck group of vector bundles on
X
. Note that for
H={\rm id}
, the equivariant groups
K_{H}(X)
and
K^{H}(X)
are equal to
K_{\circ}(X)
and
K^{\circ}(X)
. In particular, the tensor product of vector bundles defines a ring structure on
K^{\circ}(X)
, and for a non singular variety
X
we can identify
K(X)=K_{\circ}(X)\simeq K^{\circ}(X)
. Furthermore, for any coherent sheaves
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
on
X
we have

[\mathcal{F}]\cdot[\mathcal{G}]=\sum_{i=0}^{{\rm dim}X}(-1)^{i}Tor_{i}^{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}).

Furthermore, note that for an irreducible projective variety
X
, the pushforward
K_{\circ}(X)\to K_{\circ}({\rm Spec}{\mathbb{C}})
is given by :

\displaystyle\chi:K_{\circ}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle[\mathcal{F}]\rightarrow\chi(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}h^{i}(\mathcal{F}),

where
h^{i}
denote the dimension of the
i
-th cohomology group of
\mathcal{F}
; i.e.
\chi
is the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of
\mathcal{F}
.
A morphism of schemes
f:X\rightarrow Y
is called
perfect
if there is an
N
such that

Tor_{i}^{Y}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\mathcal{F})=0

for all
i>N
and for all coherent sheaves
\mathcal{F}
on
Y
. For a perfect morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, one can define the pull-back

f^{*}:K_{\circ}(Y)\rightarrow K_{\circ}(X)

by

f^{*}[\mathcal{F}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N}[Tor_{i}^{Y}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\mathcal{F})].

Furthermore suppose
f
is a morphism locally of finite type. Then if
f
is flat or
Y
is regular,
f
is perfect-cf. for example Stacks Project
37.53
.
We will also be using the following easy results.

Lemma 0.3.2.1
.

Let
Y
be an irreducible projective variety and
\Phi
be an irreducible projective subvariety of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times Y
. Denote by
\pi:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
the natural projection. Let
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
be two points on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then we have the following equality in
K_{\circ}(Y)
:

[\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{1})}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{2})}]

Démonstration.

Since the projection map
\pi
is flat, we have
\pi^{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{i}}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{i})}]
in
K_{\circ}(\Phi)
. Denote by
\Pi:\Phi\rightarrow Y
the natural projection. We obtain
\Pi_{*}\pi^{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{i}}]=\Pi_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{i})}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{i})}]
in
K_{\circ}(Y)
. Finally, the equality
[{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{1}}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{2}}]
in
K_{\circ}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
yields the result.
∎

Lemma 0.3.2.2
.

Let
X
be a projective
{\rm GL}_{n}
-variety. Consider a subvariety
Y
of
X
. Then for all
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
, we have the following equality in
K_{\circ}(X)
:

[\mathcal{O}_{g\cdot Y}]=[\mathcal{O}_{Y}].

Démonstration.

Let
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
. Since
{\rm GL}_{n}
is a dense open subset of
\mathcal{M}_{n,n}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n^{2}}
, there exits an irreducible rational curve
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\supset U\rightarrow{\rm GL}_{n}
sending points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
onto the points
g
and
{\rm id}
. Denote by
\Phi
the irreducible projective subvariety of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times X
obtained as the adherence of the image of the map
U\times Y\rightarrow U\times Y\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times X
sending
(x,y)
to
(x,f(x)\cdot y)
. Denote by
\pi:\Phi\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
the first projection. Then according to Lemma
0.3.2.1

[{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{1})}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(p_{2})}]\>\mathrm{hence}\>[\mathcal{O}_{g\cdot Y}]=[\mathcal{O}_{Y}].

∎

0.4
Rational singularities.

We recall here known definitions and results on rational singularities. The treatment in this section is inspired by
[
Bri02
]
. An irreducible complex variety
X
has
rational singularities
if there exists a desingularization
\psi:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
such that :

\psi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}\>\mathrm{and}\>\>\forall i>0,\>R^{i}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}=0.

(2)

Note that, according to Zariski’s main theorem, the equality
\psi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is equivalent to the normality of
X
.
The relevance of this notion here comes from the following results.

Theorem 0.4.0.1
.

(Viehweg
[
Vie77
]
)
The quotient of a smooth complex scheme by a finite group has rational singularities.

Note that, since for
X
convex the moduli spaces
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
have quotient singularities
[
FP96
]
, this implies that for
X
a generalized flag variety the moduli spaces
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
have rational singularities.

Let
H
be a complex linear algebraic group. Recall a variety
X
is called
unirational
if there exists a dominant map
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}\dashrightarrow X
, and
rationally connected
if there exists an irreducible rational curve joining two general points in
X
. Note that since
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
is rationally connected, a unirational variety is rationally connected.

Theorem 0.4.0.2
.

(Buch-Mihalcea
[
BM11
]
, Theorem 3.1.)
Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a surjective equivariant morphism of projective varieties with rational singularities equipped with an
H
-action. Assume that the general fiber of
f
is rationally connected. Then
f_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]\in K_{H}(Y)
.

Démonstration.

Buch and Mihalcea prove in
[
BM11
]
that this statement is satisfied if a desingularization
\psi:\widetilde{X_{y}}\rightarrow X_{y}
of the fiber of
f
over
y
general in
Y
satisfying
H^{i}(\widetilde{X_{y}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X_{y}}})=0
for all
i>0
. Since
X_{y}
is rationally connected, and
\widetilde{X_{y}}
is birational to
X_{y}
,
\widetilde{X_{y}}
is also rationally connected. Hence
\widetilde{X_{y}}
is a smooth rationally connected variety, hence according to
[
Deb13
]
,
\widetilde{X_{y}}
satisfies
H^{i}(\widetilde{X_{y}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X_{y}}})=0
for all
i>0
.
∎

Note that for
X
a full flag variety, Brion gives a nice proof of the rational singularities of Schubert varieties in
[
Bri05
]
.

Theorem 0.4.0.3
.

(Ramanathan,
[
Ram85
]
)
Schubert varieties have rational singularities.

Chapitre 1
Gromov-Witten varieties of partial flag varieties

1.1
Introduction

Removing subspaces from a partial flag gives another partial flag composed of fewer subspaces. This induces a forgetful map
\pi:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
between the corresponding flag varieties.
The main result of this chapter is that, for a degree large enough, the variety associated with rational curves of a given degree in
X
having their marked points within Schubert varieties
X_{i}
of
X
is a rationally connected fibration over its image, which is the variety associated with rational curves of a given degree in
X
having their marked points within Schubert varieties
\pi(X_{i})
of
X^{\prime}
. These subvarieties of the space of genus
0
stable maps to a homogeneous space are called Gromov-Witten varieties. The Euler characteristics of the structure sheaf of these varieties are quantum K-theoretical invariants
[
Giv00
]
. When these varieties are zero dimensional, their number of points are Gromov-Witten invariants. Our result in particular implies the equality between (non equivariant) correlators proved in Chapter
2
, providing an alternative proof-cf. chapter
2
Section
2.4
.

1.1.1
Definitions.

Let
n>0
. Let
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
be a collection of integers satisfying
0<i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{m}<n
. Denote by
Fl_{I}
the flag variety parametrizing
m
-tuples of vector spaces ordered by inclusion

V_{i_{1}}\subset V_{i_{2}}\dots\subset V_{i_{m}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}

(1.1)

such that
{\rm dim}V_{i_{k}}=i_{k}
.
Let
B
be a Borel subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{n}
. Note that
Fl_{I}
is the homogeneous variety
Fl_{I}\simeq{\rm GL}_{n}/P_{I}
, where
P_{I}
is the parabolic subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{n}
satisfying
B\subset P_{I}
with associated reductive subgroup
L_{I}\simeq\prod_{p=1}^{m+1}{\rm GL}(i_{p}-i_{p-1})
, where we set
i_{0}=0
and
i_{m+1}=n
. Let
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{M}\}
be a collection of integers satisfying
0<j_{1}<\dots<j_{M}<n
containing
I
, i.e. for each
k
there exists an element
s
such that
j_{s}=i_{k}
. Consider an integer
p
such that
1\leq p\leq M
. We denote by
J_{\geq p}
the collection
J_{\geq p}:=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{p^{\prime}},j_{p},\dots,j_{M}\}
, where
p^{\prime}
is the largest integer such that
i_{p^{\prime}}<j_{p}
. Forgetting all vector spaces included in
V_{j_{p}}
except those of the form
V_{i_{k}}
yields a forgetful morphism

\pi_{I/J_{\geq p}}:Fl_{J_{\geq p}}\rightarrow Fl_{I}.

In the same way, for
1\leq p\leq M
, we name
J_{\leq p}:=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{p},i_{p^{\prime}+1}\dots,i_{m}\}
, where
p^{\prime}
is the smallest integer such that
j_{p}<i_{p^{\prime}}
. We name
\pi_{I/J_{\leq p}}:Fl_{J_{\leq p}}\rightarrow Fl_{I}
the morphism induced by forgetting all vector spaces containing
V_{j_{p}}
except those of the form
V_{i_{k}}
.

Let
W=N_{{\rm GL}_{n}}(T)/T\simeq{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
be the Weyl group of the algebraic group
{\rm GL}_{n}
. We denote by
Fl_{J}(u)=\overline{BuP_{J}/P_{J}}
the Schubert variety of
Fl_{J}
associated with
u\in W
.
Note that the image of a Schubert variety
Fl_{J}(u)
by the projection
\pi_{I/J}
is the Schubert variety
\pi_{I/J}(Fl_{J}(u))=Fl_{I}(u)
.

Let
r>0
. The group
H_{2}(Fl_{I},{\mathbb{Z}})
is a free abelian group generated by classes of Schubert varieties of dimension
1
. Denote by
E(Fl_{I})\subset H_{2}(Fl_{I},\mathbb{Z})
the semi-group of effective classes of curves. Note that
H_{2}({\rm Gr}(k,n),{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the class of a curve on the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(k,n)
is given by the degree of its image in
{\mathbb{P}}^{\binom{n}{k}}
by the Plücker embedding. The class
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})\in E(Fl_{I})
of a curve
C
in
Fl_{I}
is determined by
m
non-negative integers
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
,
\dots
,
d_{m}
, where
d_{k}
is the degree of the projection of
C
to the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(i_{k},n)
.
For a class
{\mathbf{d}}
in
E(Fl_{I})
, we denote by
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},{\mathbf{d}})
the moduli space parametrizing stable maps
C\rightarrow Fl_{I}
from genus
0
curves
C
with
r
marked points
x_{i}\in C
to
Fl_{I}
representing the class
{\mathbf{d}}
. We name
ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow(Fl_{I})^{r}
the evaluation morphism sending the point
p
associated with a map
f_{p}:C\rightarrow Fl_{I}
to the image
\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{p}(x_{I})
of the
r
marked points
x_{i}\in C
. For any class
{\mathbf{d}}
in
E(Fl_{J})\simeq\mathbb{N}^{M}
, the morphism
\pi_{I/J}:Fl_{J}\rightarrow Fl_{I}
induces a morphism

\Pi_{I/J}\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J},{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},(\pi_{I/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(Fl_{I})^{r}}(Fl_{J})^{r}.

(1.2)

Let
1\leq k\leq M
such that the integer
j_{k}
is not in
I
. We denote by

\Pi_{J_{\geq k}}\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\geq k}},(\pi_{I/J_{\geq k}})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\geq k+1}},(\pi_{I/J_{\geq k+1}})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(Fl_{J_{\geq k+1}})^{r}}(Fl_{J_{\geq k}})^{r}

the morphism induced by forgetting the vector space
V_{j_{k}}
and by evaluating the
r
marked points. Let
1\leq\mu\leq k\leq M
such that the element
j_{k}
is not in
I
. We denote by
J_{\mu\leq k}
the collection
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{p},j_{\mu},\dots,j_{k},i_{p^{\prime}},\dots i_{m}\}
, where
p
is the largest element such that
i_{p}<j_{\mu}
and
p^{\prime}
is the smallest element such that
i_{p^{\prime}}>j_{k}
. We denote by

{\Pi}_{J_{\mu\leq k}}\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k}},(\pi_{J_{\mu\leq k}/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}},(\pi_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}})^{r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k}})^{r}

the morphism induced by forgetting the vector space
V_{j_{k}}
from
{Fl}_{\mu\leq k}
and evaluating the
r
marked points.

Definition 1.1.1.1
.

Consider a collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
of a set
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
of
m
positive integers satisfying

0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}<n,

a set
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{M}\}
of
M
positive integers satisfying

0<j_{1}<\dots<j_{M}<n

such that
I
is contained in
J
, and a set
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{M})
in
\mathbb{N}^{M}\simeq E(Fl_{J})
. We denote by
{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d^{\prime}_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\simeq E(Fl_{I})
the pushforward of
{\mathbf{d}}
by the forgetful map
\pi_{I/J}
. Let
1\leq\mu\leq M
such that
j_{\mu}
is contained in
I
. We call the collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}

stabilized with respect to
\mu
if the following conditions are satisfied. First, for all elements
1\leq k<\mu
such that the integer
j_{k}
is not contained in
I
, the two following conditions are satisfied.

\bullet

\mathrm{The}\>\mathrm{morphism}\>\Pi_{J_{\geq k}}\times ev\>\mathrm{is}\>\mathrm{surjective.}

(1.3)

\bullet

Let
k^{\prime}
be the largest integer such that
i_{k^{\prime}}<j_{k}
. For any
1\leq p<k^{\prime}
we have

d_{k}\geq\left\lceil\left(d^{\prime}_{p}-d^{\prime}_{p-1}\right)/(i_{p}-i_{p-1})\right\rceil j_{k}.

(1.4)

Futhermore, for all
\mu\leq k\leq M
such that the integer
j_{k}
is not contained in
I
the two following symmetric conditions are satisfied.

\bullet

The morphism
{\Pi}_{J_{\mu\leq k}}\times ev
is surjective.

\bullet

Let
k^{\prime}
be the smallest integer such that
i_{k^{\prime}}>j_{k}
. For
k^{\prime}<p\leq m
we have

d_{k}\geq\left\lceil\left(d^{\prime}_{p}-d^{\prime}_{p+1}\right)/(i_{p+1}-i_{p})\right\rceil(n-j_{k}).

A collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
will be called
stabilized
if there exists an integer
\mu
such that the collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
is stabilized with respect to
\mu
. A collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
will be called
stabilized with respect to
M
if for all
1\leq k\leq M
such that
j_{k}
is not contained in
I
the conditions (
) and (
) are satisfied.

Observe that when
\mu=1
, the conditions (
) and (
) are empty. For simplicity we have set
d^{\prime}_{0}=d^{\prime}_{m+1}=0=i_{0}
and
i_{m+1}=n
. We have denoted by
\lceil q\rceil
the ceiling of
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.
We will see in Section
1.6
some conditions to ensure that a collection is stabilized. The following interesting cases yield examples of stabilized collections (cf. Subsection
1.6.1
).

1.

Suppose
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
is a collection of integers satisfying
1<i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}<n
and
J=\{j_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
satisfies
0<j_{1}<i_{1}
. Note that then
Fl_{J}\rightarrow Fl_{I}
is the morphism forgetting the first vector space. Then if
d_{1}\geq j_{1}\left(r+1+\lfloor d_{2}/i_{1}\rfloor\right)
the collection
(I,J,(d_{1},d_{2},\dots d_{m+1}))
is stabilized.

2.

Suppose
J=\{k\}
and
I=\emptyset
; then
Fl_{J}
is the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(k,n)
and
Fl_{I}
is the point
{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}})
. Then if
d\geq rk
or
d\geq r(n-k)
the collection
(I,J,d)
is stabilized.

Note that the dependency on the integer
r
in the examples here above comes from the condition asking the surjectivity of the following morphisms.

{\Pi}_{J_{\mu\leq k}}\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k}},(\pi_{J_{\mu\leq k}/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}},(\pi_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k-1}})^{r}}(Fl_{J_{\mu\leq k}})^{r}

Definition 1.1.1.2
.

Let
\mathbf{d}
be an element in
E(Fl_{I})
, let
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in
W
. We call
Gromov-Witten variety of degree
\mathbf{d}
associated with
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
the subvariety

{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{1}))\cap\dots\cap ev_{r}^{-1}(g_{r}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{r}))

of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},\mathbf{d})
.

Note that for
\mathbf{d}
in
E(Fl_{I})
,
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
in
W
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}
is the variety associated with degree
{\mathbf{d}}
rational curves on
X
having their
i
-th marked point within
g_{i}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{i})
. Denote by
1
the identity in
W\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}
; note that
Fl_{I}(1):=P_{I}/P_{I}
a point in
Fl_{I}
. Furthermore, note that
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};1,\dots,1}^{g,\mathbf{d}}
is the variety associated with degree
{\mathbf{d}}
rational curves on
X
whose
i
-th marked point is the point
g_{i}P_{I}/P_{I}\in Fl_{I}
.

Remark 1.1.1.1
.

According to the proof of Kleiman’s transversality theorem
[
Kle74
]
for a general
g\in{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
the fiber product
overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},\mathbf{d})\underset{(Fl_{I})^{r}}{\times}(g_{1}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{1})\times\dots\times g_{r}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{r}))
is a reduced equidimensional scheme. Hence for
g
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
, the natural map

\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},\mathbf{d})\underset{(Fl_{I})^{r}}{\times}(g_{1}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{1})\times\dots\times g_{r}\cdot Fl_{I}(u_{r}))\to\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},\mathbf{d})\supset{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}

is a closed immersion surjecting into
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}
, and we can identify the two schemes.

1.1.2
Main results.

Definition 1.1.2.1
.

Let
Y
,
Z
be varieties. We call
Y
a
rationally connected fibration over
Z
(respectively
unirational fibration
) if there exists a morphism
Y\rightarrow Z
such that each irreducible component of
Y
dominates a different irreducible component of
Z
,
Y
dominates
Z
, and the general fiber
Y\to Z
is a rationally connected (respectively unirational) irreducible variety.
We call
Y
a
tower of unirational fibrations over
Z
if there exists a sequence of morphisms

Y_{1}=Y\rightarrow Y_{2}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Y_{n}=Z

such that, for all
1\leq i<n
,
Y_{i}
is a unirational fibration over
Y_{i+1}
.

Theorem 1.1.2.1
.

Consider a set
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{M}\}
of
M
integers satisfying
0<j_{1}<\dots<j_{M}<n
, a set
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
of
m
integers satisfying
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}<n
and a degree
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{M})\in E(Fl_{J})\simeq\mathbb{N}^{M}
such that the collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
is stabilized in the sense of Definition
1.1.1.1
.

i)

For
x
general in
(Fl_{J})^{r}
, the fiber
ev^{-1}(x)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J},{\mathbf{d}})
is a tower of unirational fibrations over the fiber
ev^{-1}(\pi_{I/J}(x))\subset\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},(\pi_{I/J})_{*}\mathbf{d})
.

ii)

Let
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in
W
. For
g
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
the Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{J};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,\mathbf{d}}
is a rationally connected fibration over the Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{Fl_{I};u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{g,(\pi_{I/J})_{*}\mathbf{d}}
.

Let
J
be a subsrt of
I
. Denote by
W_{J}:=N_{L_{J}}(T)/T
the Weyl group associated with
P_{J}
. Denote by

p:W/W_{J}\rightarrow W/W_{I}

the natural projection. For
u
in
W/W_{I}
, the inverse image
p^{-1}(u)
is the set of elements
u\cdot s
, where
s
is an element in
W_{I}/W_{J}
. Note that the Schubert variety
FL_{I}(u):=\overline{BuP_{I}/P_{I}}
is independent of the choice of a representative in
W
of the element
u
.

Corollary 1.1.2.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be a degree in
E(Fl_{I})\simeq\mathbb{N}^{m}
and
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in
W
such that the following Gromov-Witten invariant is non-zero :

n_{u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{{I},{\mathbf{d}}}:=\int_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},\mathbf{d})}ev_{1}^{*}[Fl_{I}(u_{1})]\cup\dots\cup ev_{r}^{*}[Fl_{I}(u_{r})]\neq 0.

Let
J
and
I
be sets of increasing positive integers and
{\mathbf{d}}
be a degree in
E(Fl_{J})
such that the collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
is stabilized in the sense of Definition
1.1.1.1
. Then for any elements
v_{i}\in p^{-1}(u_{i})
, for
g
general in
GL_{n}^{r}
the Gromov-Witten variety
\mathcal{W}_{Fl_{J};v_{1},\dots,v_{r}}^{g,{\mathbf{d}}}
has
n_{u_{1},\dots,u_{r}}^{{I},{\mathbf{d}}}
connected components, which are irreducible rationally connected varieties.

1.1.3
Previous research.

Gromov-Witten varieties of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
that are not rationally connected varieties have been found in
[
IMT14
]
. For
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, the locus of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3d-2}}(\mathbb{P}^{2},d)
corresponding to curves of degree
d
going through
3d-2
points is a curve whose genus depends on the degree
d
, of positive genus as soon as
d>2

[
Pan97
]
. A recursive procedure computing the geometric genus of
1
-dimensional Gromov-Witten varieties of
\mathbb{P}^{r}
was also given in
[
Ran01
]
. High degree Gromov-Witten varieties have been shown to be rational varieties for Grassmannians by Buch and Milhacea
[
BM11
]
, and more generally for cominuscule varieties by Chaput and Perrin
[
CP11a
]
.

This chapter is organized as follows.
We first prove in Section
1.2
that, when the general fiber of the morphism
\Pi_{I/J}\times ev
defined by (
) is rationally connected, Gromov-Witten varieties of
Fl_{I}
are rationally connected fibrations over Gromov-Witten varieties of
Fl_{J}
obtained by projection. We then recall in Section
1.3
results on flags of vector bundles over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Section
1.4
is dedicated to the study of the general fiber of the map
\Pi_{I/J}\times ev
. We observe that when the flag variety
Fl_{I}
is obtained from
Fl_{J}
by forgetting one vector space, the general fiber of this map is a unirational variety. This result is central in the derivation of Theorem
1.1.2.1
presented in Section
1.5
. Finally, we study the surjectivity of the morphism
\Pi_{I/J}\times ev
in Section
1.6
.

Conventions

Schemes considered in the subsections
1.2.2
and
1.2.3
will be defined over an algebraically closed field. Schemes considered in all other Sections will be defined over
\mathbb{C}
.

We say a property (P) holds for a
general
point in a variety
X
if (P) is true for points belonging to a dense open subset of
X
. We say the general fiber of a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
satisfies
(P)
if
f^{-1}(x)
satisfies
(P)
for points
x
in a dense open subset of
Y
.
Let
G
be an integral algebraic group scheme, let
Y
be an integral scheme endowed with a transitive
G
-action, let
Z
be a subscheme of
Y
, and
M\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of integral schemes. We say a property (P) holds for a subscheme
M\times_{Y}g\cdot Z
of
M
in
general position
in
M
if (P) holds for element
g
of
G
belonging to a dense open subset of
G
.

We consider here that a variety is not necessarily irreducible ; i.e. a variety is a reduced, finite type scheme defined over an algebraically closed field.

1.2
Restriction to a general subvariety

Let
X\simeq G/P
and
X^{\prime}\simeq G/P^{\prime}
be (generalized) flag varieties associated with parabolic subgroups
P\subset P^{\prime}
of a semi-simple linear algebraic group
G
. We observe here that some properties of Gromov-Witten varieties of
X
and
X^{\prime}
can be deduced from properties of the general fiber of the morphism
\Pi\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X^{\prime},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(X^{\prime})^{r}}X^{r}
-cf. Proposition
1.2.4.1
. This is one of the key ingredients in the derivation of Theorem
1.2.2.1
we provide in Section
1.5
.

More precisely, the property we prove here is the following. Let
G
be a semi-simple algebraic group, let
Z^{\prime}
be a projective variety with a transitive
G
-action, let
Z
be a projective variety over
Z^{\prime}
such that the morphism
Z\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
is
G
-equivariant. Let
\Pi:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a morphism of projective varieties over
Z^{\prime}
. Let
M\to Z
be a morphism of projective varieties, let
X
be a subvariety of
Z
. Then for
g
general in
G
the irreducibility (respectively rational connectedness) of the general fiber of the restriction map
{\Pi}_{|gX}:M_{g\cdot X}\to M^{\prime}_{g\cdot X}
induced by
\Pi
is implied by the irreducibility (respectively rational connectedness) of the general fiber of the morphism
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z
- cf. Proposition
1.2.3.1
.

1.2.1
Rationally connected, unirational and rational schemes.

We call a scheme
X

rationally connected
if there is an irreducible rational curve joining two general points on
X
. Note that a rationally connected scheme is irreducible. By contradiction suppose a scheme
Y
has several irreducible components
Y_{i}
and is rationally connected. We can then find two points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
that each belongs to an irreducible component
Y_{i}
and is not contained in any other irreducible component of
Y
such that there is an irreducible curve
C
joining the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. Then the irreducible curve
C
belongs to both
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
and is not contained in the intersection of the two irreducible components, which is a contradiction.

We call a variety
Y

rational
if there exists
N>0
such that
Y
is birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
. A variety
Y
is called
unirational
if there exists a dominant rational map
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}\dashrightarrow Y
. Note that since
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
is rationally connected, rational and unirational varieties are rationally connected.

1.2.2
Composition of rationally connected fibrations.

We call a a morphism of schemes
f:Y\to S
a rationally connected fibration if

—

Each irreducible component of
Y
dominates a different irreducible component of
S
;

—

Y
dominates
S
;

—

The general fiber of
f
is rationally connected.

We call a scheme
Y
a
rationally connected fibration over a scheme
S
if there exists a rationally connected fibration
Y\to S
.

Definition 1.2.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a subcategory of the category of schemes
\mathrm{Sch}
. We call a property
(P)

stable under composition in
\mathcal{C}
if for every morphisms
Y\xrightarrow{f}S
and
S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}S
in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
(P)
, the composition
f^{\prime}\circ f:Y\rightarrow S
also satisfies
(P)
.

Types of morphisms of schemes stable under composition are well known ; for example flat, smooth or etale morphisms are stable under composition-cf. for example Stacks Project
62.4
.
We focus here on composing rationally connected fibrations. Grabber-Harris-Starr’s Theorem on rational connectedness implies that the composition of rationally connected fibrations is itself a rationally connected fibration-cf. Theorem
1.2.2.1
. This property will be a key ingredient in the derivation of Theorem
1.1.2.1
proposed in Section
1.5
.

Theorem 1.2.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of irreducible projective schemes.

(1)

The property
"the general fiber is Irreducible"
is stable under composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

Let
(P)
be a property such that, for all morphisms
Y\rightarrow S
in
\mathcal{C}
, the following statement is verified :

if
S
and the general fiber of the morphism
Y\rightarrow S
satisfy
(P)
, then
Y
satisfies
(P)
.

Then the property
"the general fiber satisfies
(P)
and is irreducible"
is stable under composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

The property
"the general fiber is rationally connected"
is stable under composition in the category of irreducible complex projective schemes.

(4)

Let
Y
,
Z
and
S
be projective complex varieties such that
Y
is a rationally connected fibration over
Z
and
Z
is a rationally connected fibration over
S
. Then
Y
is a rationally connected fibration over
S
.

Lemma 1.2.2.1
.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow Z
be a dominant morphism of schemes, of finite presentation, where
Y
is an equidimensional noetherian scheme. Let
V
be a dense open subset of
Y
. Then the intersection of the general fiber of
f
with
V
is dense in the fiber.

Démonstration.

First, since the question is local on
Z
, we can suppose
Z
irreducible. Since
Y
is a noetherian scheme,
Y\setminus V
can be written as the union of a finite number of schemes, i.e.
Y\setminus V=\cup_{i=1}^{N}F_{i}
, where the
F_{i}
are closed subschemes of
Y
, of dimension smaller than the pure dimension of
Y
. Denote by
f_{|F_{i}}
the restriction of
f
to one of the boundary subschemes
F_{i}
. If
f_{|F_{i}}
does not dominate
Z
, then the preimage under
f_{|F_{i}}
of a general point in
Z
is empty. On the other side, if
f_{|F_{i}}
dominates
Z
, then the preimage under
f_{|F_{i}}
of a general point in
Z
is a scheme of pure dimension smaller than
\mathrm{dim}Y-\mathrm{dim}Z
. Indeed, since
f
is a morphism of finite presentation, Chevalley’s constructibility theorem implies
f(F_{i})
contains an open subset of
Z
; hence the subset of points
x
in
f(F_{i})
such that the fiber
f_{|F_{i}}^{-1}(x)
is of expected dimension is an open subset
V
of
Z
, verifying
{\rm dim}f_{|F_{i}}^{-1}(x)={\rm dim}F_{i}-{\rm dim}Z
for all points
x
in
V
. Thus, since
Y
is equidimensional, the general fiber
Y_{0}
of
f
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}Y-\mathrm{dim}Z
, whose intersection with
F_{i}
is a scheme of dimension smaller than
\mathrm{dim}Y-\mathrm{dim}Z
; this implies the intersection
Y_{0}\cap V
is dense in
Y_{0}
.
∎

Let
h:M\to M^{\prime}
and
f:M\to Z
be morphisms of schemes. We denote by
h\times f:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\times Z
the morphism induced by
h
and
f
.

Proposition 1.2.2.1
.

Let
M\xrightarrow{h}M^{\prime}
and
M\xrightarrow{f}Z
be surjective morphisms of finite presentation of integral noetherian schemes.

Then, for
z
general in
Z
:

i)

The image by
h
of any irreducible component of
f^{-1}(z)
has dimension equal to
{\rm dim}(h\times f)(M)-{\rm dim}Z
;

ii)

Each irreducible component of
f^{-1}(z)
dominates one of the irreducible components of
h(f^{-1}(z))
.

Démonstration.

i)

Consider a dense open subset
U
of
(h\times f)(M)
such that the fiber
(h\times f)^{-1}(u)
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
for all
u
in
U
. Consider a point
z
general in
Z
, and an irreducible component
Z_{0}
of
f^{-1}(z)
. Denote by
Z_{0}^{\circ}
the variety
Z_{0}
minus the intersection of
Z_{0}
with other irreducible components of
f^{-1}(z)
.
We first compute the dimension of the fiber
h^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}
, where
\mu
is a point in
(h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}
, before computing
{\rm dim}h(Z_{0})
.

Computation of
{\rm dim}{h}^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}
.
First, we have :

\displaystyle{h}^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap f^{-1}(z)

\displaystyle=(h\times f)^{-1}\left(h(\mu),z\right)

\displaystyle=(h\times f)^{-1}(h\times f)(\mu).

Since
(h\times f)(\mu)
lies in
U
, this implies
{h}^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap f^{-1}(z)
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
. Hence the intersection of the irreducible variety
Z_{0}^{\circ}
with
{h}^{-1}(h(\mu))
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
. Indeed, the intersection of the open subvariety
Z_{0}^{\circ}
of
f^{-1}(z)
with
h^{-1}(h(\mu))
is a non empty open subvariety of
h^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap Z_{0}
disconnected from the other pure dimensional irreducible components of
h^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap f^{-1}(z)
. Hence
h^{-1}(h(\mu))\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
.

Computation of
{\rm dim}h(Z_{0})
.
Note that, since
z
is general in
Z
, according to Lemma
1.2.2.1
,
(h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap f^{-1}(z)
is dense in
f^{-1}(z)
; hence
(h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}
is a dense open subset of
Z_{0}
. Consider a point
\nu
general in
h(Z_{0})
; since
\nu
is general and
h\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}\right)
is dense in the irreducible variety
h(Z_{0})
,
\nu
belongs to
h\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}\right)
, and we have :

\displaystyle{\rm dim}Z_{0}-{\rm dim}h(Z_{0})

\displaystyle={\rm dim}{h}^{-1}(\nu)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)

where the first equality is deduced from the general position of
\nu
in
h(Z_{0})
, and the second equality holds since
\nu
is in
h\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap Z_{0}^{\circ}\right)
. This implies :

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}h(Z_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}Z_{0}-\mathrm{dim}M+\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}f^{-1}(z)-\mathrm{dim}M+\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M),

where the second equality holds since
z
is general in
Z
and thus
f^{-1}(z)
is equidimensional.

ii)

By contradiction, if an irreducible component
Z_{0}
of
f^{-1}(z)
has an image strictly contained in
h\left(f^{-1}(z)\right)
, then :

{\rm dim}h(Z_{0})<{\rm dim}h\left(f^{-1}(z)\right)={\rm dim}(h\times f)(M)-{\rm dim}Z,

which would contradict
i)
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2.2.1
.

(1)

Let
h:Y\rightarrow S
and
S\rightarrow Z
be morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
whose general fiber is irreducible. Note that since we consider properties of general fibers, we can assume that both morphisms are dominant. Since both morphisms are morphisms of projective schemes, they are then surjective.

Let
U
be a dense open subset of
Z
such that the fiber
S_{z}
over a point
z
in
U
is irreducible. For
z
general in
Z
, according to Proposition
1.2.2.1
the image by
h:Y\rightarrow S
of each irreducible component of
Y_{z}
dominates one of the irreducible components of
S_{z}
, which is irreducible since
z
is general in
Z
. Since, according to Lemma
1.2.2.1
, for
z
general in
Z
the fiber
Y_{z}
has a dense intersection with the dense open subset
Y_{U}
, the general fiber
Y_{z}
is irreducible. Indeed, if we denote by
Y_{z}^{1}
and
Y_{z}^{2}
two different irreducible components of
Y
, and by
V
the dense open subset of
S_{z}
such that the fiber
Y_{s}
over a point
s
in
V
is irreducible and of exepected dimension, for all
v
in
V
the intersection
Y_{z}^{1}\cap Y_{v}
is equal to
Y_{z}^{2}\cap Y_{v}
, since both are closed subschemes of maximal dimension of an irreducible scheme. Hence the intersection of
Y_{z}
with the dense open subset
Y_{z}\cap Y_{V}
is irreducible, hence
Y_{z}
is irreducible.

(2)

Let
Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}S
and
S\xrightarrow{f_{2}}S^{\prime}
be morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
(P)
and whose general fiber is irreducible. Since we consider properties of the general fiber of
Y\rightarrow S^{\prime}
, we can assume that
Y
dominates
S
. Let
U
be a dense open subset of
S
such that the fiber of
f_{1}
over a point in
U
satisfies
(P)
. For
x
general in
S^{\prime}
, the following properties are satisfied :

(a)

The fiber
S_{x}
satisfies
(P)
;

(b)

The fiber
S_{x}
has a dense open intersection with
U
;

(c)

The morphism
Y_{x}\rightarrow S_{x}
is in
\mathcal{C}
;

(d)

The general fiber of the morphism
Y_{x}\rightarrow S_{x}
satisfies
(P)
;

(e)

Y_{x}
satisfies
(P)
.

Note that proving property
(e)
for
x
general in
S^{\prime}
is our goal here. Property
(a)
holds since
x
is general in
S^{\prime}
. According to Lemma
1.2.2.1
and since
x
is general in
S^{\prime}
, property
(b)
holds. Property
(c)
holds since
x
is general in
S^{\prime}
and according to
(1)
the general fiber of
Y\to S^{\prime}
is irreducible.
(d)
is a direct consequence of
(b)
, the fact that the fiber of
Y_{x}\rightarrow S_{x}
over a point
y
in
S_{x}
is isomorphic to the fiber
Y_{y}=Y\times_{S^{\prime}}\{y\}
, and the fact that
Y_{y}
satisfies
(P)
for
y
in
U
. Finally
(e)
is implied by
(a)
,
(c)
and
(d)
.

(3)

Note that, according to Grabber-Harris-Starr’s theorem on rational connectedness
[
GHS03
]
, if the general fiber of a morphism
Y\rightarrow S
of irreducible complex projective varieties is rationally connected, and the basis
S
is rationally connected, then
Y
is rationally connected. Hence, according to
2.
, the property "the general fiber is irreducible and rationally connected" is stable under composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

(4)

Let
Y
,
Z
and
S
be projective complex schemes, where
Y
is a rationally connected fibration over
Z
, and
Z
is a rationally connected fibration over
S
.
Denote by
f:Y\rightarrow Z
and
g:Z\rightarrow S
the associated morphisms. First, note that since
Y
dominates
Z
and
Z
dominates
S
,
Y
dominates
S
. Furthermore, each irreducible component
S_{i}
of
S
is dominated by exactly one irreducible component
Z_{i}
of
Z
, which itself is dominated by exactly one irreducible component
Y_{i}
of
Y
; hence there is a one-to-one correspondence between irreducible components of
Y
and
S
. Finally, since by hypothesis the general fibers of
Y_{i}\to Z_{i}
and
Z_{i}\to S_{i}
are rationally connected, according to
(3)
the general fiber of
Y_{i}\to S_{i}
is a rationally connected variety. Hence
Y
is a rationally connected fibration over
S
.

∎

1.2.3
Restriction to a general subvariety.

We study here the restriction of a morphism to a general subvariety.
A typical application of the proposition below will be the study of the image of a Gromov-Witten variety. More precisely, consider
\pi:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
the projection of a flag variety onto another flag variety, and denote by
\Pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X^{\prime},(\pi)_{*}\mathbf{d})
the morphism induced by
\pi
between moduli spaces of genus zero stable maps, where
\mathbf{d}
is the class of a curve in
X
. We thus obtain the commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X^{\prime},(\pi)_{*}\mathbf{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\prime})^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{r}}

to which we will apply the following proposition.

Proposition 1.2.3.1
.

Let
G
be an integral group scheme, let
f:M\rightarrow Z
,
f^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
,
\pi:Z\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
be projective morphisms, of integral noetherian schemes, where
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
admit a transitive
G
-action, and the morphism
\pi
is
G
-equivariant. Let
Y
be an integral subscheme of
Z
.
Suppose there exists a proper morphism
h:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Furthermore, suppose the morphism
h\times f:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z
induced by the morphisms
M\xrightarrow{h}M^{\prime}
and
M\xrightarrow{{f}}Z
is surjective.

Consider a point
g
general in
G
. We call
\mathcal{W}_{g}:=f^{-1}(gY)
,
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}:=(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{g})
, and
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}:=h(\mathcal{W}_{g})
. We denote by
p_{1}:M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z\rightarrow M^{\prime}
the first projection. Then :

i)

\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}gY
and
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(g\pi(Y))
;

ii)

\mathcal{W}_{g}
,
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}
are pure dimensional schemes ;

iii)

The restriction of
h\times f
to any irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}
is dominant onto an irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
;

iv)

The restriction of
p_{1}
to any irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
is dominant onto an irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let us introduce the following notation. Let
f:Y\rightarrow Z
and
\varphi:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
be morphisms of schemes, where an integral group scheme
G
acts on
Z
. We denote by
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
the fiber product induced by the morphism
\varphi_{g}:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
given by
y\rightarrow g\cdot\varphi(y)
, i.e.
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
is the fiber product associated with the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Notice that, if
G
acts on
Y^{\prime}
and
\varphi
is
G
-equivariant, the fiber product
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
will simply be the usual fiber product associated with the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gY^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Lemma 1.2.3.1
.

Let
Y\rightarrow Z
be a morphism of schemes, of finite presentation, where
Y
is an equidimensional noetherian scheme ; let
\varphi:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
be a morphism of integral schemes. Let
V
be a dense open subset of
Y
. Suppose there exists an integral group scheme
G
such that
G
acts transitively on
Z
.

Then, for a point
g
general in
G
:

i)

The scheme
V\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
is a dense subset of
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
;

ii)

The scheme
(gV)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}
is a dense subset of
(gY)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}
.

Démonstration.

i)

Since
Y
is a noetherian scheme,
Y\setminus V
can be written as the union of a finite number of irreducible schemes, i.e.
Y\setminus V=\cup_{i=1}^{N}F_{i}
, where the
F_{i}
are irreducible closed subschemes of
Y
, of dimension smaller than the pure dimension of
Y
. Consider an element
g
general in
G
. Suppose the scheme
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
is not empty. Then, according to Kleiman’s transversality theorem
[
Kle74
]
, it has pure dimension given by
\mathrm{dim}Y+\mathrm{dim}Y^{\prime}-\mathrm{dim}Z^{\prime}
. Furthermore, for
1\leq i\leq N
, the scheme
F_{i}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY^{\prime})
is either empty or of pure dimension smaller than
\mathrm{dim}Y+\mathrm{dim}Y^{\prime}-\mathrm{dim}Z^{\prime}
. Hence
V\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY^{\prime})=(Y\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{N}F_{i})\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY^{\prime})
is dense in
Y\times_{Z}(gY^{\prime})
.

ii)

Consider a point
g
general in
G
. Then, according to part
i).
, the scheme
V\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
is a dense subset of
Y\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
. Furthermore, notice that we have an isomorphism
(gY)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
. Indeed, if we denote by
g^{-1}:Z\rightarrow Z
the isomorphism of
Z
induced by the action of
g^{-1}
, and by
g:Z\rightarrow Z
the isomorphism of
Z
induced by the action of
g
, the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

implies that any morphism
T\rightarrow(gY)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}
factors through
Y\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
, and reciprocally any morphism
T\rightarrow Y\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
factors through
(gY)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}
.

Finally, in the same tautological way, notice we have an isomorphism
(gV)\times_{Z}Y^{\prime}\simeq V\times_{Z}(g^{-1}Y^{\prime})
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
1.2.3.1
.

i)

First, since, by assumption,
h\times f:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z
is surjective,
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}=(h\times f)\left(f^{-1}(gY)\right)=(h\times f)(M)\cap(M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY))
is equal to
M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY)
. Furthermore,
h(f^{-1}(gY))
is equal to the image of
(h\times f)(f^{-1}(gY))
by the first projection
p_{1}
, where
p_{1}
is defined by the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h\times f)(M)=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Meanwhile,
(f^{\prime})^{-1}(\pi(gY))
is equal to the image of
M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY)
by the first projection
p_{1}
, hence the equality between
h(f^{-1}(gY))
and
(f^{\prime})^{-1}(\pi(gY))
.

Finally, since
\pi
is
G
-equivariant, this yields :
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(g\pi(Y))
.

ii)

According to Kleiman’s transversality theorem, since
g
is general in
G
, the schemes
\mathcal{W}_{g}=f^{-1}(gY)
,
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}gY
and
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(g\pi(Y))
are either empty or pure dimensional schemes.

iii)

We consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Denote by
U
a dense open subset of
(h\times f)(M)
such that, for all
z
in
U
,
(h\times f)^{-1}(z)
has pure dimension equal to
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
. Fix an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{0}
of
\mathcal{W}_{g}
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
the variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
minus the intersection of
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
with other irreducible components o
\mathcal{W}_{g}
. We first compute the dimension of
(h\times f)^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
, where
x
is a point in
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ})\cap U
, before computing the dimension of
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
.

Computation of
{\rm dim}(h\times f))^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
.
First, the fiber of the restriction
(h\times f)_{|\mathcal{W}_{g}}
of the morphism
h\times f
to
\mathcal{W}_{g}
is isomorphic to the fiber of
h\times f
. Indeed, for
(m,z)
in
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{g})=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}gY
, the fiber
{(h\times f)_{|\mathcal{W}_{g}}}^{-1}(m,z)
is equal to
(h\times f)^{-1}(m,z)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}=h^{-1}(m)\cap f^{-1}(z)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}
, where
z
lies in
gY
. Meanwhile, we observe
f^{-1}(z)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}=f^{-1}(z)\cap f^{-1}(gY)=f^{-1}(z)
, hence
{(h\times f)_{|\mathcal{W}_{g}}}^{-1}(m)
is equal to
(h\times f)^{-1}(m)
.

Since
x
lies in
U
, this implies
{(h\times f)}^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
. Hence the intersection of the irreducible variety
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
with
{(h\times f)}^{-1}(x)
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
. Indeed, the intersection of the open subvariety
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
of
\mathcal{W}_{g}
with
(h\times f)^{-1}(x)
is a non empty open subvariety of
(h\times f)^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}
disconnected from the other pure dimensional irreducible components of
(h\times f)^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}
. Hence
(h\times f)^{-1}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
is a scheme of pure dimension
\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
.

Computation of
{\rm dim}(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
.
Note that, since
g
is general in
G
, according to Lemma
1.2.3.1
,
(h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{W}_{g}
is dense in
\mathcal{W}_{g}
; hence
(h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}
is a dense open subset of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. Consider a general point
\nu
in
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
; since
\nu
is general and
(h\times f)\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}\right)
is dense in the irreducible variety
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
,
\nu
belongs to
(h\times f)\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}\right)
, and we have :

\displaystyle{\rm dim}\mathcal{W}_{0}-{\rm dim}(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})

\displaystyle={\rm dim}{(h\times f)}^{-1}(\nu)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}M-\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)

where the first equality is deduced from the general position of
\nu
in
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
, and the second equality holds since
\nu
is in
(h\times f)\left((h\times f)^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}\right)
. This implies :

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}\mathcal{W}_{0}-\mathrm{dim}M+\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}\mathcal{W}_{g}-\mathrm{dim}M+\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M),

where the second equality holds since
\mathcal{W}_{g}
is equidimensional. Since
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
is obviously contained in
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}=(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{g})
, and this dimension does not depend on the irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}
considered, this implies the irreducible variety
(h\times f)(\mathcal{W}_{0})
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
, which has pure dimension equal to
\mathrm{dim}\mathcal{W}_{g}-\mathrm{dim}M+\mathrm{dim}(h\times f)(M)
.

iv)

First, denote by
\pi_{|Y}:Y\rightarrow\pi(Y)
the restriction of the morphism
\pi
to
Y
. Fix a dense open subset
U
of
\pi(Y)
such that, for all
x
in
U
, the fiber
\pi_{|Y}^{-1}(x)
is a scheme of pure dimension equal to
\mathrm{dim}Y-\mathrm{dim}\pi(Y)
.

We consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

where
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are respectively the first and second projection morphisms. Consider a general element
g
in
G
. We denote by
p_{1}^{\prime}
the restriction of the morphism
p_{1}
to
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}gY
. We want to prove here that the image of any irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
by
p_{1}^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(g\pi(Y))
. In order to do this, we study the dimension of the fiber
(p_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(m)
of
p_{1}^{\prime}
at a point
m
in
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}
.

We will use the following observations.

Lemma 1.2.3.2
.

Let
f^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
and
\pi:Z\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
be projective morphisms of integral noetherian schemes. Let
Y
be an integral subscheme of
Z
. Suppose there exists an integral group scheme
G
such that
G
acts transitively on
Z
, and
\pi
is
G
-equivariant. Consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

where
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are respectively the first and second projection morphisms.

(a)

Consider an element
g
in
G
. The fiber
p_{1}^{-1}(m)\cap{p_{2}}^{-1}(gY)
of the restriction of the morphism
p_{1}
to
(p_{2})^{-1}(gY)
is isomorphic to
{\pi}_{|Y}^{-1}(g^{-1}f^{\prime}(m))
, where we denote by
\pi_{|Y}:Y\rightarrow\pi(Y)
the restriction of the morphism
\pi
to
Y
;

(b)

Consider a dense open subset
U
of
Y
. Then, for an element
g
general in
G
,
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gU)
is dense in
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gY)
.

Proof of Lemma
1.2.3.2
.

(a)

Denote by
h^{\prime}
the restriction of the morphism
p_{1}
to
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gY)
. The fiber
(h^{\prime})^{-1}(m)
is equal to
\{m\}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY)
, which is isomorphic to
\pi^{-1}(f^{\prime}(m))\cap(gY)
. Furthermore, since
\pi
is
G
-equivariant, the morphism
y\rightarrow g^{-1}y
induces an isomorphism between
\pi^{-1}(f^{\prime}(m))\cap(gY)
and
\pi^{-1}(g^{-1}f^{\prime}(m))\cap(Y)
. Finally, notice
\pi^{-1}(g^{-1}f^{\prime}(m))\cap(Y)
is equal to
{\pi}_{|Y}^{-1}(g^{-1}f^{\prime}(m))
.

(b)

According to Lemma
1.2.3.1

ii)
, since
g
is general in
G
, the scheme
M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gU)
is dense in
M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY)
. Since the morphism
\pi
is
G
-equivariant, we have here the following equalities :
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gY)=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gY)
and
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gU)=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}(gU)
. Hence the scheme
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gU)
is dense in
{p_{2}}^{-1}(gY)
.

∎

Fix an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
of
p_{2}^{-1}(gY)=M^{\prime}\times_{Z^{\prime}}gY
. For
\mu
general in
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
, we have :

\displaystyle{\rm dim}\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}-{\rm dim}p_{1}(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\rm dim}p_{1}^{-1}(p_{1}(\mu))\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\rm dim}\pi_{|Y}^{-1}\left(g^{-1}\cdot(f^{\prime}\circ p_{1})(\mu)\right)

\displaystyle={\rm dim}Y-{\rm dim}\pi(Y),

where the first equality holds since the image of a dense open subset of
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
by
p_{1}
is a dense open subset of
p_{1}(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime})
, the second equality is given by Lemma
1.2.3.2

(a)
, and the last equality holds since
(f^{\prime}\circ p_{1})(\mu)
is in
g\cdot\pi(U)
for
\mu
general in
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
. Indeed, according to Lemma
1.2.3.2

(b)
,
p_{2}^{-1}(g\pi^{-1}(U))
is dense in
p_{2}^{-1}(gY)
, hence
p_{2}^{-1}(g\pi^{-1}(U))\cap\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
is dense in
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
, hence a point in
(p_{2}\circ\pi)^{-1}(gU)=(f^{\prime}\circ p_{1})^{-1}(gU)
is general in
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
.

Finally, notice that, since
g
is general in
G
, any irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime}
will be of dimension equal to
\mathrm{dim}M^{\prime}+\mathrm{dim}Y-\mathrm{dim}Z^{\prime}
, hence :

\mathrm{dim}p_{1}(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime})=\mathrm{dim}M^{\prime}+\mathrm{dim}\pi(Y)-\mathrm{dim}Z^{\prime},

which is equal to the pure dimension of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(g\pi(Y))
since
g
is general in
G
. Since
p_{1}(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime})
is obviously contained in
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}
, this implies that
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}
dominates an irreducible component of
\mathcal{W}_{g}^{\prime\prime}
.

∎

1.2.4
Application to Gromov-Witten varieties.

Consider a semi-simple algebraic group
G
, a maximal torus
T
of
G
, a Borel subgroup
B
and parabolic subgroups
P
and
P^{\prime}
satisfying
T\subset B\subset P\subset P^{\prime}
. Denote by
X:=G/P
and
X^{\prime}:=G/P^{\prime}
the corresponding homogeneous varieties, and by

\pi:X=G/P\rightarrow X^{\prime}=G/P^{\prime}

the natural projection. For an element
u
in the Weyl group
W:=N_{G}(T)/T
, denote by
X(u):=\overline{BuP}/P
the associated Schubert variety. Note that
\pi(X(u))=X^{\prime}(u)
.
Let
r\geq 0
,
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in
W
, and
\mathbf{d}
be a degree in the semi-group
E(G/P)
of effective classes of curves in
H_{2}(G/P,\mathbb{Z})
. Note that the morphism
\pi:G/P\rightarrow G/P^{\prime}
induces a morphism :

\Pi\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(G/P,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(G/P^{\prime},\pi_{*}\mathbf{d})\times_{(G/P^{\prime})^{r}}(G/P)^{r},

where we denote by
\Pi:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(G/P,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(G/P^{\prime},\pi_{*}\mathbf{d})
the morphism induced by
\pi
. For an element
g=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
in
G^{r}
, we will consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(G/P,\mathbf{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P)^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}g_{i}X(u_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(G/P^{\prime},\pi_{*}\mathbf{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P^{\prime})^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}g_{i}X^{\prime}(u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm ev}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

This commutative diagram allows us to define the following fiber products :

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{g}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})\times_{X^{r}}\prod_{i}g_{i}X(u_{i})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})
