\Pi_{J\geq 1}\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{J},{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Fl_{I},(\pi_{I/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(Fl_{I})^{r}}(Fl_{J})^{r}
is surjective.

Note that since there is no integer
i_{k^{\prime}}
such that
i_{k^{\prime}}<j_{k}
, the collection
\{I,J,{\mathbf{d}}\}
does not have to satisfy any other condition. Furthermore, according to Proposition
1.6.0.1

i)
, if
d_{0}=0\leq\lfloor\frac{d_{2}}{i_{1}}\rfloor
and
d_{1}\geq d_{0}+(j_{1}-0)(\lfloor\frac{d_{2}-d_{0}}{i_{1}-0}\rfloor+1)=j_{1}(\lfloor\frac{d_{2}}{i_{1}}\rfloor+1)
, then the morphism
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}}(Fl_{J},{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}}(Fl_{I},(\pi_{I/J})_{*}{\mathbf{d}})
is surjective. Note that the first condition is always satisfied. Hence according to Proposition
1.6.0.1

ii)
, if

d_{1}\geq j_{1}(\lfloor\frac{d_{2}}{i_{1}}\rfloor+1)+r(j_{1}-0)=j_{1}(\lfloor\frac{d_{2}}{i_{1}}\rfloor+r+1)

then the morphism
\Pi_{J\geq 1}\times ev
is surjective.

To conclude, we observe the following property :

if
d_{1}\geq j_{1}\left(r+1+\lfloor d_{2}/i_{1}\rfloor\right)
the collection
(I,J,(d_{1},d_{2},\dots d_{m+1}))
is stabilized.

2.

Projection from a Grassmannian to the point.
Suppose
J=\{k\}
and
I=\emptyset
. Then
Fl_{J}
is the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(k,n)
and
Fl_{J}={\rm Gr}(k,n)\rightarrow Fl_{I}={\rm Spec}{\mathbb{C}}
is the projection to the point. By definition, the collection
\{I,J,d\}
is stabilized iff the morphism

\Pi_{J\geq 1}\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}({\rm Gr}(k,n),d)\rightarrow\overline{{M}_{0,r}}\times({\rm Gr}(k,n))^{r}

is surjective. Since the projection
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}}({\rm Gr}(k,n),d)\rightarrow\overline{{M}_{0,3}}={\rm Spec}{\mathbb{C}}
is always surjective, according to Proposition
1.6.0.1

ii)
, if

d\geq r(k-0)=rk,

then
\Pi_{J\geq 1}\times ev
is surjective. Hence :

if
d\geq rk
the collection
\{I,J,d\}
is stabilized.

Finally, since the collection
\{I,J,d\}
is stabilized, according to Lemma
1.5.3.1
, the dual collection
\{I^{*},J^{*},d^{*}=d\}
is also stabilized. Note that
Fl_{J^{*}}={\rm Gr}(n-k,n)
. Hence :

if
d\geq r(n-k)
the collection
\{I,J,d\}
is also stabilized.

Appendix A

Let
Y
be an irreducible projective complex variety, let
\gamma\in H_{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, and
r\geq 0
. We first recall the usual definitions associated with the space of stable maps
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
.
The data of a morphism
\varphi:C\rightarrow Y
from a genus zero projective connected reduced and nodal curve
C
to
Y
along with
r
distinct non singular points
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\}
on
C
is called a stable map if any irreducible component of
C
getting sent to a point contains at least three points which are nodal or marked.

Let
S
be a complex scheme. A family of
r
-pointed quasi-stable maps over
S
consists of the data of morphisms
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Y
and
\pi:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow S
along with
r
sections
p_{i}:S\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, such that each geometric fiber is a genus zero projective connected reduced and nodal curve
C_{s}
and the marked points
p_{i}(s)
are distinct and non singular. By construction,
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
is the coarse moduli space parametrizing isomorphism classes of families over
S
of
r
-pointed, quasi-stable maps from genus zero curves to
Y
representing the class
\gamma
, and such that each geometric fiber
\mathcal{C}_{s}
over a point in
S
defines a stable map
\mathcal{C}_{s}\rightarrow Y

[
FP96
]
.

Lemma 1.6.1.1
.

Fix distinct points
p_{1}
,
\dots
,
p_{r}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
Let
S
be a complex scheme, and let
\mu:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow Y
be a morphism such that, for all closed points
s\in S
, the restriction
\mu_{s}
of
\mu
to
s\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
verifies
\mu_{s*}[\mathbb{P}^{1}]=\gamma\in A_{1}(Y)
. Let
pr_{1}
be the natural projection from
S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
S
, and
p_{i}:S\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
1\leq i\leq r
, be the morphisms respectively defined by
p_{i}:s\rightarrow(s,p_{i})
.
Then the family

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}

together with the sections
p_{i}:S\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a stable family on
S
of maps from
r
-pointed genus
0
curves to
Y
representing the class
\gamma
.

Furthermore, the morphism
f:S\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)}
associated with this family sends a point
s\in S
into the point of
\mathcal{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)
associated with
[\mu_{s}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow Y,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\}]
.

Démonstration.

Let
s
be a closed point of
S
. Then the geometric fiber in
s
verifies
pr_{1}^{-1}(s)=(S\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\times_{S}{\rm Spec}(\mathbb{C}(s))\simeq{\rm Spec}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, i.e. is an irreducible rational projective curve. Hence
pr_{1}^{-1}(s)
is a projective, connected, smooth, reduced, rational curve with
r
distinct non singular points.

Since
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{C})=0
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is flat on
{\rm Spec}\mathbb{Z}
, and
pr_{1}:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S
is a flat morphism. Furthermore,
pr_{1}
may be written down as
S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{1}\times{\rm Spec}\mathbb{C}\times S\rightarrow{\rm Spec}\mathbb{C}\times S\simeq S
, hence is a projective morphism. The family
(pr_{1}:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\})
is thus a family of
r
-pointed genus
0
quasi stable curves on
S
.

For
s
in
S
, denote by
\mu_{s}:C_{s}\rightarrow Y
the restriction of
\mu
to the geometric fiber
C_{s}:=pr_{1}^{-1}(s)
. Note that
\mu_{s}
is a map from the
r
-pointed quasi-stable map
(C_{s},\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\})
to
Y
. The map
\mu_{s}
is stable, and by hypothesis represents the class
\gamma
. The family
(pr_{1}:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow Y)
hence is a stable family on
S
of maps from
r
-pointed genus
0
curves to
Y
representing the class
\gamma
, i.e.
o=(pr_{1}:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow Y)/\sim
is an element of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)(S)
.

Let
s:\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow S
be a point of
S
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
is a coarse moduli space, the functor
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
associates to
s
the map from
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)(S)\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C}))
associating to a stable family
(\pi:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow S,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Y)/\sim
corresponding to an element in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)(S)
the isomorphism class of :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}(\mathbb{C})\times_{S}\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}(\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

We observe
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)(s)
sends
o
to the element in
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C}))
associated with
(C_{s},\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu_{s}:C_{s}\rightarrow Y)
.

Since
\overline{{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)
is a coarse moduli space, there exists a natural transformation of functors :

\Phi:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}om_{Sch}(S,\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)})

Let
f:S\rightarrow\overline{{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)}
be the morphism associated by
\Phi
with
o=(pr_{1}:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow Y)/\sim
, i.e.
f=\Phi(o)
. We obtain :

\Phi(S)(o)\circ s=\Phi(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C}))(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(Y,\gamma)(s)),

i.e.
f
sends
s
to the point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}(Y,\gamma)}
associated with
(C_{s},\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\},\mu_{s})
.
∎

Chapitre 2
A comparison formula between genus
0
correlators of partial flag varieties

2.1
Introduction

Removing a subspace from a partial flag gives another partial flag composed of fewer subspaces. This induces a forgetful map between the corresponding flag varieties.
The main result of this chapter is that quantum
K
-theoretical genus 0 correlators of these two flag varieties are equal to each other, provided their degree is high enough.

2.1.1
Genus zero correlators of
G/P
.

Quantum
K
-theory takes its simplest form for homogeneous varieties, and genus zero quantum
K
-theory actually first introduced and defined in this setting by Givental some twenty years ago
[
Giv00
]
.

Let
X
be a homogeneous variety
G/P
, where
G
is an algebraic group, and
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
G
. Fix a degree
d
in the semi-group
E(X)
of effective classes of
1
-cycles in
A_{1}(X)\simeq H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}(X,d)
the moduli space parametrizing stable maps
(\mu:C\rightarrow X,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\})
from a genus zero curve
C
to
X
verifying
\mu_{*}[C]={d}
, with
n
marked points
p_{1}
,
\dots
,
p_{n}
on
C
. If
X
is a homogeneous variety,
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}(X,d)
is an irreducible rational variety
[
KP01
,
Tho98
]
, and the subvariety
{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}(X,d)
parametrizing stable maps
(\mu:\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow X,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\})
from the projective line to
X
is a dense open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}(X,d)

[
FP96
]
.
The
evaluation morphism

{\rm ev}_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}(X,d)\rightarrow X
assigns to a map the image of its
i
-th marked point. If
X
is a homogeneous variety, the evaluation morphism is flat.

Consider an algebraic group
H
acting on
X=G/P
. Let
\phi_{1}
,
\dots
,
\phi_{r}
be elements in the Grothendieck ring
K_{H}(X)
of
H
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
X
. The
equivariant correlator

\langle\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\rangle_{H,d}^{X}
of elements
\phi_{i}
in
K_{\circ}(X)
is defined as :

\langle\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\rangle_{H,d}^{X}:=\chi_{H}\left({\rm ev}_{1}^{*}(\phi_{1})\cdot\>\dots\>\cdot{\rm ev}_{r}^{*}(\phi_{r})\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d)}]\right),

where
\chi_{H}
denotes the
H
-equivariant sheaf Euler characteristic.

2.1.2
H
-equivariant decomposition of the diagonal.

Let
H
be a complex linear algebraic group, let
X
be an irreducible variety with an
H
-action. Let
R(H)
denote the representation ring of
H
, and
K_{H}(X)
denote the Grothendieck ring of
H
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
X
. We say
X

admits a diagonal decomposition in
K_{H}(X)
if there exists a basis
(\alpha)_{i\in I}
of the
R(H)
-module
K_{H}(X)
, and a dual basis
(\alpha^{\vee})_{i\in I}
of
K^{H}(X)
such that

\Delta_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\alpha\boxtimes(\alpha^{\vee}\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}])\in K_{H}(X\times X),

where
\Delta:X\rightarrow X\times X
is the diagonal embedding, and
(\alpha^{\vee})_{i\in I}
is the dual basis of
(\alpha)_{i\in I}
with respect to the pairing given by the
H
-equivariant sheaf Euler characteristic, i.e.
\chi_{H}(\alpha^{\vee}\otimes\beta)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
for all elements
\alpha
,
\beta
in
K_{H}(X)
.

We study here the following setting. Consider a complex linear algebraic subgroup
H
of
{\rm GL}_{n}
acting on flag varieties via left multiplication such that flag varieties
{\rm GL}_{n}/P
admit a diagonal decomposition in
K_{H}(X)
. Note that this is in particular true when considering the non equivariant setting or, according to
[
GK08
]
, when considering the action of a maximal torus
T
of
{\rm GL}_{n}
.

2.1.3
Main result.

Fix two positive integers
m<n
. Let
0<n_{1}<\dots<n_{m}<n
be a strictly increasing collection of non negative integers. Denote by
X
the
m
-step flag variety parametrizing
m
-tuples of vector spaces ordered by inclusion

0\subset V_{n_{1}}\subset\dots\subset V_{n_{m}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}

such that
{\rm dim}V_{n_{i}}=n_{i}
.
Fix a positive integer
k
,
1\leq k\leq m
. Denote by
X_{\widehat{k}}
the
(m-1)
-step flag variety obtained from
X
by forgetting the
k
-th vector subspace. Denote by
\pi_{\widehat{k}}:X\rightarrow X_{\widehat{k}}
the projection.
The class
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
of a curve
C
in
X
is determined by non-negative integers
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
,
\dots
,
d_{m}
, where
d_{i}
is the Plücker degree of the projection of
C
to the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(n_{i},n)
. Note that
\pi_{\widehat{k}}
and the evaluation morphism
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X^{r}
induce a morphism
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\times_{{X_{\widehat{k}}}^{r}}X^{r}
. Denote by
H
an algebraic subgroup of
G
acting via left multiplication on
X
and
X_{\widehat{k}}
, such that
X_{\widehat{k}}
admits a diagonal decomposition in
K_{H}(X_{\widehat{k}})
.

Theorem 2.1.3.1
.

Let
r>0
,
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be a nef class of
1
-cycles such that
\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
and such that the morphism

\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\underset{{X_{\widehat{k}}}^{r}}{\times}X^{r}

is surjective.
Then for all elements
\phi_{1}
,
\dots
,
\phi_{r}
in
K_{H}(X)
, the correlators of
X
and
X_{\widehat{k}}
associated with
H
,
\mathbf{d}
, and the elements
\phi_{i}
are equal :

\langle\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\rangle_{H,\mathbf{d}}^{X}=\langle(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}(\phi_{1}),\dots,(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}(\phi_{r})\rangle_{H,(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d}}^{X_{\widehat{k}}}.

According to Chapter
1
Proposition
1.6.0.1
, Theorem
2.1.3.1
implies the following result.

Theorem 1bis
.

Let
r>0
,
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be a nef class of
1
-cycles such that

–

\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
,

–

d_{k-1}\leq\lfloor\frac{d_{k+1}}{n_{k+1}}\rfloor
,

–

d_{k}\geq r(n_{k}-n_{k-1})+d_{k-1}+(n_{k}-n_{k-1})(\lfloor\frac{d_{k+1}-d_{k-1}}{n_{k+1}-n_{k-1}}\rfloor+1)
.

Then for any elements
\phi_{1}
,
\dots
,
\phi_{r}
in
K_{H}(X)
, the correlators of
X
and
X_{\widehat{k}}
associated with
H
,
\mathbf{d}
, and the elements
\phi_{i}
are equal :

\langle\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\rangle_{H,\mathbf{d}}^{X}=\langle(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}(\phi_{1}),\dots,(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}(\phi_{r})\rangle_{H,(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d}}^{X_{\widehat{k}}}.

This Chapter is organized as follows. Sections
2.2
is devoted to recalling some standard facts about equivariant algebraic
K
-theory, and fixing the notations we use in the rest of the chapter. A proof of Theorem
2.1.3.1
is presented in Section
2.3
, using results of Chapter
1
. In the non equivariant setting a more geometric proof of Theorem
2.1.3.1
is provided in part
2.4
, relying heavily on results from Chapter
1
.

2.2
Preliminaries on equivariant algebraic
K
-theory

2.2.1
Diagonal decomposition of
K_{T}(X)
.

Recall
G
is a semi-simple linear complex algebraic group,
T
is a maximal torus of
G
,
B
is a Borel subgroup of
G
and
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
G
such that
T\subset B\subset P
.
For any element
u
in the Weyl group
W
recall
X(u):=\overline{BuP/P}
is a Schubert variety of
X=G/P
, and
Y(u):=w_{0}\cdot\overline{Bw_{0}uP/P}
is the Schubert variety opposite to it. Furthermore, denote by
\partial Y(u):=Y(u)\setminus(w_{0}Bw_{0}uP/P)
the complementary of the dense open Schubert cell in
Y(u)
.
According to
[
GK08
]
we have the following decomposition in
K_{T}(X)
.

\Delta_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]=\sum_{u\in W^{P}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(u)}]\boxtimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y(u)}(-\partial Y(u))]\in K_{T}(X\times X),

where
\Delta:X\rightarrow X\times X
denotes the diagonal embedding. Note that this was first observed in the non equivariant setting by Brion
[
Bri02
]
.

2.2.2
Equivariant flat base change.

We describe here the class of an equivariant flat base change. The main application of this result will be the following setting. Let
H
be a linear subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{n}
, let
X
be the flag variety parametrizing flags of vector spaces
V_{n_{1}}\subset\dots\subset V_{n_{m}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, let
X_{\widehat{k}}
be the flag variety obtained by forgetting the vector
V_{n_{k}}
, and denote by
\pi_{\widehat{k}}:X\rightarrow X_{\widehat{k}}
the associated morphism. For a class
\beta
of curve on
X_{\widehat{k}}
, we consider the fiber product described by the following
H
-equivariant Cartesian diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}\underset{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}{\times}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}

Note that, since the action of
({\rm GL}_{n})^{r}
on
(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
is transitive, the morphism
(\pi_{\widehat{k}})^{r}:X^{r}\rightarrow(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
is flat.

Consider two morphisms of schemes
f_{1}:M_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
and
f_{2}:M_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
. We denote by
f_{1}\times f_{2}:M_{1}\times M_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}\times X_{2}
the morphism induced by
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, i.e. the morphism induced by the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\times M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\times f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where
p_{i}:M_{1}\times M_{2}\rightarrow M_{i}
and
\pi_{i}:X_{1}\times X_{2}\rightarrow X_{i}
are the projection morphisms. Finally, let
H
be a complex linear algebraic acting on
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. We denote by

\displaystyle\boxtimes:\>\>\>K^{H}(M_{1})\times K^{H}(M_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow K^{H}(M_{1}\times M_{2})

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)\>\>\>

\displaystyle\rightarrow(p_{1}^{*}\phi)\otimes(p_{2}^{*}\psi)

the external product.

Proposition 2.2.2.1
.

Let
H
be a linear algebraic group. Consider two equivariant morphisms of projective varieties with an
H
-action
f_{1}:M_{1}\rightarrow Y
and
f_{2}:M_{2}\rightarrow Y
, such that
M_{1}\rightarrow Y
is flat. Denote by
\Delta:Y\rightarrow Y\times Y
the diagonal morphism,
p_{i}:M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}\rightarrow M_{i}
the projection morphisms, and
i:M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}\times M_{2}
the natural morphism ; i.e. we consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\times M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\times f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Suppose
Y
is non singular. Let
(\phi_{e})_{e\in I}
be a basis of
K_{H}(Y)\simeq K^{H}(Y)
. Suppose there exists a decomposition of the diagonal in
K_{H}(Y)
, i.e. suppose there exists a basis
(\phi_{e}^{\vee})_{e\in I}
of
K_{H}(Y)
such that

\Delta_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]=\sum_{e\in I}\phi_{e}\boxtimes\phi_{e}^{\vee}\in K_{H}(Y\times Y)

where we consider the diagonal action of
H
on
Y\times Y
. We then obtain :

i_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}]=\sum_{e,f\in I}g^{ef}\big(f_{1}^{*}\phi_{e}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}\phi_{f}\big)\in K_{H}(M_{1}\times M_{2}),

where we denote by
g^{ef}:=g^{-1}_{ef}
the pairing dual to
g_{ef}
, and consider the diagonal action of
H
on
M_{1}\times M_{2}
and
M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}
.

Lemma 2.2.2.1

(Flat base change in equivariant
K
-theory)
.

Let
H
be a linear algebraic group. Consider a Cartesian diagram of proper, equivariant morphisms of varieties with an
H
-action :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is a flat morphism. Then, for all
\alpha
in
K_{H}(X)
:

f^{\prime}_{*}(g^{\prime*}\alpha)=g^{*}(f_{*}\alpha)\in K_{H}(S).

Démonstration.

Cf.
[
C+09
]
Proposition 5.3.15.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.2.2.1
.

We call
f:=f_{1}\circ p_{1}=f_{2}\circ p_{2}
. First, notice that, since
f_{1}
and
p_{1}
are flat,
f
is a flat
H
-equivariant morphism. We hence have :
[\mathcal{O}_{M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}]=f^{*}([\mathcal{O}_{Y}])
.
Now consider the diagonal action of
H
on
M_{1}\times M_{2}
and
Y\times Y
. Since
\Delta:Y\rightarrow Y\times Y
and
i:M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}\times M_{2}
are proper
H
-equivariant morphism,
f_{1}\times f_{2}
is an
H
-equivariant morphism, and the morphism
f_{1}
is flat, we can apply Lemma
2.2.2.1
to the following commutative diagram of
H
-equivariant maps.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

We obtain :

i_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}]=i_{*}f^{*}[\mathcal{O}_{Y}]=(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}\Delta_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{Y}]\in K_{H}(Y)

Furthermore, we have supposed :
\Delta_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{Y}]=\sum_{e,f\in I}g^{ef}\phi_{e}\boxtimes\phi_{f}\in K_{H}(Y)\simeq K^{H}(Y)
. We thus obtain :

i_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{M_{1}\times_{Y}M_{2}}]=(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}\big(\sum_{e,f\in I}g^{ef}\phi_{e}\boxtimes\phi_{f}\big)=\sum_{e,f\in I}g^{ef}\big(f_{1}^{*}\phi_{e}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}\phi_{f}\big)\in K_{H}(M_{1}\times M_{2}).

∎

2.3
Proof of Theorem
2.1.3.1
.

2.3.1
Properties of
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
.

We use here the same notations as in the preceding parts. Let
X
be the flag variety parametrizing flags of vector spaces
V_{n_{1}}\subset\dots\subset V_{n_{m}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
. For
1\leq k\leq m
, we denote by
X_{\widehat{k}}
the flag variety obtained from
X
by forgetting the
k
-th vector space, and call
\pi=\pi_{\widehat{k}}:X\rightarrow X_{\widehat{k}}
the forgetful morphism. For a class
\beta
of curve on
X_{\widehat{k}}
, we denote by
M^{\prime}:=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\beta)
the moduli space parametrizing stable maps from genus
0

r
-pointed curves to
X_{\widehat{k}}
. We observe here that the scheme
M^{\prime}\times_{{X_{\widehat{k}}}^{r}}X^{r}
associated with the following Cartesian diagram is a projective variety with rational singularities, obtained via flat base change of
M^{\prime}
over
(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}\times_{{X_{\widehat{k}}}^{r}}X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}

Lemma 2.3.1.1
.

\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
is a projective variety with rational singularities.

Démonstration.

For simplicity set
M^{\prime}:=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})
. First, notice that since
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
is a projective base change, it is projective over the projective scheme
X^{r}
and hence over
{\rm Spec}{\mathbb{C}}
.
Furthermore, according to Kleiman’s transversality theorem, for
g
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
the scheme
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}g\cdot X^{r}
is reduced. Since the morphism
X^{r}\rightarrow(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
is
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
-equivariant, and the action of
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
on
(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
is transitive, the action of
g^{-1}
induces an isomorphism
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}g\cdot X^{r}\simeq M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
. Hence
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
is reduced. Note that according to Theorem
0.4.0.1
,
M^{\prime}
has rational singularities.
Hence, according to
[
CMBP13
]
, Theorem 2.5, since all varieties considered here have rational singularities, for
g
general in
{\rm GL}_{n}^{r}
the scheme
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}g\cdot X^{r}
has rational singularities. Hence
M^{\prime}\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}
has rational singularities.
∎

Finally, note that since
\pi_{\widehat{k}}:X\rightarrow X_{\widehat{k}}
is a fibration in Grassmanians, it is flat.

2.3.2
Proof of Theorem
2.1.3.1
.

We denote by
i:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\underset{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}{\times}X^{r}\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\times X^{r}
the natural morphism.

Let
(\phi_{e})_{e\in I}
be a basis of
K_{H}(X)\simeq K^{H}(X)
. By hypothesis there exists a basis
(\phi_{e}^{\vee})_{e\in I}
such that
\phi_{e}\boxtimes\phi_{f}
is a diagonal decomposition of
X
. Note that
X^{r}
admits a diagonal decomposition in
K_{H}(X^{r})
, which is given by
\Delta_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{r}}]=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\>\T1\i I}(\boxtimes_{1\leq i\leq r}\phi_{e_{i}})_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}(\boxtimes_{1\leq i\leq r}\phi_{e_{i}}^{\vee})_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\in K_{H}(X^{r}\times X^{r})
.

Lemma 2.3.2.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be a degree in
E(X)
such that
\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
. Suppose the morphism
\Pi\times ev:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})\underset{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}{\times}X^{r}
is surjective. Then

\displaystyle i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}\right]=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\boxtimes\left(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\right)

Démonstration.

For simplicity, set
M^{\prime}:=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},(\pi_{\widehat{k}})_{*}\mathbf{d})
,
S:=X^{r}
, and
S^{\prime}:=(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}
. According to Lemma
2.3.1.1
,
M^{\prime}\times_{S^{\prime}}S
is a projective variety with rational singularities. Furthermore, since
\Pi\times ev
is surjective,
M^{\prime}\times_{S^{\prime}}S
is irreducible. Finally, according to Chapter
1
Proposition
1.4.0.1
since
\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
the general fiber of
\Pi\times ev
is a rationally connected variety. Hence
\Pi\times ev
is an
H
-equivariant surjective morphism of irreducible projective varieties with rational singularities with rationally connected general fiber, hence according to Theorem
0.4.0.2
we have in
K_{H}(M^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}\right]

\displaystyle=i_{*}\left[{\mathcal{O}}_{(\Pi\times ev)\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}\right]

\displaystyle=i_{*}\left[{\mathcal{O}}_{M^{\prime}\times_{S^{\prime}}S}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}ev^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}\boxtimes\dots\phi_{e_{r}})\boxtimes((\pi)^{r})^{*}\left(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\boxtimes\left(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\right)

where the third equality holds since the morphism
(\pi)^{r}
is a flat
H
-equivariant morphism-cf. Lemma
2.2.2.1
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.2.2
.

Let
Y
and
Z
be projective varieties with an
H
-action. Suppose
Z
non singular and suppose both
Y
and
Z
are irreducible. Then for
\alpha\in K_{H}(Y)
,
\beta\in K_{H}(Z)\simeq K^{H}(Z)
, we have
\chi_{H}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta)=\chi_{H}(\alpha)\chi_{H}(\beta)
.

Démonstration.

We consider the following Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where we have denoted by
p_{1}:Y\times Z\rightarrow Y
and
p_{2}:Y\times Z\rightarrow Z
the
H
-equivariant projections. Since
Y
is irreducible the morphism
Y\rightarrow{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}})
is flat. Since
\chi_{H}
is the
H
-equivariant pushforward to the point, we have :

\displaystyle\chi_{H}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta)

\displaystyle=\chi_{H}(p_{1}^{*}\alpha\otimes p_{2}^{*}\beta)

\displaystyle=\chi_{H}(\alpha\otimes(p_{1})_{*}p_{2}^{*}\beta)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{1})_{*}\left(\alpha\otimes\pi_{1}^{*}((\pi_{2})_{*}\beta)\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{H}(\alpha)\chi_{H}(\beta),

where the third equality holds according to Lemma
2.2.2.1
and the last equality holds according to the projection formula.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.1.3.1
.

Let
\alpha_{1}
,
\dots
,
\alpha_{r}
be elements in
K^{H}(X)
.
Note that the following diagram is a commutative diagram of proper morphisms :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}}X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi\times ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

We obtain in
K_{H}(\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times X^{r})
:

\displaystyle i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left(ev_{1}^{*}\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes ev_{r}^{*}\alpha_{r}\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left(ev^{*}(\alpha_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\alpha_{r})\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left((p_{2}\circ i\circ(\Pi\times ev))^{*}(\alpha_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\alpha_{r})\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left((p_{2})^{*}(\alpha_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\alpha_{r})\right)\otimes i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]

\displaystyle=\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})}]\boxtimes(\alpha_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\alpha_{r})\right)\otimes i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]

\displaystyle=\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})}]\boxtimes(\alpha_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\alpha_{r})\right)\otimes\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\boxtimes\left(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\boxtimes\left((\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{1})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{r}))\right),

where the third equality holds according to the projection formula, and the last equality holds according to Lemma
2.3.2.1
. Finally, decompose each element
\pi_{*}\alpha_{i}
in the basis
(\phi_{e})_{e\in I}
of
K_{H}(X_{\widehat{k}})\simeq K^{H}(X_{\widehat{k}})
:
\pi_{*}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{e\in I}a_{i,e}\phi_{e}
. Note that we have
\chi_{H}(\pi_{*}\alpha_{i}\otimes\phi_{f}^{\vee})=a_{i,f}
. Denote by
p_{2}:\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X_{\widehat{k}},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times X^{r}\rightarrow X^{r}
the second projection, and by
p:(X_{\widehat{k}})^{r}\rightarrow{\rm Spec}{\mathbb{C}}
the
H
-equivariant projection to the point. We obtain :

\displaystyle\chi_{H}

\displaystyle\left(ev_{1}^{*}\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes ev_{r}^{*}\alpha_{r}\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=p_{*}(\pi^{r})_{*}(p_{2})_{*}i_{*}(\Pi\times ev)_{*}\left(ev_{1}^{*}\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes ev_{r}^{*}\alpha_{r}\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=p_{*}(\pi^{r})_{*}(p_{2})_{*}\left(\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\boxtimes\left((\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{1})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{r})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\chi_{H}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)p_{*}(\pi^{r})_{*}\left((\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{1}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{1})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes(\pi^{*}(\phi_{e_{r}}^{\vee})\otimes\alpha_{r})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\chi_{H}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}\chi_{H}(\phi_{e_{i}}^{\vee}\otimes\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\chi_{H}\left((ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}a_{i,e_{i}}

where the before last equality is obtained by applying the projection formula to the flat morphism
(\pi)^{r}
. Finally, we obtain :

\displaystyle\chi_{H}

\displaystyle\left(ev_{1}^{*}\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes ev_{r}^{*}\alpha_{r}\otimes[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in I}\chi_{H}\left(a_{1,e_{1}}(ev_{1}^{*}\phi_{e_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes a_{r,e_{r}}(ev_{r}^{*}\phi_{e_{r}})\right)

which yields the expected result since
\sum_{e\in I}a_{i,e}\phi_{e}=\pi_{*}\alpha_{i}
.
∎

2.4
Geometric interpretation in the non equivariant setting

Consider the case when
H
is the identity element of
G
. We provide here an alternative proof of the equality between correlators provided by Theorem
2.1.3.1
, using results of Chapter
1
.

We use here the same notations as in Chapter
1
. As usual, we say a property (P) holds for a general point in a variety
X
if (P) is true for points belonging to a dense open subset of
X
. Recall a flag variety
X
can be written as
G/P
, where
G={\rm GL}_{n}
and
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
X
containing a Borel subgroup
B\subset P\subset G
. For an element
u
in the Weyl group
W
of
G={\rm GL}_{n}
we denote by
X(u):=\overline{BuP/P}
the associated Schubert variety, and by
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}:=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(u)}]
the associated class in the Grothendieck ring
K(X)
of coherent sheaves on
X
. Let
r\geq 0
. Let
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in
W
. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})
be a degree in
E(X)
. Recall for an element
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
in
G^{r}
we call
Gromov-Witten variety associated with the
u_{i}
the variety

{{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\times_{X^{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{i}X(u_{i})

parametrizing genus zero degree
{\mathbf{d}}
stable maps into
X
sending their
i
-th marked point within the variety
g_{i}X(u_{i})
. Note that for an element
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
the correlators
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}
satisfy

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}^{X}=\chi({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X}).

The flag variety
X
parametrizes flags of vector spaces
V_{n_{1}}\subset\dots\subset V_{n_{m}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, where
0<n_{1}<\dots<n_{m}<n
. Let
X^{\prime}
be the flag variety obtained from
X
by forgetting the
k
-th vector space. Denote by
\pi:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
the forgetful map. Note that for all
i
we have
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{i}}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}(u_{i})}]={\mathcal{O}}_{u_{i}}\in K(X^{\prime})
. We obtain

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}}\rangle_{\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}}}^{X^{\prime}}=\chi({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X^{\prime}}).

Now suppose the morphism
\Pi\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X^{\prime},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{(X^{\prime})^{r}}X^{r}
is surjective. Furthermore suppose
\forall\>p<k,\>d_{k}\geq n_{k}\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}-d_{p-1}}{n_{p}-n_{p-1}}\right\rceil
. Then according to Chapter
1
Theorem
1.1.2.1
for an element
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
each irreducible component of the variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X}
surjects into a different irreducible component of the variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X^{\prime}}
, and the general fiber of the projection
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X}\rightarrow{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}^{X^{\prime}}
is a rationnally connected variety. Furthermore according to
[
CMBP13
]
Theorem
2.5
for an element
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
the variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},\dots,g_{r}}
has rational singularities. Theorem
0.4.0.2
then yields

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}^{X}=\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}},

which is the equality described by Theorem
2.1.3.1
.

Chapitre 3
Schubert calculus for
Fl_{1,n-1}

3.1
Introduction

Let
X=Fl_{1,n-1}
be the incidence variety parametrizing lines contained in hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. We study here the product of Schubert classes in the small quantum
K
-ring of
X
.
Part
3.2
is devoted to recalling some standard facts about incidence varieties, and fixing the notations we use in the rest of the chapter. The aim of parts
3.3
and
3.4
is to prove a closed formula for Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(X)
. Part
3.3
describes one parameter degenerations of Richardson varieties, from which the expression of Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(X)
is derived in Part
3.4
. The aim of Part
3.5.2
is to compute all three points quantum
K
-theoretical correlators of
X
. Note that in the context of small quantum cohomology the product of two Schubert classes is given directly by three points Gromov-Witten invariants. In small quantum
K
-theory, the product of two Schubert classes is an alternate sum involving three points correlators and two points correlators. Part
3.6
is dedicated to finding a boundary on the degree of the product of two classes in
QK_{s}(X)
. A Chevalley formula in
QK_{s}(X)
is proven in Part
3.7
. Finally we study Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
QK_{s}(X)
in Part
3.8
. We provide an algorithm computing these coefficients, and conjecture a closed formula for them. We additionally prove that they satisfy a "positivity rule".

3.2
Incidence varieties.

Let us begin by fixing down some of the usual notations associated with a generalized flag variety. Let
G:={\rm GL}_{n}
, let
T
be a maximal torus of
G
and
B
be a Borel subgroup of
G
containing
T
. A generalized flag variety can be written as
X:={\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})/P
, where
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{n}
and satisfying

T\subset B\subset P\subset G.

Let us name
W=N_{G}(T)/T\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}
the Weyl group of
G
,
W_{P}:=N_{L}(T)/T
the subgroup of
W
associated with
P
, and
W^{P}:=W/W_{P}
. For
w\in W^{P}
, note that the variety
\overline{Bw^{\prime}P/P}
does not depend on the choice of an element
w^{\prime}
in
W
representing
w
; we obtain a
Schubert variety

X(w):=\overline{BwP/P}
. The homology classes
[X(w)]
from a basis of the integral homology of
X
, where
w
runs over
W^{P}
. The classes
{\mathcal{O}}_{w}:=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w)}]
form an additive basis of the Grothendieck ring
K_{\circ}(X)
of coherent sheaves on
X
. Furthermore, we denote by
({\mathcal{I}}_{w})_{w\in W^{P}}
the dual basis of
({\mathcal{O}}_{w})
for the Euler characteristic. If we denote by
\delta_{u,v}
the symbol equal to
1
if
u=v
, and else equal to
0
, we have for all
u,v\in W^{P}
:

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{I}}_{v})=\delta_{u,v}.

For an element
w
in
W^{P}
, we denote by
\ell(w)
the length of a minimal length representant of
w
. Note that
\ell(w)={\rm dim}X(w)
.

We consider here the incidence variety
X=Fl_{1,n-1}
parametrizing lines included in hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Forgetting a line or a hyperplane yields a forgetful map
X\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
which is a fibration in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-2}
’s. Fix a basis
<e_{1},\dots,e_{n}>
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In this basis, consider the maximal torus in
G={\rm GL}_{n}
consisting of diagonal matrices, the Borel subgroup
B
consisting of upper triangular matrices of
{\rm GL}_{n}
, and the parabolic subgroup
P\supset B
associated with the reductive group
{\rm GL}_{1}\times{\rm GL}_{n-2}\times{\rm GL}_{1}
. Note that
W=N_{G}(T)/T\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We denote by
w_{0}
the element of
W
of greatest length. Note that
w_{0}
is the permutation of
1

\dots

n
defined by
w_{0}(i)=n-i+1
. Furthermore, an element in
W^{P}\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}/\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}
is uniquely determined by the choice of a permutation sending
1
to
i
and
n
to
j
, where
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
i\neq j
. We name
w_{i,j}
the element in
W^{P}
thus defined. Schubert varieties of
X\simeq{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})/P
are described by :

\displaystyle X(i,j):

\displaystyle=X(w_{i,j})=\overline{Bw_{i,j}P/P}

\displaystyle=\left\{(L,H)\in X\bigr\rvert L\subset<e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}>,\><e_{1},\cdots,e_{j-1}>\subset H\right\}.

(3.1)

Schubert varieties can be embedded in
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
as :

X(i,j)=\left\{\left([x_{1}:\dots:x_{i}:0:\dots:0],[0:\dots 0:y_{j}:\dots:y_{n}]\right)\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\biggr\rvert\sum x_{k}y_{k}=0\right\}.

(3.2)

Note that for
i<j
the condition
\sum x_{k}y_{k}=0
is redundant, and
X(i,j)
can simply be described as :

X(i,j)=\left\{\left([x_{1}:\dots:x_{i}:0:\dots:0],[0:\dots 0:y_{j}:\dots:y_{n}]\right)\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\right\}.

(3.3)

For
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
i\neq j
, we name

{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j}:=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{i,j})}]\in K_{\circ}(X)

the class of the pushforward of the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{i,j})}
in the Grothendieck group
K_{\circ}(X)
, and by
{\mathcal{I}}_{i,j}:={\mathcal{I}}_{w_{i,j}}
its dual with respect to the pairing
\chi:\alpha,\beta\rightarrow\chi(\alpha\cdot\beta)
.
For any
u\in W^{P}
, the Schubert variety
opposite
to the Schubert variety
X(u)
is

X^{u}:=w_{0}X(w_{0}u).

Applying
w_{0}
to (
3.1
) yields the following description of opposite Schubert varieties of
X
:

X^{w_{0}w_{k,l}}=w_{0}X(w_{k,l})=\left\{(L,H)\in X\bigr\rvert L\subset<e_{n-k+1},\cdots,e_{n}>,\><e_{n-l+2},\cdots,e_{n}>\subset H\right\}.

(3.4)

Given
v
,
w
in
W^{P}
, the corresponding
Richardson variety
is
X_{w}^{v}:=X(w)\cap w_{0}X(w_{0}v)
. Consider
1\leq i,j,k,l\leq n
,
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
. Richardson varieties of
X
are described by :

\displaystyle X_{i,j}^{k,l}

\displaystyle:=X_{w_{i,j}}^{w_{0}w_{k,l}}=X(w_{i,j})\cap w_{0}X(w_{k,l})

\displaystyle=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i}>;\><e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n}>\subset H\},

(3.5)

if
n-k+1\leq i
and
j-1\leq n-l+2
, and
X_{i,j}^{k,l}=\emptyset
else. The second equality comes from considering the description of Schubert varieties given by (
3.1
) and of opposite Schubert varieties given by (
3.4
).

3.3
Degenerating Richardson varieties of
Fl_{1,n-1}
.

We describe here one parameter families of deformations of Richardson varieties. More precisely, we describe irreducible projective varieties
\Phi
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X
such that one fiber of the projection morphism
\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a Richardson variety, while another fiber is a union of Schubert varieties, up to multiplication by elements of
{\rm GL}_{n}
.

To facilitate the proof, let us first introduce an auxilliary variety. Set
e_{n+1}=0=e_{0}
. For
1\leq r\leq h\leq n
,
0\leq p\leq n-r-1
, we will consider the following subvariety of
X
:

Y_{r,p}^{h}:=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots e_{h}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{r},e_{n-p+1},\dots,e_{n}>\subset H\}.

(3.6)

The Plücker embedding
X\hookrightarrow Gr(1,n)\times Gr(n-1,n)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
embeds
Y_{r,p}^{h}
as :

Y_{r,p}^{h}:=\{[x_{1},\dots,x_{h},0,\dots,0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n-p}:0:\dots:0]\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}|\sum_{a}x_{a}y_{a}=0\}.

(3.7)

Lemma 3.3.0.1
.

1.

Let
1\leq i,j,k,l\leq n
, where
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
. Suppose
n-k+1\leq i
and
j-1\leq n-l+2
.

Denote by
r
the number of shared vectors between
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
and
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
. Call
h:=i+k-n
, and
p:=j+l-2-r
.

There exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
such that
g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}=Y_{r,p}^{h}
.

2.

Fix
1\leq r\leq n
,
0\leq p\leq n-r-1
.Then there exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
such that
Y_{r,p}^{r}=g\cdot X(r,r+p+1)
.

3.

Fix
1\leq r\leq h\leq n
. Then
Y_{r,0}^{h}=X(h,r+1)
.

Démonstration.

1.

Recall we have defined
r
as the number of shared vectors between
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
and
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
, and
p=j+l-2-r
. Notice :

\displaystyle p=j+l-2-r

\displaystyle=j-1+n-(n-l+1)-r

\displaystyle=\#(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})-r,

hence
p
is the number of vectors in
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
not belonging to
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
. Now call
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{p})
these
p
vectors, and
(\epsilon_{p+1},\dots,\epsilon_{p+r})
the
r
vectors in
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
belonging to
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
. Finally, call
(\eta_{r+1},\dots,\eta_{h})
the
\#(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})-r=h-r
vectors of
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
that do not belong to
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
, and
(\eta_{h+1},\dots,\eta_{n-p})
the remaining vectors. Denote by
g
the following permutation of the basis vectors :

(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})\rightarrow(\epsilon_{p+1},\dots,\epsilon_{p+r},\eta_{r+1},\dots,\eta_{h},\eta_{h+1},\dots,\eta_{n-p},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{p}).

Then, according to (
3.1
), we have :

\displaystyle g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}

\displaystyle=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots,e_{h}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{r},e_{n-p+1},\dots,e_{n}>\subset H\}

\displaystyle=Y_{r,p}^{h}

where the first equality follows directly from the definition of
g
and the definition (
) of
X_{i,j}^{k,l}
:

X_{i,j}^{k,l}=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i}>;\><e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n}>\subset H\}.

2.

By definition, we have :

Y_{r,p}^{r}:=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots e_{r}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{r},e_{n-p+1},\dots,e_{n}>\subset H\}.

If
r=n-p
, set
g:=id
. Else, denote by
g
the permutation of the basis vectors sending :

(e_{1},\dots,e_{r},e_{r+1},\dots,e_{n-p},e_{n-p+1},\dots,e_{n})\rightarrow(e_{1},\dots,e_{r},e_{n-p+1},\dots,e_{n},e_{r+1},\dots,e_{n-p}).

Then, according to (
3.1
), we have :

\displaystyle g\cdot Y_{r,p}^{r}

\displaystyle=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots,e_{r}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{r},e_{r+1},\dots,e_{r+p}>\subset H\}

\displaystyle=X(r,r+p+1)

3.

We have :

\displaystyle Y_{r,0}^{h}:

\displaystyle=\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots e_{h}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{r}>\subset H\}

\displaystyle=X(h,r+1)

where the first equality is the definition (
) of
Y_{r,0}^{h}
and the third equality is the definition (
3.1
) of Schubert varieties.

∎

Proposition 3.3.0.1
.

Let
1\leq i,j,k,l\leq n
, where
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
.

1.

If
n+1>i+k
or
j+l\geq n+2
, then
X_{i,j}^{k,l}=\emptyset
;

2.

Suppose
i+k\geq n+1
and
j+l\leq n+1
and (
i<j
or
k<l
). Then there exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
such that :

X_{i,j}^{k,l}=g\cdot X(i+k-n,j+l-1);

3.

Suppose
i+k-n\geq j+l
and
j+l\leq n+1
. Then there exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
, and an irreducible projective subvariety
\Phi
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X
such that :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([1:0])=\{[1:0]\}\times g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([0:1])=\{[0:1]\}\times X(i+k-n,j+p-1);

where
\pi:\Phi\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the natural projection ;

4.

Suppose
1\leq i+k-n\leq j+l-1\leq n
and
i>j
and
k>l
. Then there exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
, and an irreducible projective subvariety
\Phi
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X
such that :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([1:0])

\displaystyle=\{[1:0]\}\times g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([0:1])

\displaystyle=\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{i+k-n-1,j+l-1-i-k+n}^{i+k-n-1}\cup\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{i+k-n,j+l-1-i-k+n}^{i+k-n}

where
\pi:\Phi\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the natural projection.

Démonstration.

1.

By definition of
X_{i,j}^{k,l}
.

2.

Notice that then all vectors of
E_{1}=(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
belong to
E_{2}=(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
, hence the number
r
of shared vectors between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is equal to
h=\#E_{1}=i+k-n
. Hence, according to Lemma
3.3.0.1

(i)
, there exists
g
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
such that

g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}=Y_{h,p}^{h},

where
h=i+k-n
and
p=j+l-2-h
.

Furthermore, according to Lemma
3.3.0.1

(ii)
, there exists
g^{\prime}
in
{\rm GL}_{n}
such that

Y_{h,p}^{h}=g^{\prime}\cdot X(i+k-n,j+l-1).

Hence :

X_{i,j}^{k,l}=g^{-1}\cdot Y_{h,p}^{h}=g^{-1}g^{\prime}\cdot X(i+k-n,j+l-1).

3.

We use here the same notations as in Lemma
3.3.0.1
. Denote by
r
the number of shared vectors between
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
and
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
. Call
h:=i+k-n
, and
p:=j+l-2-r
.

Let
\Phi
be the subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined by :

\Phi=\{([u:v];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n}])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\\
\left|\sum_{a=r+1}^{h}x_{a}y_{a}=0\>\mathrm{and}\>\forall\>r<a\leq r+p,\>vy_{a}=uy_{n-p+a-r}\}\right.

As usual, we denote by
D(v)
(respectively
D(u)
) the subvariety of elements
([u:v];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{n}];[y_{1}:\dots:y_{n}])
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
satisfying
v\neq 0
(resp.
u\neq 0
). On
D(v)
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{|D(v)}\simeq\{(t;[x_{1},\dots,x_{h}];[0:\dots:0:ty_{n-p+1}:\dots:ty_{n}:y_{r+p+1}:\dots:y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}

\displaystyle|t\sum_{a=r+1}^{r+p}x_{a}y_{n-p+a-r}+\sum_{a=r+p+1}^{h}x_{a}y_{a}=0\}.

Since
r+p=j+l-2<i+k-n-1=h-1<h
,
\Phi_{|D(v)}
is irreducible. In the same way,
\Phi_{|D(u)}
is also irreducible. Hence
\Phi
is an irreducible projective subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Denote by
\pi:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
the natural projection.
\pi
verifies :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(\{[1:0]\})

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{([1:0];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n-p},0,\dots,0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}|

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\sum_{i=r+1}^{h}x_{i}y_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[1:0]\}\times Y_{r,p}^{h}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[1:0]\}\times g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}

where the existence of the element
g
is ensured by Lemma
3.3.0.1

(ii)
, and the second equality is the definition (
) of
Y_{r,p}^{h}
.

Finally,
\pi
verifies :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([0:1])

\displaystyle=\{([0:1];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+p+1}:\dots:y_{n}])\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}|\sum_{a=r+1}^{h}x_{a}y_{a}=0\}

\displaystyle=\{[0:1]\}\times\{(L,H)\in X|L\subset<e_{1},\dots e_{h}>,\><e_{1},\dots e_{p+r}>\subset H\}

\displaystyle=\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{j+l-2,0}^{i+k-n}

\displaystyle=\{[0:1]\}\times X(i+k-n,j+l-1)

where the second equality is the definition (
) and the last equality holds according to Lemma
3.3.0.1

(iii)
.

4.

Again, we use the same notations as in Lemma
3.3.0.1
. Denote by
r
the number of shared vectors between
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
and
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
. Call
h:=i+k-n
, and
p:=j+l-2-r
.

Let
\Phi
be the subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined by :

\Phi=\bigg\{([u:v];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n-p-r+h-1}:0:\dots:0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\\
\left|\forall\>1\leq a\leq h-1-r,\>vy_{r+a}=uy_{n-p+a},\>\mathrm{and}\>\sum_{a=r+1}^{h}x_{a}y_{a}=0\right\}.

On
D(v)
, we have
\Phi_{|D(u)}\simeq\mathrm{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t]\times\mathbb{P}^{h-1}\times Y
, where
Y=\{[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n}]\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}|\}
.

\displaystyle\Phi_{|D(v)}\simeq\{(t;[x_{1},\dots,x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:ty_{n-p+r+1}:\dots:ty_{n-p+r+h-1}:y_{h}:\dots:y_{n-p+r-h-1}:0\dots:0]

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\left|t\sum_{a=r+1}^{h-1}x_{a}y_{n-p+a}+x_{h}y_{h}=0\right\}.

Now notice that, since
j<n-l+2
, the number
r
of shared vectors between
(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
and
(e_{1},\dots e_{j-1},e_{n-l+2},\dots,e_{n})
satisfies
r<h=\#(e_{n-k+1},\dots,e_{i})
. Hence
\Phi_{|D(u)}
is irreducible. In the same way,
\Phi_{|D(u)}
is also irreducible. Hence
\Phi
is an irreducible projective subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Denote by
\pi:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
the natural projection.
\pi
satisfies :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([1:0])

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{([1:0];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{r+1}:\dots:y_{n-p},0,\dots,0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>|\sum_{a=r+1}^{h}x_{a}y_{a}=0\}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[1:0]\}\times Y_{r,p}^{h}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[1:0]\}\times g\cdot X_{i,j}^{k,l}

where the existence of the element
g
is ensured by Lemma
3.3.0.1

(ii)
, and the second equality is the definition (
) of
Y_{r,p}^{h}
.

Finally, we observe :

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}([0:1])

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{([0:1];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{h}:\dots:y_{n-p-r+h-1},0,\dots,0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}|x_{h}y_{h}=0\}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{([0:1];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h-1}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{h}:\dots:y_{n-p-r+h-1},0,\dots,0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\cup\{([0:1];[x_{1}:\dots:x_{h}:0:\dots:0];[0:\dots:0:y_{h+1}:\dots:y_{n-p-r+h-1},0,\dots,0])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{h-1,p+r-h+1}^{h-1}\cup\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{h,p+r-h+1}^{h}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>=\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{i+k-n-1,j+l-1-i-k+n}^{i+k-n-1}\cup\{[0:1]\}\times Y_{i+k-n,j+l-1-i-k+n}^{i+k-n}

where the third equality is the definition (
) and the last equality is deduced from
h=i+k-n
and
r+p=j+l-2
.

∎

3.4
Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
.

3.4.1
Computing Littlewood-Richardson coefficients.

We deduce here Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
from the results of Part
3.3
. For simplicity, we set
{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j}=0
if
i<1
or
j>n
.

Proposition 3.4.1.1
.

Let
1\leq i,j,k,p\leq n
, where
i\neq j
and
k\neq p
. Then

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{k,p}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{i,j}&=\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n,j+p-1}\>&\mathrm{if}\>i+k-n\geq j+p\>\mathrm{or}\>i<j\>\mathrm{or}\>k<p;\\
\mathcal{O}_{k,p}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{i,j}&=\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n-1,j+p-1}+\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n,j+p}-\mathcal{O}_{i+k-n-1,j+p}\>&\mathrm{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

where, for all
1\leq k,p\leq n
,
k\neq p
, we call
\mathcal{O}_{k,p}:=[\mathcal{O}_{X(w_{k,p})}]
the class in
K_{\circ}(X)
associated with the longest length element
w_{k,p}
in the
n
-th symetric group sending
1
to
k
and
n
to
p
.

Lemma 3.4.1.1
.

Let
1\leq h\leq n
,
0\leq p\leq n-h-1
.

Then we have the following equality in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
:

[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}\cup Y_{h,p}^{h}}]=[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}}]+[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h,p}^{h}}]-[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h+1,p}^{h-1}}]

Démonstration.

Denote by
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
the bihomogeneous ideals defining
Y_{h,p}^{h}
and
Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}
embedded in
\mathrm{Proj}\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\times\mathrm{Proj}\mathbb{C}[y_{1},\dots,y_{n}]
.

According to (
) we have :

\displaystyle I_{1}=<x_{h+1},\dots,x_{n};y_{1},\dots,y_{h},y_{n-p+1},\dots,y_{n}>

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle I_{2}=<x_{h},\dots,x_{n};y_{1},\dots,y_{h-1},y_{n-p+1},\dots,y_{n}>.

Call
S=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
and
T=\mathbb{C}[y_{1},\dots,y_{n}]
the graded rings. The following is an exact sequence of
S\otimes T
bigraded-modules :

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow S\otimes T_{/<I_{1}\cap I_{2}>}\rightarrow S\otimes T_{/I_{1}}\oplus S\otimes T_{/I_{2}}\rightarrow S\otimes T_{/<I_{1},I_{2}>}\rightarrow 0.

This induces the following exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
-modules :

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}\cup Y_{h,p}^{h}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h,p}^{h}}\rightarrow\widetilde{S\otimes T}_{/<I_{1},I_{2}>}\rightarrow 0,

where
\widetilde{S\otimes T}_{/<I_{1},I_{2}>}
is the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
-module associated to the
S\otimes T
bigraded-module
S\otimes T_{/<x_{h},\dots,x_{n};y_{1},\dots,y_{h},y_{n-p+1},\dots,y_{n}>}
. This implies the following equalities in
K(Fl_{1,n-1})
:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{O}_{X(i,j)\cup X(i-1,j-1)}]

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h,p}^{h}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}}]-[\widetilde{S\otimes T}_{/<I_{1},I_{2}}]

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h,p}^{h}}]+[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h-1,p}^{h-1}}]-[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h+1,p}^{h-1}}].

The second equality is deduced from
\widetilde{S\otimes T}_{/<I_{1},I_{2}>}=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{h+1,p}^{h-1}}
, which is a direct consequence of (
).
∎

Finally, Proposition
3.4.1.1
follows directly from Lemmas
0.3.2.2
,
0.3.2.1
,
3.4.1.1
and from Proposition
3.3.0.1
.
Indeed, notice for any
1\leq i,j,k,l\leq n
, such that
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
, we have :

[\mathcal{O}_{X(i,j)}]\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{X(k,l)}]=[\mathcal{O}_{X_{i,j}^{k,l}}],

since the intersection between a Schubert varieties and its opposite variety is transverse, and Schubert varieties have rational singularities-cf. for example
[
Bri05
]
, Theorem
4.2.1

(i)
. Furthermore, according to Lemmas
0.3.2.1
,
3.4.1.1
, translations by
g
and one parameter deformations do not change the sheaf class. Finally, Lemma
3.4.1.1
and Proposition
3.3.0.1
give the result.

3.4.2
Link with cohomological Littlewood-Richardson coefficients.

Let
i
,
k
,
j
and
l
be integers between
1
and
n
such that
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
. Since the morphism
A_{*}(X)\rightarrow GrK_{\circ}(X)
is a morphism of graded abelian groups, we can deduce the intersection product between two classes
[X(i,j)]
and
[X(k,l)]
in
A_{*}(X)
from the product betweeen
[\mathcal{O}_{i,j}]
and
[\mathcal{O}_{k,l}]
.
Proposition
3.4.1.1
describes Littlewood-Richardson coefficients in
A_{*}(X)\simeq H^{*}(X)
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \>[X(k,l)]\cup[X(i,j)]&=0\>&&\mathrm{if}\>i+k\leq n\>\mathrm{or}\>j+l\geq n+2;\\
[X(k,l)]\cup[X(i,j)]&=[X(i+k-n-1,j+l-1)]+&&[X(i+k-n,j+l)]\>\\
&&&\mathrm{if}\>1\leq i+k-n\leq j+l-1\leq n\>\mathrm{and}\>i>j,\>k>l;\>\\
[X(k,l)]\cup[X(i,j)]&=[X(i+k-n,j+l-1)]\>&&\mathrm{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 3.4.2.1
.

Chevalley’s formula for generalized flag varieties already gives us the intersection product between a codimension
1
Schubert class and any other Schubert class. Let us check we do recover the same formula.

According to the generalization of Chevalley’s formula to general
G/P
(cf. for example
[
FW04
]
, lemma 8.1), for all
u\in W/W_{P}
, for all
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\Delta_{P}
, we have :

[Y(s_{\beta})]\cup[Y(u)]=\Sigma h_{\alpha}(\omega_{\beta})[Y(\tilde{u}s_{\alpha})],

where the sum goes over all postive roots
\alpha
such that
\ell([\tilde{u}s_{\alpha}])=\ell(u)+1
, where
h_{\alpha}(\omega_{\beta})=n_{\alpha\beta}(\beta,\beta)/(\alpha,\alpha)
(where
n_{\alpha\beta}
is the coefficient of
\beta
in the expansion of
\alpha
as a positive linear combination of positive roots), and where we write
\tilde{u}
a minimal length representative of
u
in
W
.

We thus obtain here :

[Y(\alpha_{1})]\cup[Y(u)]=\Sigma[Y(\tilde{u}s_{\alpha})],

where the sum goes over the
\alpha\in\{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{i}|2\leq i\leq n\}\subset R^{+}-R_{P}^{+}
verifying
\ell([\tilde{u}s_{\alpha}])=\ell(u)+1
.

Let
\tilde{u}=w_{i,j}
and
s_{l}=s_{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{l}}
. We observe :
\ell(w_{i,j})=i-1+n-j
if
i<j
, and
\ell(w_{i,j})=n+i-j-2
else ; hence
\ell([\tilde{u}s_{l}])=\ell(u)+1
iff(
l=i+1
and
i<j
) or (
l=n
and
j=i+1
) or (
l=i
and
i>j
), from which we deduce :

\displaystyle[Y(\alpha_{1})]\cup[Y(i,j)]

\displaystyle=[Y({i+1,j})]

\displaystyle\>\mathrm{if}\>j\neq i+1,\>i\neq n;

\displaystyle[Y(\alpha_{1})]\cup[Y(n,j)]

\displaystyle=0;

\displaystyle[Y(\alpha_{1})]\cup[Y(i,i+1)]

\displaystyle=[Y(i+2,i+1)]+[Y(i+1,i)],

which does indeed gives us
[X(n-1,1)]\cup[X(k,p)]=[X(k-1,p)]
if
k\neq p+1
,
k\neq 1
,
[X(n-1,1)]\cup[X(1,p)]=0
, and
[X(n-1,1)]\cup[X(k,k-1)]=[X(k-2,k-1)]+[X(k-1,k)]
.

Remark 3.4.2.2
.

Let
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\Delta_{P}
. If we use the notations of Remark
3.4.2.1
, we can write here :

[\mathcal{O}_{Y(s_{\beta})}]\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{Y(u)}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+},\\
\ell([\tilde{u}s_{\alpha}])=\ell(u)+1\end{subarray}}h_{\alpha}(\omega_{\beta})[\mathcal{O}_{Y(\tilde{u}s_{\alpha})}]-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\gamma\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}\\
\ell([\tilde{u}s_{\alpha}])=\ell(u)+1=\ell([\tilde{u}s_{\gamma}])\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+},\>\ell(v)=\ell(u)+2,\\
v\succcurlyeq\alpha\>\mathrm{et}\>v\succcurlyeq\gamma\end{subarray}}h_{\alpha}(\omega_{\beta})h_{\gamma}(\omega_{\beta})[\mathcal{O}_{Y(v)}].

3.5
Three points correlators of
Fl_{1,n-1}
.

3.5.1
Three points correlators of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
.

Let
n>0
. We compute here genus zero correlators of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
. Note that Buch-Mihalcea fully described the product of two Schubert classes in the ring
QK_{s}({\mathbb{P}}^{n})

[
BM11
]
; we could compute three points correlators using this description. We give here a different proof, by studying the geometry of Gromov-Witten varieties of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
. This result plays a key role in our derivation of correlators of
X
presented Part
3.5.3
. For
1\leq i\leq n+1
, we denote by
L_{i}
the Schubert variety associated with the permutation
w_{1,i}
permuting
1
and
i
:

L_{i}:=\{[x_{1}:\dots:x_{i}:0:\dots:0]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\mid x_{1},\dots,x_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}\}.

Let
d
in
E({\mathbb{P}}^{n})\simeq\mathbb{N}
. We consider here the compactification of morphisms
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
given by the space of quasi-maps
[
Bra06
]
. A degree
d
morphism
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is defined by
(n+1)
homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
. We may thus parametrize degree
d
morphisms
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
by
P={\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{C}}^{(d+1)(n+1)})
, where we view a point in
{\mathbb{C}}^{(d+1)(n+1)}
as a set of
(n+1)
degree
d
polynomials. For a point
p
in
P={\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{C}}^{(d+1)(n+1)})
, we will denote by

f_{p}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}

the associated map. Note that if the polynomials associated with a point
p
in
P
have common roots
x_{i}
, then
f_{p}
is a rational map which is not defined on the points
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
The dense subvariety
U
of
P
parametrizing polynomials having no common roots is in bijection with
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},d)
. Fix distinct points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. According to the appendix of Chapter
2
, sending a point
p
in
U
to the point in
\mathcal{M}_{0,3}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
associated with
(f_{p}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n},\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
defines a morphism
\phi:U\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0,3}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{0,3}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
is irreducible
[
KP01
]
and
\phi
is an injective dominant morphism of quasi-projective normal complex varieties,
\phi
is birational. We may hence identify a dense open subset
V
of
\mathcal{M}_{0,3}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
with its reciprocal image by
\phi
. Finally, note that for an element
p
in
V\subset P
, the evaluation morphism
ev_{i}
assigns to the
(n+1)
polynomials associated with
p
the projectivization of their value at
p_{i}
.

Proposition 3.5.1.1
.

Let
1\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq n+1
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

i)

Let
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
,
g_{3}
be elements in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}
, let
z
be an element in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
. The variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})\cap V
is either empty or an irreducible rational variety. The same holds for the variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(z)\cap V
.

ii)

For
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}^{3}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})
is either empty or an irreducible rational variety. In the same way, for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the general fiber of the morphism
ev_{3}:ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}L_{i_{2}})\rightarrow ev_{3}\left(ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}L_{i_{2}})\right)\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
evaluating the third marked point is an irreducible rational variety.

iii)

\langle w_{1,i_{1}},w_{1,i_{2}},w_{1,i_{3}}\rangle_{d}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mathrm{if}\>d=1\>\mathrm{and}\>i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}<n+2\\
1&\mathrm{else}\end{array}\right.

Démonstration.

i)

We denote by
L:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})\cap V
, which we see here as a subvariety of
P={\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{C}}^{(d+1)(n+1)})
. Note that, if we denote by
(P_{1},\dots,P_{n+1})
the
(n+1)
polynomials associated to a point in
P
,
L
is defined by :

L=\left\{(P_{1},\dots,P_{n+1})\in V\subset({\mathbb{C}}^{d+1})^{n+1}\mid\forall 1\leq k\leq 3,\>[P_{1}(p_{k}):\dots:P_{n+1}(p_{k})]\in g_{k}\cdot L_{i_{k}}\right\}.

Since
g_{k}\cdot L_{i_{k}}
is a linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
, the compactification of
L
in
P=({\mathbb{C}}^{d+1})^{n+1}
is defined by linear equations. Hence
L
is the intersection of the dense open subset
V
with a linear subspace of
P
, hence
L
is an irreducible rational variety.

In the same way, if
L:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(z)\cap V
, since
g_{k}\cdot L_{i_{k}}
is a linear subspace of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
and a point in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
can be described as the zero locus of a set of linear equations,
L
is an irreducible rational variety.

ii)

Since
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is convex,
{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
is a dense open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)

[
FP96
]
, hence
V
is a dense open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
. According to Chapter
1
, for
g=(g_{k})
general in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}^{3}
,
ev^{-1}(g\cdot(L_{i_{1}}\times L_{i_{2}}\times L_{i_{3}}))
has a dense intersection with the dense open subset
V
of
{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},d)
, hence is an irreducible rational variety according to
i)
.

For
(g_{1},g_{2})
in
G^{2}
denote by
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})
. For
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
the variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
has a dense intersection with the dense open subset
V
; hence for
z
general in
ev_{3}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}})
, the fiber
ev_{3}^{-1}(z)\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
has a dense intersection with the open subset
V
. Hence for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
and
z
general in
ev_{3}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}})
, the variety
ev_{3}^{-1}(z)\cap{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
is an irreducible rational variety.

iii)

We name
G:={\rm GL}_{n+1}
. Since
L_{i}
is the Schubert variety associated with
w_{1,i+1}
, according to (
) we have for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
:

\displaystyle\langle w_{1,i_{1}+1},w_{1,i_{2}+1},w_{1,i_{3}+1}\rangle_{d}=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})}).

Suppose
i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+1<n
and
d=1
. Then according to Lemma
3.5.1.1
for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})
is empty, hence
\langle w_{1,i_{1}+1},w_{1,i_{2}+1},w_{1,i_{3}+1}\rangle_{1}=0
. Now suppose
d>1
, or
d=1
and
i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+1\geq n
. Then according to Lemma
3.5.1.1
for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}})\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}})
is non empty.
Furthermore, according to
[
CMBP13
]
Theorem 2.5. for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
this variety has rational singularities. Hence its sheaf Euler characteristic is equal to the sheaf Euler characteristic of its desingularization, which is also a projective rational variety ; hence it is equal to
1
.

∎

Let
1\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq n
.

Lemma 3.5.1.1
.

i)

For
d\geq 2
, there exists a genus zero stable map of degree
d
joining the varieties
g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}}
,
g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}
and
g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}}
for any element
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}^{3}
;

ii)

For
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}^{3}
, there exists a line joining the varieties
g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}}
,
g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}
and
g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}}
iff
i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\geq n+2
.

Démonstration.

i)

We first consider the case when
d_{2}=2
. It is enough to show that for any three points
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
P_{3}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
there is a genus zero stable map of degree
2
whose image contains all points
P_{i}
. Consider a line joining the points
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
; now glue this line at
P_{2}
with a line joining the points
P_{2}
and
P_{3}
. We obtain a genus zero degree
2
stable map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
joining all three points
P_{i}
.

Now suppose
d\geq 2
. Consider a genus zero degree
2
stable map
f:C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
joining all three points
P_{i}
. Let
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
be a genus zero degree
d-2
map, whose image intersects
f(C)
at the image
f(p)
of a non special point
p\in C
. Such a map can ba obtained by translating the image of any degree
d-2
map by an element in
{\rm GL}_{n+1}
. Now glue
C
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
together at the point
p
. We obtain a degree
d
stable map
C\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
joining all three points
P_{i}
.

ii)

Name
G:={\rm GL}_{n+1}
. Let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be elements in
G^{2}
. By the functorial definition of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
, there exist vectors
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
such that
g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}}
parametrizes lines in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
included in the linear subspace
<u_{1},\dots,u_{i_{1}}>\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
and
g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}
parametrizes lines in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
included in the linear subspace
<v_{1},\dots,v_{i_{2}}>\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
. For
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the vector space generated by the vectors
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
is of dimension
\mathrm{min}(n,i_{1}+i_{2}+1)
. Hence for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
the set of points lying on a line joining
g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}}
and
g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}
parametrizes lines in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
included in the linear subspace

<u_{1},\dots,u_{i_{1}},v_{1},\dots,v_{i_{2}}>\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{min}(n+1,i_{1}+i_{2})}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}.

This can be rephrased more formally as :

ev_{3}(ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}))=h\cdot L_{\mathrm{min}(n+1,i_{1}+i_{2})},

where
h
lies in
G
and we denote by
ev_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},1)
the evaluation morphism. Hence for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
, there exists a line joining the varieties
g_{1}\cdot L_{i_{1}}
,
g_{2}\cdot L_{i_{2}}
and
g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}}
iff the intersection between
h\cdot L_{\mathrm{min}(n+1,i_{1}+i_{2})}
and
g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}}
is non empty for
g_{3}
general in
G
-where
h
is an element in
G
depending only on the choice of an element
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
. This is equivalent to the following condition in the Chow ring
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n})
:

[g_{3}\cdot L_{i_{3}}]\cup[h\cdot L_{\mathrm{min}(n,i_{1}+i_{2})}]=[L_{i_{3}}]\cup[L_{\mathrm{min}(n+1,i_{1}+i_{2})}]\neq 0.

Finally, the equality
[L_{i}]\cup[L_{j}]=[L_{\mathrm{min}(n,i+j-n)}]
in
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n})
yields the result, where we set
L_{i}=\emptyset
for
i<0
.

∎

3.5.2
Computing correlators of
X
.

We list here properties of
X
that will allow us to compute genus zero correlators of
X
in Part
3.5.3
. Recall
X
can be described as
X\simeq{\rm GL}_{n}/P
, where
P\supset B\supset T
is a parabolic subgroup of
G={\rm GL}_{n}
. Note that
P
is a parabolic subgroup with associated reductive subgroup the block diagonal matrix
{\rm GL}_{1}\times{\rm GL}_{n-2}\times{\rm GL}_{1}
with
{\rm GL}_{n-2}
as the central block. Recall for
1\leq i,j\leq n
, where
i\neq j
, we denote by
w_{i,j}
the representant in
W/W_{P}
of any permutation in
N_{G}(T)/T\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{n}
sending
1
to
i
and
n
to
j
. Let
r\geq 0
, let
1\leq i_{1},\dots,i_{r},j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\leq n
, where
i_{k}\neq j_{k}
. For an element
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
in
G^{r}
, we denote by

\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\cap\dots\cap ev_{r}^{-1}(g_{r}\cdot X(w_{i_{r},j_{r}}))

the Gromov-Witten variety parametrizing rational curves of degree
{\mathbf{d}}
with marked points in the Schubert varieties
g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
.

(a)

Computing correlators by considering the image of Gromov-Witten varieties.

Proposition 3.5.2.1
.

i)

Let
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
be an element in
G^{r}
such that

A)

The general fiber of the evaluation morphism
ev_{r+1}:\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}:=\cap_{k=1}^{r}ev_{k}^{-1}(g_{k}\cdot X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}}))\rightarrow{\rm Im}(ev_{r+1})\subset X
is a rationally connected variety ;

B)

Tor_{i}^{X^{r}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})},{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})})=0
for all
i>0
;

C)

The Gromov-Witten variety
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{k}}
has rational singularities ;

D)

The projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{r+1}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{k}})
has rational singularities ;

Then for any
\alpha
in
K^{\circ}(X)

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{r+1}(\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}})}]\right).

ii)

If for
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
:

A)

The general fiber of the evaluation morphism
ev_{r+1}:\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}:=\cap_{k=1}^{r}ev_{k}^{-1}(g_{k}\cdot X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}}))\rightarrow{\rm Im}(ev_{r+1})\subset X
is a rationally connected variety ;

B)

The projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{r+1}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{k}})
has rational singularities

then for any
\alpha
in
K^{\circ}(X)
and for
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
:

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{r+1}(\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}})}]\right)

iii)

If

A)

For
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the general fiber of the evaluation morphism
ev_{3}:\mathcal{W}_{g_{1},g_{2}}=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}X(i_{1},j_{1}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}w_{0}X(i_{2},j_{2}))\rightarrow{\rm Im}(ev_{3})\subset X
is a rationally connected variety ;

B)

The projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{1,w_{0}})
has rational singularities,

then

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{1,w_{0}})}]\right)

Démonstration.

i)

Since
Tor_{i}^{X^{r}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})},{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})})=0
for all
i>0
, we have the following identity in
K_{\circ}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}))
.

\displaystyle ev^{*}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{r}{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{k},j_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=ev^{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 0}[Tor_{i}^{X^{r}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}))},{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})})]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\times_{X^{r}}\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})}]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}}].

(3.8)

By definition for
\alpha
in
K^{\circ}(X)
the correlator associated with
i_{k}
,
j_{k}
and
\alpha
is given by

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(ev_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\dots ev_{r}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot ev_{r+1}^{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}))}]\right)

Using (
) here above and the projection formula in
K_{\circ}(X)
, we obtain :

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left(ev_{r+1}^{*}\alpha\cdot ev_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\dots ev_{r}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}))}]\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(ev_{r+1}^{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}}]\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot(ev_{r+1})_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}}]\right).

Moreover, since the projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{r+1}(\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}})
has rational singularities,
according to
[
BM11
]
Theorem
3.1
we have in
K_{\circ}(X)
:

\displaystyle(ev_{r+1})_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{r+1}(\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}})}],

(3.9)

which yields the expected equality.

ii)

First, according to
[
CMBP13
]
Theorem 2.5 for
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
the Gromov-Witten variety
\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}
has rational singularities. Furthermore, according to Sierra’s homological Kleiman-Bertini theorem
[
Sie09
]
, for
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
, we have
Tor_{i}^{X^{r}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r+1}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})},{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{k=1}^{r}g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})})=0
for all
i>0
. Hypothesis
A)
and
B)
finally imply the expected equality according to
i)
.

iii)

Since for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the general fiber of the evaluation morphism
ev_{3}:\mathcal{W}_{g_{k}}\rightarrow{\rm Im}(ev_{3})\subset X
is a rationally connected variety there exists a dense open subset
U
of
G^{2}
such that elements in
U
satisfy conditions
A)
and
B)
and
C)
of part
i)
. Since the evaluation morphism
ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X^{3}
is
G
-equivariant,
U
is invariant by the diagonal action of
G
. Hence according to
[
FW04
]
Lemma
7.1
, there exists
(h_{1},h_{2})
in
U
such that
h_{1}X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})=X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})
and
