h_{2}X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})=w_{0}X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})
. Since the projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{h_{1},h_{2}})
has rational singularities, we obtain according to
i)
:

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{1,w_{0}})}]\right).

(3.10)

∎

(b)

Computing correlators of
Fl_{1,n-1}
when the natural map
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}X^{3}
is surjective.
Recall
X
parametrizes lines included in hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Denote by
\pi:X\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
the projection induced by forgetting the hyperplane. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2}):=d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}
be an effective class of curve in
E(X)
. Note that
\pi_{*}(d_{1},d_{2})=d_{1}\in E({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
. The forgetful morphism
\pi
induces a morphism
\Pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})
. Together with the evaluation morphisms
ev_{i}
, this induces a morphism :

\Pi\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}X^{3}.

Proposition 3.5.2.2
.

If

—

The morphism
\Pi\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},\pi_{*}{\mathbf{d}})\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}X^{3}
is surjective,

—

The projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{3}({\mathcal{W}}_{1,w_{0}})=ev_{3}\left(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(i_{1},j_{1}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(w_{0}X(i_{2},j_{2}))\right)
has rational singularities,

Then

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}^{\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{W}_{1,w_{0}})}]\right).

Démonstration.

Denote by
P_{m}\supset B
the parabolic subgroup associated with
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, i.e.
P_{m}
is a parabolic subgroup satisfying

B\subset P\subset P_{m}

with associated reductive subgroup the block diagonal matrix
{\rm GL}_{1}\times{\rm GL}_{n-1}
whose first block is
{\rm GL}_{1}
. Note that the image of
w_{i,j}
by the forgetful morphism
W/W_{P}\rightarrow W/W_{P_{m}}
is the element
w_{1,i}
in
W/W_{P_{m}}
associated with any permutation sending
1
to
i
. Recall the Schubert variety of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
associated with the element
w_{1,i}
in
W/W_{P_{m}}
is
L_{i}:=\{[x_{1}:\dots:x_{i}:0:\dots:0]\}.

For an element
(g_{1},g_{2})
in
G^{3}
, denote by

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\supset{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{k}}:=\prod_{k=1,2}ev_{k}^{-1}(g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}}))

\displaystyle\>\>\>\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{k}}^{\prime}:=\prod_{k=1,2}ev_{k}^{-1}(g_{k}L_{i_{k}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})

the Gromov-Witten variety of
X
(respectively
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
) associated with the translated Schubert varieties
g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})
(respectively
g_{k}L_{i_{k}}
).

Since the morphism
\Pi\times ev
is surjective and
X
is a two step flag variety, according to Chapter
2
(Proposition
1.4.0.1
) the general fiber of
\Pi\times ev
is rational. Hence according to Chapter
1
(Proposition
1.2.4.1
) the general fiber of the restriction
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\prime}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}g_{1}X(i_{1},j_{1})\times g_{2}X(i_{2},j_{2})\times X
of
\Pi\times ev
is rationally connected. Furthermore, according to Proposition
3.5.1.1
, the general fiber of the morphism

ev_{3}^{d_{1}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\prime}\rightarrow ev_{3}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\prime})\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}

evaluating the third marked point is an irreducible rational variety. Note that since
X\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
is a fibration in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-2}
’s, it is a flat morphism. Recall from Chapter
1
that the flat base change and composition of a rationally connected fibration is a rationally connected fibration (Theorem
1.2.2.1
(3) and Lemma
1.5.1.1
) ; hence the general fiber of the morphism
{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\prime}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}g_{1}X(i_{1},j_{1})\times g_{2}X(i_{2},j_{2})\times X\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}g_{1}X(i_{1},j_{1})\times g_{2}X(i_{2},j_{2})\times X=X
is also rationally connected. We obtain the following commutative diagram of rationally connected fibrations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\prime}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}g_{1}X(i_{1},j_{1})\times g_{2}X(i_{2},j_{2})\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi\times ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{3}^{d_{1}}}

By composition the general fiber of the morphism
ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}:{\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\rightarrow ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathcal{W}}_{g_{1},g_{2}})
is rationally connected. Since the projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathcal{W}}_{1,w_{0}})
has rational singularities, then according to Proposition
3.5.2.1
for any
\alpha
in
K_{\circ}(X)
we have
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}},\alpha\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{1,w_{0}})}]\right)
.
∎

(c)

Symmetry of correlators of
Fl_{1,n-1}
.
The isomorphism
Gr(1,n)\simeq Gr(n-1,n)
yields an automorphism
\varphi
of
X
, which implies the following symmetry for correlators of
X
.

Proposition 3.5.2.3
.

\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,w_{i_{r},j_{r}}\rangle_{d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}=\langle w_{n-j_{1}+1,n-i_{1}+1},\dots,w_{n-j_{r}+1,n-i_{r}+1}\rangle_{d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2}}.

Démonstration.

We consider here
X
as the flag variety parametrizing lines included in hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. We identify explicitly a line in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with a hyperplane in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
in the following way. Consider a Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. For vectors
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, we denote by
<u_{1},\dots,u_{r}>
the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear vector space generated by the
u_{i}
. For any linear space
E=<u_{1},\dots,u_{r}>
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
denote by
E^{\bot}
its orthogonal with respect to
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
;
E^{\bot}
may be defined as the intersection of the kernels of the
r
linear maps sending a vector
v
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
to the complex number
\langle\overline{u_{i}}\mid v\rangle
, where we denote by
\overline{u_{i}}
the conjugate of
u_{i}
. Then the morphism
[L]\rightarrow[L^{\bot}]
yields an identification
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\simeq Gr(n-1,n)
. Furthermore, for any hyperplane
H
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
consider the one dimensional linear space
H^{\bot}
orthogonal to it. Since
(L^{\bot})^{\bot}=L
, we obtain the inverse map
Gr(n-1,n)\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
.
Now consider the natural embedding
X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. The automorphism
\varphi
may be seen as the restriction to
X
of the map
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times Gr(n-1,n)\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times Gr(n-1,n)
sending the projectivization of a line and a hyperplane
([L],[H])
onto the projectivization of the orthogonal spaces
([H^{\bot}],[L^{\bot}])
. Note that
\varphi
exchanges classes
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
, and sends a Schubert variety
X(w_{i,j})
onto a translate of the Schubert variety
X(w_{n-j+1,n-i+1})
. Indeed, according to (
3.1
) the image by
\varphi
of any Schubert variety
X(w_{i,j})
may be described in the following way, where we consider a basis
(e_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
which is orthogonal with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
.

\displaystyle\varphi(X(w_{i,j}))

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(\left\{(L,H)\in X\bigr\rvert L\subset<e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}>,\><e_{1},\cdots,e_{j-1}>\subset H\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{(H^{\bot},L^{\bot})\in X\bigr\rvert L^{\bot}\supset(<e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}>)^{\bot},\>(<e_{1},\cdots,e_{j-1}>)^{\bot}\supset H^{\bot}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(L,H)\in X\bigr\rvert H\supset<e_{i+1},\cdots,e_{n}>,\><e_{j},\cdots,e_{n}>)^{\bot}\supset L\right\}

\displaystyle=w_{0}\cdot\left\{(L,H)\in X\bigr\rvert H\supset<e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-i}>,\><e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-j+1}>)^{\bot}\supset L\right\}

\displaystyle=w_{0}\cdot X(w_{n-j+1,n-i+1}),

where
w_{0}
is the element in
G={\rm GL}_{n}
associated with a maximal length permutation of the basis vectors
e_{i}
. Furthermore, we have
\varphi_{*}(l_{1})=[\varphi(X(w_{2,n}))]=[w_{0}\cdot X(w_{1,n-1})]=l_{2}
, and in the same way
\varphi_{*}(l_{2})=l_{1}
. The isomorphism
\varphi:X\rightarrow X
induces an isomorphism
\phi:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,\varphi_{*}(d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}))=\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2})
. We obtain the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{r}}

Since
\varphi
and
\phi
are isomorphisms, for
\alpha
in
K^{\circ}(X)\simeq K_{\circ}(X)
the projection formula implies

\displaystyle\phi_{*}\left((\epsilon v_{i})^{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\phi_{*}\left((\epsilon v)_{i}^{*}\varphi^{*}\varphi_{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\phi_{*}\left(\phi^{*}ev_{i}^{*}\varphi_{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=ev_{i}^{*}\varphi_{*}\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2})}].

This yields the following equalities, where we denote by
\chi:K_{\circ}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}))\rightarrow K_{\circ}({\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}))
the pushforward to the point.

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,w_{i_{r},j_{r}}\rangle_{d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left((\epsilon v_{1})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{1},j_{1}}}\cdot\dots\cdot(\epsilon v_{r})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{r},j_{r}}}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\phi_{*}\left(\phi^{*}ev_{1}^{*}\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{1},j_{1}}}\cdot\dots\cdot\phi^{*}ev_{r}^{*}\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{r},j_{r}}}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})}]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(ev_{1}^{*}(\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{1},j_{1}}})\cdot\dots\cdot ev_{r}^{*}(\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i_{r},j_{r}}})\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(ev_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{w_{n-i_{1}+1,n-j_{1}+1}})\cdot\dots\cdot ev_{r}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{w_{n-i_{r}+1,n-j_{r}+1}})\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2})}]\right),

where the last equality holds since
\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{i,j}}=\varphi_{*}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{i,j})}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\varphi(X(w_{i,j}))}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{w_{0}\cdot X(w_{n-j+1,n-i+1})}]={\mathcal{O}}_{n-j+1,n-i+1}.

To sum up, correlators of
X
exhibit the following symmetry.

\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,w_{i_{r},j_{r}}\rangle_{d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}=\langle w_{n-j_{1}+1,n-i_{1}+1},\dots,w_{n-j_{r}+1,n-i_{r}+1}\rangle_{d_{2}l_{1}+d_{1}l_{2}}.

∎

Remark 3.5.2.1
.

Let
G
be a semi-simple linear algebraic group, let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
and
H
be a subgroup of
G
acting on
G/P
by left multiplication. Then point
(a)
holds for
H
-equivariant correlators of
G/P
. If
G={\rm GL}_{n}
, then point
(c)
implies that
H
-equivariant correlators of the flag variety
{\rm GL}_{n}/P
are equal to the corresponding correlators of the dual flag variety. The symmetry observed here comes from the fact that
Fl_{1,n-1}
is self dual.

3.5.3
Three points correlators of
X
.

We use here the results of the preceding parts to compute all genus zero three points correlators of
X={\rm GL}_{n}/P
. Let
1\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\leq n
, where
i_{k}\neq j_{k}
. Recall for
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
i\neq j
, we denote by
w_{i,j}
the element in
W^{P}
representing any permutation sending
1
to
i
and
n
to
j
. For a point
x
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, we denote by

(L_{x},H_{x})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{n}

vectors satisfying
x=([L_{x}],[H_{x}])
. We consider here
X
as a subvariety cut out from
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
by the incidence relation "line included in hyperplane" in the following way. Consider a Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Consider a basis
(e_{i})
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
orthogonal with respect to
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
. Denote by
\overline{u}
the conjugate of a complex number. The isomorphism
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\simeq Gr(n-1,n)
identifies
H_{x}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with the kernel
H_{x}^{\bot}
of the linear map
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\ni v\rightarrow\langle\overline{H_{x}}\mid v\rangle\in{\mathbb{C}}
, which is a hyperplane of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. For any vectors
L_{x}
,
H_{x}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
the associated point
([L_{x}],[H_{x}])
lies in
X
iff
L_{x}
lies within the hyperplane
H_{x}^{\bot}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, i.e. iff
\langle L_{x}\mid\overline{H_{x}}\rangle=0
. For an element
g
in
G={\rm GL}_{n}
, we denote by
g^{\bot}
the adjoint of
\overline{g}
for the inner product
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
, i.e.
\langle gL\mid\overline{g^{\bot}L^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle L\mid\overline{L^{\prime}}\rangle
for any
L
,
L^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. We consider here the following action of
G
.

\displaystyle G\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\left(g,([L_{x}],[H_{x}])\right)\rightarrow

\displaystyle([g\cdot L_{x}],[g^{\bot}\cdot H_{x}])

The action of
G
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
we just described induces by restriction the natural action of
G
by left multilication on
X\simeq G/P
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, we set

L_{i}:=\{[x_{1}:\dots:x_{i}:0\dots,0]\}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}.

Recall we denote by
w_{0}
the permutation of
1

\dots

n
of largest length. Note that we have

\displaystyle w_{0}L_{i}

\displaystyle=\{[0:\dots;0:x_{n-i+1}:\dots:x_{n}]\}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}

\displaystyle L_{i}^{\bot}

\displaystyle=\{[0:\dots;0:x_{i+1}:\dots:x_{n}]\}=w_{0}L_{n-i}.

Recall for
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
i\lx@nobreakspace\neq j
,
w_{i,j}
denotes the element in
W^{P}
representing any permutation sending
1
to
i
and
n
to
j
. Using (
3.2
) Schubert varieties of
X
can be described in the following way.

\displaystyle X(w_{i,j})

\displaystyle=\left\{x=([L_{x}],[H_{x}])\in X\mid L_{x}\subset<e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}>,\>H_{x}\subset<e_{j},\cdots,e_{n}>\right\}

\displaystyle=(L_{i}\times w_{0}L_{n-j+1})\cap X.

(3.11)

Let
{\mathbf{d}}
be a degree in
E(X)
. For
(g_{1},g_{2})
in
G^{2}
, we denote by

X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\mathbf{d}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})

the Gromov-Witten variety parametrizing genus zero degree
{\mathbf{d}}
stable maps sending their marked points into the translated Schubert varieties
g_{k}\lx@nobreakspace\cdot X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}})
.

(a)

When
{\mathbf{d}}=l_{2}
.
Denote by
\pi_{1}:X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
(respectively
\pi_{2}
) the first (respectively second) projection. Note that since
l_{2}
is a generating element in the Chow ring
A_{*}(X)
, curves of class
l_{2}
are irreducible. Furthermore, since
{\pi_{1}}_{*}l_{2}=0\in A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
, a curve
C
of class
l_{2}
in
X
projects to the point, and since
{\pi_{2}}_{*}l_{2}=l_{2}\in A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
, a curve
C
of class
l_{2}
projects to a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Hence curves of class
l_{2}
in
X
are curves of the form
\{x\}\times L
, where
x
is a point in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
and
L
is a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Since lines are rational, any such curves are rational. In particular we observe that there is either a unique rational curve of class
l_{2}
joining two distinct points in
X
, or none.

We denote by
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}
the restriction of the morphism evaluating the third marked point to the Gromov-Witten variety
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
. Denote by
U\subset X^{3}
the dense open subset parametrizing distinct triplets of points in
X^{2}
.

Lemma 3.5.3.1
.

i)

For
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the variety
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
has a dense intersection with
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
.

ii)

For
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the general fiber of
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}
is a rational variety.

iii)

Suppose
j_{1}+j_{2}\leq n+2
. Then there exists an element
h
in
G
such that
ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}}^{l_{2}})=h\cdot X(w_{i_{1}+i_{2}-n,1})
if
i_{1}+i_{2}>n+1
, and
ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}}^{l_{2}})=h\cdot X(w_{1,2})
if
i_{1}+i_{2}=n+1
, and is empty else.

Démonstration.

We set
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}:=X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
.

i)

Note that for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the intersection of
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
with
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
is a dense open subset of
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
. Indeed, since
X
is convex, the subset
{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{2})\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{2})
parametrizing degree
l_{2}
stable maps
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
is a dense open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{2})

[
FP96
]
, whose image is contained in
U
. Hence
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
is a dense open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{2})
. Then for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
the variety
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}
has a dense intersection with
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
.

ii)

Let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be an element in
G^{2}
, let
z=(z_{1},z_{2})
be a point in
ev_{3}(X_{g_{1},g_{2}})
. According to (
), we have :

\displaystyle p_{2}\left(g_{1}X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})\times g_{2}X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})\right)

\displaystyle=g_{1}^{\bot}\cdot p_{2}(X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\times g_{2}^{\bot}\cdot p_{2}(X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}

\displaystyle=(g_{1}^{\bot}\cdot w_{0}L_{n-j_{1}+1})\times(g_{2}^{\bot}\cdot w_{0}L_{n-j_{2}+1})

\displaystyle=L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}},

where we denote by
L_{g_{1}}:=g_{1}^{\bot}\cdot w_{0}L_{n-j_{1}+1}
and
L_{g_{2}}:=g_{2}^{\bot}\cdot w_{0}L_{n-j_{2}+1}
. The point
z_{1}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
defines by duality a codimension one linear subspace
H_{1}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\simeq Gr(n-1,n)
. We consider the set of points in
(L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}})\cap H_{1}
distinct from
z
that lie in a line containing
z_{2}=[z^{1}:\dots:z^{n}]
; this can be seen as the intersection of the image of the following map with
(L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}})\cap H_{1}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{1}\times L_{g_{1}}\setminus\{z_{2}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle(t,x=[x^{1}:\dots:x^{n}])

\displaystyle\rightarrow(x,[x^{1}+tz^{1}:\dots:x^{n}+tz^{n}]).

Note that since
L_{g_{2}}
and
H_{1}
are described by linear equations,
{\rm Im}f\cap(L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}})
is described by linear equations on the
x^{i}
and
t
, and hence is a rational variety. We now consider the morphism
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\rightarrow X^{2}\subset({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{2}
evaluating the first two marked points. Note that this morphism factors through
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\rightarrow(\{z_{1}\}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{2}\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{2}
. We denote by
f_{U}
the restriction of this morphism to the subset
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\cap(ev_{1}\times ev_{2})^{-1}(U)
. By definition,
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\cap(ev_{1}\times ev_{2})^{-1}(U)
parametrizes degree
l_{2}
stable maps
(h:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow\{z_{1}\}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
satisfying the following conditions :

—

h
sends its first two marked points into the projection
\pi_{1}(g_{k}X(w_{i_{k},j_{k}}))
of the Schubert varieties considered here, i.e.
h(p_{1})\in L_{g_{1}}
,
h(p_{2})\in L_{g_{2}}
;

—

h
sends its third marked point into
z
, i.e.
h(p_{3})=z_{2}
;

—

h(p_{1})\neq z_{2}
and
h(p_{2})\neq z_{2}
;

—

The image of
h
is contained in
X
, i.e.
h(p_{1})\in H_{1}
and
h(p_{2})\in H_{1}
.

Since a degree
l_{2}
map
({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
is uniquely determined by the data of three distinct points in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
joined by a line, points in the fiber
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\cap(ev_{1}\times ev_{2})^{-1}(U)
are in one-to-one correspondence with distinct points in
{\rm Im}f\cap(L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}})\cap H_{1}
. Hence
f_{U}
is an injective morphism of quasi-projective complex varieties, dominating the rational variety
{\rm Im}f\cap(L_{g_{1}}\times L_{g_{2}})\cap H_{1}
. Hence
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)\cap(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
is a rational variety.

Finally, since according to
i)
for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
,
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}
has a dense intersection with
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
, for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the general fiber
{ev_{3}}_{|g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}(z)
has a dense intersection with
(ev_{1}\times ev_{3})^{-1}(U)
, and hence is also a rational variety.

iii)

ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}})
parametrizes elements
(L,H)
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
such that :

–

There exists a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
joining
H
with elements
L_{1}\subset<e_{j_{1}},\dots,e_{n}>
and
L_{2}\subset<e_{1},\dots,e_{n-j_{2}+1}>
;

–

L\subset<e_{1},\dots,e_{i_{1}}>\cap<e_{n-i_{2}+1},\dots,e_{n}>
;

–

L\subset H^{\bot}
.

First note that the subset of elements
H
satisfying the first condition is the vector subspace generated by
<e_{1},\dots,e_{n-j_{2}+1},e_{j_{1}},\dots,e_{n}>\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{\min(n,2n-j_{1}-j_{2}+2)}
.
Note that the second condition is impossible iff
i_{1}<n-i_{2}+1
, and else describes elements
L
in
<e_{n-i_{2}+1},\dots,e_{i_{1}}>\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{i_{1}+i_{2}-n+1}.
Hence
ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}})
is the set of elements
(L,H)
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
such that

–

H\subset<e_{1},\dots,e_{n-j_{2}+1},e_{j_{1}},\dots,e_{n}>
;

–

L\subset<e_{n-i_{2}+1},\dots,e_{i_{1}}>
;

–

L\subset H^{\bot}
.

Note that for
j_{1}+j_{2}\leq n+2
, the linear subspace
<e_{1},\dots,e_{n-j_{2}+1},e_{j_{1}},\dots,e_{n}>
is
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, and hence the first condition is allways satsified. Then

ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}})=\{([0:\dots:0:x_{n-i_{2}+1}\dots x_{i_{1}}:0\dots 0],[y_{1}\dots y_{n}])\in X\}.

Translation by a permutation
h
of the basis vectors yields

\displaystyle ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}})=h\cdot\{([x_{1}\dots x_{i_{1}+i_{2}-n}:0:\dots:0],[y_{1}\dots y_{n}])\in X\},

which is equal to
h\cdot X(w_{i_{1}+i_{2}-n,1})
if
i_{1}+i_{2}-n\neq 1
, and is equal to
h\cdot X(w_{1,2})
if
i_{1}+i_{2}=n+1
.

∎

Suppose
j_{1}+j_{2}\leq n+2
and
i_{1}+i_{2}\geq n+1
. According to Lemma
3.5.3.1

ii)
the general fiber of the map
ev_{3}:{X}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{l_{2}}\rightarrow X
induced by evaluating the third point is a rational variety. Furthermore, according to Lemma

iii)
its image
ev_{3}(\mathcal{X}_{1,w_{0}})
is a translate of a Schubert variety by an element
h
in
G
, and hence has rational singularities according to Theorem
0.4.0.3
. According to Part
3.5.2

(b)
, we have for
i_{1}+i_{2}>n+1
:

\displaystyle\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{l_{2}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{h\cdot X(w_{i_{1}+i_{2}-n,1})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{i_{1}+i_{2}-n,1})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right).

(3.12)

and if
i_{1}+i_{2}=n+1

\displaystyle\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{l_{2}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{1,2})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{i_{3},1}\delta_{j_{3},2}

(3.13)

Furthermore, according to Lemma
3.5.3.1

iii)
if
j_{1}+j_{2}\leq n+2
and
i_{1}+i_{2}<n+1
, then

\displaystyle\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{l_{2}}=0

(3.14)

(b)

When
{\mathbf{d}}=l_{1}+l_{2}
.
An irreducible rational curve of degree
(d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})
on
X
may be parametrized as

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ni[u:v]\rightarrow\left([P_{1}(u:v)],[P_{2}(u:v)]\right)\in X,

where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
respectively. Furthermore, for an element
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
, we denote by

\mathcal{M}_{g_{k},w_{i_{k},j_{k}}}^{\mathbf{d}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))\cap ev_{3}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot X(w_{i_{3},j_{3}}))

the Gromov-Witten variety associated with the
i_{k}
,
j_{k}
, where
ev_{k}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X
denotes evaluation at the
k
-th marked point.
For a point
(x,y)
general in
X^{2}
we denote by
C_{x,y}
the line joining the points
[L_{x}]
and
[L_{y}]
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, and by
C_{x,y}^{\prime}
the line joining the points
[H_{x}]
and
[H_{y}]
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Recall the varieties
h_{i}
are the classes of the Schubert varieties of codimension
1
of
X
. Note that the class
[h_{1}]
(respectively
[h_{2}]
) in the Chow ring
A_{*}X
is the pullbacks of the Cartier divisor
c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}(-1))
by the first (respectively second) projection
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
.

Lemma 3.5.3.2
.

i)

For a point
(x,y)
in
X^{2}
one of the two following properties is verified :

—

There exists a unique connected rational curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining the points
x
and
y
. This curve is the intersection of the variety
C_{x,y}\times C_{x,y}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
with
X
.

—

There are infinitely many rational curves of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining the points
x
and
y
. These curves cover the intersection of the variety
C_{x,y}\times C_{x,y}^{\prime}
with
X
.

ii)

For a point
(x,y)
general in
X^{2}
, the rational curve in
X
of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining the points
x
and
y
is unique and irreducible.

iii)

For
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
\mathcal{M}_{g_{k},w_{i_{k},j_{k}}}^{l_{1}+l_{2}}
is birational to its image in
X^{3}
by the evaluation morphism.

iv)

Let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be a general element in
G^{2}
. Then

ev_{3}\left(ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot h_{i})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))\right)=X.

Démonstration.

i)

We consider
X
as a divisor of class
[h_{1}]+[h_{2}]
in
A^{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
. Let
p_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
and
p_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})
be points on
X
. If
x_{1}=x_{2}
, we can construct infinitely many genus zero stable map of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
whose image contains the points
p_{i}
by glueing together the degree
l_{2}
map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
joining
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
with any map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
of degree
l_{1}
intersecting this curve at another point than the
p_{i}
. The same holds if
y_{1}=y_{2}
. Now suppose
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\neq y_{2}
. Denote by
L_{1}
a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
joining the points
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, and by
L_{2}
a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
joining the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. Note that any rational curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
in
X
joining the points
p_{i}
projects to
L_{k}
, hence any such rational curve is contained in the intersection
\left(L_{1}\times L_{2}\right)\cap X
. Since the projective variety
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
is smooth, the intersection of
X
with
L_{1}\times L_{2}
has dimension at least
1
. If the variety
(L_{1}\times L_{2})\cap X
has dimension
2
, then any parametrization
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow L_{1}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow L_{2}
sending the points
[1:0]
and
[0:1]
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
onto the points
p_{i}
yield a degree
(l_{1}+l_{2})
map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
joining the points
p_{i}
. We thus obtain infinitely many irreducible rational curves of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining the points
p_{i}
. Indeed, since the classes
l_{i}
generate
A_{1}(X)
, the map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
cannot be a multiple covering onto its image, which is then a curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
. Now suppose the variety
(L_{1}\times L_{2})\cap X
has dimension
1
. Note that we have the following equality in the Chow ring
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
:

\displaystyle[C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[X]\cup[L_{1}\times L_{2}]

\displaystyle=([h_{1}]+[h_{2}])\cup[L_{1}]\boxtimes[L_{2}]

\displaystyle=([h_{1}]+[h_{2}])\cup(l_{1}+l_{2})

\displaystyle=l_{1}+l_{2}

where we also denote by
l_{i}
the pushforward to
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
of the classes
l_{i}\in H_{*}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq A_{*}(X)
. Hence
C^{\prime}
is a curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
in
X
. We now only have to consider the case where
C^{\prime}
has several irreducible components. Since
C^{\prime}
is a curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
contained in
L_{1}\times L_{2}
,
C^{\prime}
can be described as
C^{\prime}=(L_{1}\times\{y\})\cup(\{x\}\times L_{2})
where
x
and
y
are points in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Note that since
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\neq y_{2}
, both points
p_{i}
cannot belong to the same irreducible component. For example suppose
p_{1}
lies on
C_{1}
and
p_{2}
lies on
C_{2}
. Then
C^{\prime}=(L_{1}\times\{y_{1}\})\cup(\{x_{2}\}\times L_{2})
. Since
L_{1}
joins
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, we obtain that
(x_{2},y_{1})
belongs to
L_{1}\times\{y_{1}\}
. In the same way,
(x_{2},y_{1})
belongs to
\{x_{2}\}\times L_{2}
; hence the two irreducible components of
C^{\prime}
come together at
(x_{2},y_{1})
. We obtain a genus zero degree
(l_{1}+l_{2})
stable map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\simeq(L_{1}\times\{y_{1}\})\cup(\{x_{2}\}\times L_{2})\rightarrow X
whose image contains the points
p_{i}
.

ii)

According to here above, the morphism of irreducible projective varieties

ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,l_{1}+l_{2})\rightarrow X^{2}

is dominant, and hence is surjective. Hence for
(x,y)
general in
X^{2}
, the general fiber
ev^{-1}(x,y)
has dimension :

\displaystyle{\rm dim}ev^{-1}(x,y)

\displaystyle={\rm dim}\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,l_{1}+l_{2})-2{\rm dim}X

\displaystyle={\rm dim}X+\int_{l_{1}+l_{2}}c_{1}(T_{X})-1-2{\rm dim}X

\displaystyle=\int_{l_{1}+l_{2}}((n-1)[h_{1}]+(n-1)[h_{2}])-{\rm dim}X-1

\displaystyle=0.

The general fiber
ev^{-1}(x,y)
thus contains finitely many points ; hence according to
i)
it contains only one point. The connected rational curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining two points of
X
in general position thus is unique. Finally, according to here above such a curve is irreducible.

iii)

According to
ii)
, there exists a dense open subset
U\subset X^{2}
such that there exists a unique irreducible rational curve of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining any two points in
U
. Since the evaluation map
ev_{1}\times ev_{2}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{1}+l_{2})\rightarrow X^{2}
is surjective, according to Chapter
1
Lemma
1.2.3.1
for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
the variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))
has a dense intersection with the dense open subset
(ev_{1}\times ev_{2})^{-1}(U)
. Now denote by
V
the dense open subset of
U\times X
containing distinct points
x
,
y
and
z
in
U\times X
. Note that the restriction of the evaluation morphism

ev_{g_{1},g_{2}}:\left(ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}}))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))\right)\cap ev^{-1}(V)\rightarrow X^{3}

is an injective map. Indeed, if we specify two points
x
and
y
in
U
, by definition there is a unique irreducible curve
C
of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining
x
and
y
. Consider a third point
z
lying on
C
, distinct from
x
and
y
. There exists a unique parametrization
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow C
sending the points
[1:0]
,
[0:1]
and
[1:1]
onto the points
x
,
y
and
z
; hence the data of three distinct points
x
,
y
and
z
in
U\times X
defines a unique point in
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,l_{1}+l_{2})
. The restriction of the evaluation morphism to
\mathcal{M}_{g_{k},w_{i_{k},j_{k}}}^{l_{1}+l_{2}}\cap ev^{-1}(V)={ev_{g_{1},g_{2}}}^{-1}(g_{3}\cdot X(w_{i_{3},j_{3}}))
thus is an injective morphism of quasi-projective complex varieties, hence is birational onto its image. Finally, note that for
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
general in
G^{3}
according to Chapter
1
Lemma
1.2.3.1
the variety
\mathcal{M}_{g_{k},w_{i_{k},j_{k}}}^{l_{1}+l_{2}}
has a dense open intersection with
ev^{-1}(V)
, and hence is birational onto its image by the evaluation morphism.

iv)

Consider points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
respectively in
g_{2}\cdot X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})
and
X
. According to
i)
there exists a connected rational curve
C
in
X
of class
(l_{1}+l_{2})
joining
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Denote by
i:X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
the natural embedding. We have the following equality in
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
:

[h_{i}]\cup[C]=[h_{i}]\cup(l_{1}+l_{2})=[pt],

hence the intersection between
h_{i}
and
C
is non empty.

∎

According to Lemma
3.5.3.2

iii)
for
(g_{1},g_{2})
general in
G^{2}
, the Gromov-Witten variety
\mathcal{W}_{g_{1},g_{2}}:=ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot h_{1})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(g_{2}X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))
is birational onto its image in
X^{3}
via the evaluation map. Then the map
ev_{3}:\mathcal{W}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\rightarrow X
induced by evaluating the third point is a degree
1
map of projective complex varieties. Furthermore, according to Lemma
3.5.3.2

iv)
its image
ev_{3}(\mathcal{W}_{w_{0}})
is the smooth variety
X
. According to Part
3.5.2

(b)
, we have :

\displaystyle\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{l_{1}+l_{2}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X(w_{n,1})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{n,i_{3}}\delta_{1,j_{3}}.

(c)

When
{\mathbf{d}}=d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}
, where
0\leq d_{1}\leq d_{2}
and
d_{2}\geq 2
.
Recall
X
is the variety parametrizing lines included in hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. A point in
X
corresponds to the inclusion of a line in a hyperplane of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Denote by
\pi:X\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
the projection induced by forgetting the hyperplane. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2}):=d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}\in E(X)
. Note that
\pi_{*}(d_{1},d_{2})=d_{1}\in E({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
. The forgetful morphism
\pi
induces a morphism
\Pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})
. Together with the evaluation morphisms
ev_{i}
, this induces a morphism :

\Pi\times ev:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}X^{3}.

We begin by studying when
\Pi\times ev
is surjective, before computing correlators of
X
.

Proposition 3.5.3.1
.

i)

If
{\mathbf{d}}=(0,d_{2})
, where
d_{2}\geq 2
, then
\Pi\times ev
is surjective ;

ii)

Suppose
{\mathbf{d}}=(1,d_{2})
, where
d_{2}\geq 2
. The morphism
\Pi\times ev
is surjective ;

iii)

Suppose
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2})
, where
d_{1}\geq 2
and
d_{2}\geq d_{1}
. Then
\Pi\times ev
is surjective.

iv)

Suppose
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2})
, where
d_{1}\geq 2
and
d_{2}\geq 2
. Then
ev^{\mathbf{d}}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X^{3}
is surjective.

v)

Suppose
{\mathbf{d}}=(1,d_{2})
, where
d_{2}\geq 2
. Then
ev_{3}(X_{1,w_{0}}^{l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}})=h\cdot X(w_{i_{1}+i_{2},1})
, where
h
is an element in
G
.

Lemma 3.5.3.3
.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow Z
be a morphism of projective irreducible varieties. Let
Z_{0}
be a subvariety of
Z
of codimension
1
in
Z
. Suppose the image of
f
contains
Z_{0}
. Furthermore, suppose the inverse image by
f
of
Z_{0}
is a strict subvariety of
Y
, i.e. suppose
f^{-1}(Z_{0})\subsetneq Y
. Then
f
is surjective.

Démonstration.

By contradiction, suppose
f
is not surjective. Then
f(Y)
has dimension smaller than
{\rm dim}f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))
. Indeed, we have
{\rm dim}f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))={\rm dim}Z_{0}={\rm dim}Z-1
, hence
{\rm dim}f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))\geq{\rm dim}f(Y)
since
f(Y)
is a strict closed subvariety of the irreducible variety
Z
.
Furthermore, note that the irreducible projective variety
f(Y)
contains the projective variety
f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))
. Hence
f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))
is a projective subvariety of the irreducible variety
f(Y)
of dimension larger than
f(Y)
; hence
f(Y)=f(f^{-1}(Z_{0}))
and
f(Y)
is contained in
Z_{0}
. Thus
Y=f^{-1}(Z_{0})
, which contradicts the fact that
f^{-1}(Z_{0})
is a strict closed subvariety of
Y
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5.3.1
.

We consider here
X
as a subvariety of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, where
X
is cut out from
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
by the relation "line included in hyperplane" in the following way. Consider a point
(x,y)
in
P^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. By the functorial definition of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
,
x
defines a line
L_{x}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and, if we identify
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
with its dual,
y
defines an hyperplane
H_{y}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Then
(x,y)
lies in
X
iff
L_{x}\subset H_{y}
. We call
curve
a projective variety of dimension
1
. We will denote by
pi_{1}
(respectively
\pi_{2}
) the first (respectively second) projection
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
.

i)

Consider a point
p
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
; denote by
L_{p}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
the line defined by
p
. Let
(p,y_{i})
be three points on
X
, where the point
y_{i}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
correspond to a hyperplane
H_{i}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
containing the line
L_{p}
. Our goal here is to construct a genus zero stable map
C\rightarrow X
of degree
d_{2}l_{2}
whose three marked points are the points
(p,y_{i})
. We first construct a curve of degree
l_{2}
joining the points
(p,y_{1})
and
(p,y_{2})
, before constructing a genus zero stable map of degree
2l_{2}
, and finally a genus zero stable map of degree
d_{2}l_{2}
joining all three points
(p,y_{i})
.

Suppose the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are distinct. Denote by
C
a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
joining the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
;
C
can be described as the subset of points in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
associated with hyperplanes
uH_{1}+vH_{2}
, where
[u:v]
runs over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Since the line
L_{p}
is included in
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, for all
[u:v]
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
it is also included in
uH_{1}+vH_{2}
. Hence the curve
C_{0}:=\{p\}\times C
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
induced by
C
is a curve of class
l_{2}
lying in
X
joining the points
(p,y_{1})
and
(p,y_{2})
. In the same way, if the points
y_{2}
and
y_{3}
are distinct, we may construct a rational curve
C^{\prime}\subset X
of class
l_{2}
joining the points
(p,y_{2})
and
(p,y_{3})
. Consider parametrizations
f_{i}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow C_{i}
, where we fix points
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that
f_{1}
sends
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
to
(p,y_{1})
and
(p,y_{2})
, and
f_{2}
sends
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
to
(p,y_{2})
and
(p,y_{3})
. Consider the tree of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with
3
marked points obtained by glueing
({\mathbb{P}}^{1},\{q_{1},q_{2}\})
with
({\mathbb{P}}^{1},\{q_{3},q_{4}\})
by identifying
q_{2}
and
q_{3}
. The morphisms
f_{i}
induce a genus zero, degree
2l_{2}
stable map
(f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X,\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{4}\})
joining all three points
(p,y_{i})
. Finally, notice that if two points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
come together, a degree
2l_{2}
stable map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
collapsing an irreducible component
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
to the point
(p,y_{1})
joins all three points
(p,y_{i})
.

Now suppose
d_{2}\geq 2
. Consider the degree
2l_{2}
stable map
(f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X,\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{4}\})
described here above. Now add an irreducible component
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, which we glue to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
at a non marked point, and send this irreducible component to
X
via a degree
(d_{2}-2)l_{2}
map. We obtain a degree
d_{2}l_{2}
stable map
({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X,\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{4}\})
joining all three points
(p,y_{i})
.

ii)

Let
L
be a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, let
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
be three points on
L
. Denote by
(p_{i},y_{i})
three points on
X
satisfying
\pi(p_{i},y_{i})=p_{i}
, i.e. the point
y_{i}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
corresponds to a hyperplane in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
containing the line
L_{i}
associated with the point
p_{i}
. Our goal here is to prove that there exists a genus zero stable map of degree
l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}
projecting to
L
and whose image contains the points
(p_{i},y_{i})
. Consider a genus zero stable map
({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow C\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},\{n_{1},n_{2}\})
of class
d_{2}l_{2}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
sending its marked points
n_{i}
onto the points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. If
d_{2}\geq 2
, we also consider the case where
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
is a genus zero stable map of class
d_{2}l_{2}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
sending its marked points onto the three points
y_{i}
. Since
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
is a smooth variety and the intersection
(L\times C)\cap X
is non empty,
(L\times C)\cap X
is of dimension at least
1
. If it has dimension
2
, then
L\times C
is contained in
X
. Then identify the line
L
with
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
; by considering the product of the closed immersion
L\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
with the map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, we obtain a genus zero stable map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow X
of degree
(l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2})
whose image contains all points
(p_{i},y_{i})
.

Now consider the case where the dimension of the intersection
(L\times C)\cap X
is
1
. Denote by
h_{1}
(respectively
h_{2}
) the pullbacks of the Cartier divisor
c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}(-1))
by the first (respectively second) projection. Note that we have the following equality in
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
:
[X]=h_{1}+h_{2}
. Furthermore, denote by
\delta
the degree of
C
; note that
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow C
is a degree
d_{2}/\delta
covering. If the variety
(L\times C)\cap X
has dimension
1
, it is a curve
C^{\prime}
in
X
satisfying the following equality in the Chow ring
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
:

\displaystyle[C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[L\times C]\cup[X]

\displaystyle=(l_{1}\boxtimes\delta l_{2})\cup(h_{1}+h_{2})

\displaystyle=l_{1}+\delta l_{2},

where we also denote by
l_{i}
the pushforward to
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
of the classes
l_{i}\in H_{*}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq A_{*}(X)
. Hence
C^{\prime}
is a curve of class
(l_{1}+\delta l_{2})
in
X
. Note that, since the points
(p_{i},y_{i})
belong to both
L\times C
and
X
, they lie on
C^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the image of
C^{\prime}
by the first projection
\pi_{1}:{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
is
L
. Indeed,
C^{\prime}
is a curve of class
(l_{1}+\delta l_{2})
contained in
L\times C
; hence its image by the projection
\pi_{1}
is a curve of class
(\pi_{1})_{*}(l_{1}+\delta l_{2})=1\in A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
contained in the irreducible degree one curve
L\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Hence
C^{\prime}
surjects onto
L
.

We now only have to prove that
C^{\prime}
can be descibed as the image of a stable map. Note that the curve
C^{\prime}
is either the union of two curves
C_{i}
satisfying
(\pi_{2})_{*}[C_{1}]+(\pi_{2})_{*}[C_{1}]=\delta=\pi_{2}(C^{\prime})
, or is an irreducible curve of class
(l_{1}+\delta l_{2})
. First consider the case where
C^{\prime}
is the union of two curves
C_{i}
, where the first curve
C_{1}
surjects onto
L
. Then
C_{2}
is obtained as
\{Q_{2}\}\times C
, where
Q_{2}
is a point on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Indeed,
C_{2}
is a curve satisfying
(\pi_{2})_{*}[C_{2}]=\pi_{2}(C_{2})
projecting to the irreducible degree
\delta
curve
C
, hence the restriction
{\pi_{2}}_{|C_{2}}
of the projection map to
C_{2}
is a degree
1
map onto
C
, hence is an isomorphism. In particular
C_{2}
is a curve of class
\delta l_{2}
. In the same way,
C_{1}
is a curve of class
l_{1}
projecting to
L
, hence is obtained as
L\times\{Q_{1}\}
, where
Q_{1}
is a point in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Then either the points
(p_{i},y_{i})
all lie on the irreducible rational curve
C_{2}
-which yields a rational curve of class
\delta l_{2}
containg all points
(p_{i},y_{i})
- or
C_{1}
contains one or two point
(p_{i},y_{i})
. We will consider here the case where
C_{1}
contains one point
(p_{i},y_{i})
, the same proof holds when
C_{1}
contains two points. If
C_{1}=L\times\{Q_{1}\}
contains a point
(p_{i},yi)
, for example the point
(p_{1},y_{1})
then
Q_{1}=y_{1}
and both
(p_{1},y_{1})
and
(p_{2},y_{1})
lie on
C_{1}
. We obtain a genus zero degree
l_{1}
stable map
f_{1}:=({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\simeq L\hookrightarrow X,\{(p_{1},y_{1}),(p_{2},y_{1})\})
. Denote by
f_{2}:=(C_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X,\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\})
a genus zero stable map of class
d_{2}l_{2}
sending its marked points
c_{i}
onto the points
(p_{2},y_{1})
and
(p_{2},y_{2})
and
(p_{2},y_{3})
; such a map exists according to
i)
. Now glue
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
C_{2}^{\prime}
together by identifying the points
(p_{2},y_{1})
and
c_{1}
; we obtain a genus zero stable map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
of class
(l_{1}+\delta l_{2})
joining all points
(p_{i},y_{i})
.

Finally consider the case where
C^{\prime}
is irreducible. Denote by
\pi_{1}:{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
the first projection. Since
C^{\prime}
is irreducible and
(\pi_{1})_{*}[C^{\prime}]=1=[\pi_{1}(C^{\prime})]
in
A_{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})
, the projection
(\pi_{1})_{|C^{\prime}}
of the curve
C^{\prime}
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
is a degree
1
map ; hence
C^{\prime}
is birational to its image
L
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
, hence
C^{\prime}
is an irreducible rational curve.

iii)

We proceed by iteration on
(d_{1},d_{2})
. Let us name
D(d_{1}-1,1)
the boundary locus of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1})
which is naturally isomorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},d_{1}-1)\times_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1},1)
, and by
D({\mathbf{d}}-(l_{1}+l_{2}),l_{1}+l_{2})
the boundary locus of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}-(l_{1}+l_{2}))
ismorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}-(l_{1}+l_{2}))\times_{X}\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,l_{1}+l_{2})
. Note that, since
D({\mathbf{d}}-(l_{1}+l_{2}),l_{1}+l_{2})
is a strict closed subvariety of the irredudible projective variety
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
, according to Lemma
3.5.3.3
it is enough to prove that

surjects into

in

. Denote by

and

the genus zero stable maps associated with

;

is a degree
(d_{1}-1)
map and

is a degree

map defining a line

intersecting the curve

at

Lemma
3.5.3.2
, ensure that there is a genus zero degree
({\mathbf{d}}-(l_{1}+l_{2}))
stable map
(f_{p}^{\prime}:C_{p}^{\prime}\rightarrow X,\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
such that

–

The image by
\Pi
of
f_{p}^{\prime}
is
f_{p}
;

–

The points
f_{p}^{\prime}(p_{1})
and

in

are projected to the points

in

.

If

note that according to Lemma

there exists a genus zero degree

stable map projecting to

there exists a genus zero degree

and the point

in

stable map

is

.

iv)

Let

,

. According to

, there exist genus zero degree

.

v)

Let
{\mathcal{W}}:=X_{1,w_{0}}^{l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}

associated with the

,

, and

. We obtain the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{2}}X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})\times X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}(ev_{3}^{d_{1}}({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}))\subset X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{3}^{d_{1}}({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime})\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{3}^{d_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Since according to
(c)ii)
the map
\Pi\times ev
is surjective, according to Chapter
1

{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\times_{({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1})^{3}}X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})\times X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rightarrow\pi^{-1}(ev_{3}^{d_{1}}({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}))
is also surjective. Hence by composition
ev_{3}^{\mathbf{d}}:{\mathcal{W}}\rightarrow\pi^{-1}(ev_{3}^{d_{1}}({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}))\cap\left(X(w_{i_{3},j_{3}})\right)
is also surjective. Note that
ev_{3}^{d_{1}}({\mathcal{W}}^{\prime})
is the locus of points
p
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
such that

–

;

–

a point in

lies on

;

–

w_{0}L_{i_{2}}=\{[0:\dots:0:x_{n-i_{2}+1}:\dots:x_{n}]\}
lies on

.

p

, where

is a permutation in

. We obtain

\displaystyle ev_{3}({\mathcal{W}})

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}(h\cdot L_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2})})

\displaystyle=h\cdot\pi^{-1}(L_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2})})

\displaystyle=h\cdot\{([x_{1}:\dots:x_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2})}:0\dots:0],[y_{1}:\dots:y_{n}])\in X\}

\displaystyle=h\cdot X(w_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2}),1}).

∎

Suppose

. According to Proposition
3.5.3.1

. Part

then implies the following identity.

\displaystyle\forall\>\>d_{2}\geq 2,\>\>\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}^{l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}}({X}_{1,w_{0}})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{h\cdot X(w_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2}),1})}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\min(n,i_{1}+i_{2}),1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right).

(3.15)

Consider the partial order
\geq
on
E(X)
defined by :

{\mathbf{d}}\geq{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}\>\mathrm{iff}\>{\mathbf{d}}-{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}\in E(X).

Note that classes

and

in

satisfy

iff

and
d_{2}\geq d_{2}^{\prime}
. According to Proposition
3.5.3.1

iv)
the image of
ev_{1}^{-1}(X(w_{i_{1},j_{1}})\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(w_{0}X(w_{i_{2},j_{2}}))
by the morphism
ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}}
evaluating the third marked point is
X
for
{\mathbf{d}}\geq 2(l_{1}+l_{2})
. Since

is smooth, Proposition
3.5.3.1

iii)
and Part

(b)
yield the following implication.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{d}}\geq(2l_{1}+2l_{2})\>\>\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\langle w_{i_{1},j_{1}},w_{i_{2},j_{2}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\rangle_{\mathbf{d}}=\chi\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{3},j_{3}}\right).

(3.16)

(d)

When

, where

.

3.6
Degree of
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
.

3.6.1
Definitions.

Consider a degree
{\mathbf{d}}
in the semi-group
E(X)\simeq\mathbb{N}^{2}
of effective classes of curves in
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. For
r\geq 0
, denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
the variety parametrizing degree
{\mathbf{d}}
genus zero stable maps to
X
.
The
evaluation morphism

ev_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow X
assigns to a map the image of its
i
-th marked point. For
X
a homogeneous variety, the evaluation morphism is flat.
Recall for
u
in
W^{P}
, we denote by
[{\mathcal{O}}_{u}]:=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X(u)}]
the associated Schubert class in the Grothendieck group
K_{\circ}(X)
of coherent sheaves. Since
X
is non singular, an element
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
in
K_{\circ}(X)
is assimilated with an element in the Grothendieck group
K^{\circ}(X)\simeq K_{\circ}(X)
of locally free sheaves on
X
. Let
u_{1}
,
\dots
,
u_{r}
be elements in the Weyl group
W
; the
correlator

\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{r}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}
associated with the elements
u_{i}
is defined by :

\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{r}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}:=\chi\left(ev_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{u_{1}})\cdot\>\dots\>\cdot ev_{r}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{u_{r}})\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,r}}(X,d)}]\right).

Note that for
X
a homogeneous variety, this correlator is equal to the sheaf Euler characteristic of the Gromov-Witten variety associated with the
u_{i}
(cf. for example
[
BM11
]
, Part 4.1) ; i.e. for
(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
general in
G^{r}
, we have :

\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{r}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}:=\chi\left({\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(u_{1}))\cap\dots\cap ev_{r}^{-1}(g_{r}\cdot X(u_{r}))}\right).

(3.17)

We consider the basis
({\mathcal{O}}_{u})_{u\in W^{P}}
of
K(X):=K^{\circ}(X)\simeq K_{\circ}(X)
. Let
t=\sum_{\alpha\in W^{P}}t^{\alpha}{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}
. Classes
l_{1}:=[X(w_{2,n})]
and
l_{2}:=[X(w_{1,n-1})]
of one dimensional Schubert varieties form a nef basis of
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Denote by
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
the Novikov variables associated with
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
. For a degree
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2}):=d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}
in
E(X)
, we write
Q^{\mathbf{d}}:=Q_{1}^{d_{1}}Q_{2}^{d_{2}}
.
Introduce the Gromov-Witten potential

\mathcal{G}(t):=\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)}\sum_{r=0}^{+\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbf{d}}}{r!}\langle\underbrace{t,\dots,t}_{r}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}},

We define a
deformed metric
by
({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha},{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta})=\delta^{\alpha}\delta^{\beta}\mathcal{G}(0)
, where
\delta^{\alpha}=\delta/\delta t^{\alpha}
.
The
genus zero big quantum product

{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\bullet{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}
of two basis elements of
K^{\circ}(X)
is defined by :

({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\bullet{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta},{\mathcal{O}}_{\gamma}):=\delta^{\alpha}\delta^{\beta}\delta^{\gamma}\mathcal{G}(t).

It is then extended by bilinearity over
K^{\circ}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Q}[[Q,t]]
and defines the
big quantum K-ring

(K^{\circ}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Q}[[Q,t]],\bullet)
. This ring is associative and commutative
[
Lee04
]
, and we recover the ring
K^{\circ}(X)
by taking the classical limit
Q\rightarrow 0
.
The restriction of the big quantum K-ring to
t=0
defines the
small
quantum K-ring
(QK_{s}(X),\star)
. Note that for
X
a homogeneous variety, the product of two basis elements in
QK_{s}(X)
is a polynomial in
Q

[
ACT18
]
.
The pairing defined by

{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha},{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta})={\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha,\beta}=({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha},{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta})

is extended to a
{\mathbb{Q}}[[Q,t]]
-bilinear pairing
\mathcal{G}
on
K(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}[[Q,t]]
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}
the inverse matrix. By definition, we have :

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{u}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}=\sum_{\gamma,w\in W^{P}}\delta^{u}\delta^{v}\delta^{\gamma}\mathcal{G}(0)\>\mathcal{G}^{\gamma,w}{\mathcal{O}}_{w}.

3.6.2
A combinatorial lemma.

We prove here a purely combinatorial lemma that will imply that the product of two elements in
K(X)
is a polynomial in the Novikov variable
Q
.

Lemma 3.6.2.1
.

Let
n
,
m
and
N
be positive integers.

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,p)\in\llbracket 0;n\rrbracket\times\llbracket 0;m\rrbracket,\\
k+p=N\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{k}\binom{m}{p}=\binom{n+m}{N}.

Hence for
0\leq N\leq n,m
:

\sum_{k=0}^{N}\binom{n}{k}\binom{m}{N-k}=\binom{m+n}{N},

and for
N\geq n
,
N\leq m
:

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\binom{m}{N-k}=\binom{m+n}{N}.

Démonstration.

(x+y)^{n}(x+y)^{m}=(x+y)^{n+m}.

∎

Lemma 3.6.2.2
.

Let
n
and
k
be positive integers, with
n\geq 2
.

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(-1)^{k}k=0.

Démonstration.

Let
f:x\rightarrow(x-1)^{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(-1)^{n-k}x^{k}
.
Then
f^{\prime}(1)=0
.
∎

Lemma 3.6.2.3
.

Let
(d,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, let
(d_{0},\delta_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that
\delta_{0}<\delta-1
or
d_{0}<d-1
. Then

\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\boldsymbol{\delta})=((d_{0},\delta_{0});\cdots;(d_{r},\delta_{r}))}(-1)^{r}=0,

where we consider the sum over all integers
r
and all possible decompositions of a pair
(d,\delta)
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
on a
(r+1)
-tuple
((d_{0},\delta_{0});\cdots;(d_{r},\delta_{r}))
satisfying
\sum_{i=0}^{r}d_{i}=d
,
\sum_{i=0}^{r}\delta_{i}=\delta
, and
d_{i}>0
or
\delta_{i}>0
for all
i>0
.

Démonstration.

Let
0\leq d_{0}<d
and
0\leq\delta_{0}<\delta-1
.
We set
S=\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\boldsymbol{\delta})=((d_{0},\delta_{0});\cdots;(d_{r},\delta_{r}))}(-1)^{r}
.
Let
r_{1}
be the elements
d_{i}
other than
d_{0}
that satisfy
d_{i}\neq 0
. Note that
1\leq r_{1}\leq d-d_{0}
. For a given
r_{1}
the number
r
varies from
r_{1}
to
\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}
. Denote by
r_{2}
the number of elements
\delta_{i}\neq 0
where
i\neq 0
; for given
r_{1}
and
r
,
r_{2}
varies from
\mathrm{max}(1,r-r_{1})
to
\mathrm{min}(\delta-\delta_{0},r)
. For given
r_{1}
,
r
and
r_{2}
, we have
\binom{r}{r_{1}}
possible choice of an
i
satisfying
d_{i}\neq 0
,
\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}-r+r_{1}}
possible choice of an
i
satisfying
\delta_{i}\neq 0
,
\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}
possible choice of the
d_{i}
, and
\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1}{r_{2}-1}
choice for the integers
\delta_{i}
. Hence :

S=\sum_{r_{1}=1}^{d-d_{0}}\sum_{r=r_{1}}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}\sum_{r_{2}=\mathrm{max}(1,r-r_{1})}^{\mathrm{min}(\delta-\delta_{0},r)}(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}-r+r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1}{r_{2}-1}.

Let us now consider
S(r_{1},r)=\sum_{r_{2}=\mathrm{max}(1,r-r_{1})}^{\mathrm{min}(\delta-\delta_{0},r)}(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}-r+r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1}{r_{2}-1}
; alors

S=\sum_{r_{1}=1}^{d-d_{0}}\sum_{r=r_{1}}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}S(r_{1},r).

If
r\geq\delta-\delta_{0}
.
Then according to Lemma
3.6.2.1
, if we set
k=r_{2}-1
,
n=\delta-\delta_{0}-1
,
m=r
,
N=r-1
, we obtain :

S(r,r)=(-1)^{r}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r}{r-1}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}.

Let
r>r_{1}
. Set
k=r_{2}-r+r_{1}
,
n=r_{1}
,
m=\delta-\delta_{0}-1
,
N=\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}
. Note that
\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}\leq r_{1}
and
\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}\leq\delta-\delta_{0}-1
. Then according to Lemma
3.6.2.1
we have

S(r_{1},r)=(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r_{1}}{\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}.

If
r\leq\delta-\delta_{0}
.
Set
k=r_{2}-1
,
n=\delta-\delta_{0}-1
,
m=r
,
N=r-1
. Then according to Lemma
3.6.2.1
we have

S(r,r)=(-1)^{r}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r}{r-1}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}.

Let
r_{1}<r
. Set
k=r_{2}-r+r_{1}
,
N=r_{1}
,
n=\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}-r
,
m=r-1
. Note that
\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}\geq r_{1}
et
r_{1}\leq r-1
. Then according to Lemma
3.6.2.1
we have

\displaystyle S(r_{1},r)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r_{1}}{r_{1}}\frac{r_{1}!(\delta-\delta_{0}-1)!}{(r-1)!(\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}-r)!}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1};

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r_{1}}{r-1}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1};

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1+r_{1}}{\delta-\delta_{0}-r+r_{1}}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}.

Note that
\delta-\delta_{0}\geq 2
. According to Lemma
3.6.2.2
,
\sum_{r=r_{1}+1}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}(-1)^{r}r\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}}{r-r_{1}}=(-1)^{r_{1}+1}
, which yields

\displaystyle\sum_{r=r_{1}+1}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}S(r_{1},r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=r_{1}+1}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}(-1)^{r}r\frac{(\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}-1)!}{r_{1}!(r-r_{1})!(\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}-r)!}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1};

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{1}+1}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}-1}{r_{1}-1}\binom{d-d_{0}-1}{r_{1}-1}.

Hence
\sum_{r=r_{1}}^{\delta-\delta_{0}+r_{1}}S(r_{1},r)=0
, hence
S=0
.

Finally, since this problem is symmetric relatively to
d
and
\delta
, the same result holds for
d_{0}<d-1
and
\delta_{0}<\delta
. We now only have left to consider the case whare
d_{0}=d
and
\delta_{0}<\delta-1
, or
\delta_{0}=\delta
and
d_{0}<d-1
. For example suppose
\delta_{0}=d
. Then, since
\delta_{0}<\delta-1

S=\sum_{r=1}^{\delta-\delta_{0}}(-1)^{r}\binom{\delta-\delta_{0}-1}{r-1}=-(1-1)^{\delta-\delta_{0}-1}=0.

∎

3.6.3
Geometric interpretation of elements in
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p}
for
k\neq p+1
.

From now on we fix elements
u
and
v
in
W^{P}
such that
X(u)=h_{i}
and
v=w_{k,p}
, where
k\neq p+1
. Recall
h_{1}:=X(w_{n-1,1})
and
h_{2}:=X(w_{n,2})
. We fix a degree
{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\}
. We suppose
{\mathbf{d}}\geq l_{1},l_{2}
, i.e. we suppose
{\mathbf{d}}=d_{1}l_{1}+d_{2}l_{2}
where
d_{1}\geq 1
or
d_{2}\geq 1
.

Let
{\mathbf{d}}={\mathbf{d}}_{0}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}
be a decomposition of
{\mathbf{d}}
into a sum of degrees
{\mathbf{d}}_{i}
. We denote by
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}
the following scheme

M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}=\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{0})\times_{X}\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{1})\times_{X}\dots\times_{X}\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}).

Note that
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}
is in bijection with the boundary stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
parametrizing genus zero stable maps
(f:C_{0}\cup C_{1}\dots\cup C_{k}\rightarrow X,\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
such that

–

The curve
\cup_{0\leq i\leq k}C_{i}
is a union of
k
genus zero quasi-stable curves
C_{i}
meeting in a point.

–

The first two marked points
p_{j}
lie on
C_{0}
and
p_{3}
lies on
C_{k}

–

For all
0\leq i\leq k
, the map
f_{|C_{i}}
represents the degree
{\mathbf{d}}_{i}
.

Furthermore, note that
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}
is equipped with evaluation maps
ev_{i}
, where
1\leq i\leq 3
. We call
boundary Gromov-Witten variety associated with
u
and
v
the inverse image

M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v):=ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u))\cap ev_{2}^{-1}(w_{0}X(v))\subset M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}.

Note that
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)
parametrizes stable maps
(f:C_{0}\cup C_{1}\dots\cup C_{k}\rightarrow X,\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
satisfying the three conditions here above and sending the first two marked points
p_{j}
within the varieties
X(u)
and
w_{0}X(v)
respectively. Finally, we denote by
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)
the projection of the boundary Gromov-Witten variety
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)
by the third evaluation morphism, i.e.

\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v):=ev_{3}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v))\subset X.

Note that
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)
is the set of points
x
in
X
such that there exists a stable map
(\varphi:C\to X,\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\})
associated with an element in
M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}
such that the image
\varphi(p_{1})
of the first marked point belongs to the Schubert variety
X(u)
, the image
\varphi(p_{2})
of the second marked point belongs to the opposite Schubert variety
w_{0}X(v)
and the image of the third marked point
\varphi(p_{3})
is the point
x
.

Lemma 3.6.3.1
.

i)

Let
f:M\rightarrow X
and
g_{i}:M^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be morphisms of schemes, where
i=1,2
. Let
Y
be a subvariety of
X
. Denote by
M\times_{X}M^{\prime}
the fiber product defined by
f
and
g_{1}
, and by
g:M\times_{X}M^{\prime}\rightarrow X
the morphism induced by
g_{2}
. Note that the morphim
f
induces a map
f^{\prime}:M\times_{X}Y\to X
, and the morphisms
g_{i}
induce maps
g_{i}^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\times_{X}Y\to X
. Then

g(M\times_{X}\times M^{\prime}\times_{X}Y)=g_{2}^{\prime}\left({g_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}(f^{\prime}(M\times_{X}Y))\right).

ii)

Let
k\geq 1
. Set
Y:=ev_{3}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}}(u,v))
.
Then
ev_{3}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v))=ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(Y)).

Démonstration.

i)

This is a direct consequence of
M\times_{X}\times M^{\prime}\times_{X}Y=(M\times_{X}Y)\times_{X}M^{\prime}.

ii)

Apply
i)
to
M:=M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}}
,
M^{\prime}=\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{k})
and
Y=X(u)
.

∎

Lemma 3.6.3.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{d}}
be a degree in
E(X)
. Then
ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u)))
is a Schubert variety, and for any
\alpha
in
K(X)
,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},\alpha\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}=\chi(\alpha\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u)))}])
.

Démonstration.

We consider the Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u))\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})
. Let us consider the morphism
ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}})\supset ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u))\rightarrow X
evaluating the second marked point. According to
[
BM11
]
Proposition 3.2., the general fiber of
ev_{1}
is an irreducible rational variety and the image
ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u)))
is a Schubert variety, and hence has rational singularities. Note that since
ev_{1}
and
ev_{2}
are
G
-equivariant, for any element
g_{1}
in
G
the variety
ev_{2}^{\mathbf{d}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}\cdot X(u)))=g_{1}\cdot ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(u)))
is also a Schubert variety. Finally Part
3.5.2

(a)
yields the result.
∎

Proposition 3.6.3.1
.

Let
u
be an element in
W^{P}
such that
X(u)
is a codimension
1
Schubert variety
h_{i}
, and
v=w_{k,p}
be an element in
W^{P}
satisfying
k\neq p+1
. Let
{\mathbf{d}}=\sum_{k=1}^{k}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}
be an element in
E(X)
, where
k>0
.

1.

The projected Gromov-Witten variety
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v):=ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}+\dots+{\mathbf{d}}_{r}}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v))
is the translate of a Schubert variety of
X
.

2.

\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{v},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}\dots\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{k}},{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}=\chi([{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)}],{\mathcal{I}}_{w}).

Démonstration.

1.

By iteration on
k
. First note that for
{\mathbf{d}}_{0}>0
according to Lemma
3.5.3.2
and Proposition
3.5.3.1
there exists an element
h
in
G
such that
ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}(u,v))=ev_{3}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{0})\times_{X^{2}}(X(u)\times w_{0}X(v))
is given by the translation by
h
of a Schubert variety. For
{\mathbf{d}}_{0}=0
, since
k\neq p+1
, according to Proposition
3.3.0.1

ev_{3}^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{0})\times_{X^{2}}(X(u)\times w_{0}X(v))=X(u)\cap w_{0}X(v)
is the translation by ane element
h
in
G
of a Schubert variety. Now suppose the induction hypothesis verified for all
i<k
. Then the projected Gromov-Witten variety
ev_{3}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}}(u,v))
is the translation by an element
h
in
G
of a Schubert variety
X(w)
. According to Lemma
3.6.3.1
we have

ev_{3}(M_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v))=ev_{2}(ev_{1}^{-1}(h\cdot X(w)))=h\cdot ev_{2}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(w))),

where we consider the maps
ev_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{0,2}}(X,{\mathbf{d}}_{k})\rightarrow X
,
i=1,2
. Finally according to
[
CMBP13
]
Proposition 3.2 the variety
ev_{2}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(w)))
is a Schubert variety.

2.

By iteration on
k
. According to Lemma
3.5.3.2
and Proposition
3.5.3.1
since
{\mathbf{d}}_{0}>l_{1},l_{2}
we have

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{v},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}=\chi\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\right),

where
h
is an element in
G
and
\beta
is an element in
W^{P}
such that
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}(u,v)=h\cdot X(\beta)
. Finally notice that
\chi([{\mathcal{O}}_{h\cdot X(\beta)}],{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}})=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{\beta},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}})
.

Let us now suppose the induction hypothesis satisfied for
i=k-1
. According to
i)
there exists
h
in
G
and
\beta
in
W^{P}
such that
\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)=h\cdot X(\beta)
. Hence the induction hypothesis yields

\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P},1\leq i\leq k-1}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{v},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}\dots\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{k-1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{k-1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}=\chi([{\mathcal{O}}_{h\cdot X(\beta)}],{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{k-1}})=\delta_{\beta,\alpha_{k-1}},

where the symbol
\delta_{\beta,\alpha_{k-1}}
is one if
\beta=\alpha_{k-1}
,
0
else.
We now only have to compute
\sum_{\alpha_{k-1}\in W^{P}}\delta_{\beta,\alpha_{k-1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{w},{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}
. Let
u^{\prime}
be the element in
W^{P}
such that
ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(\beta))=X(u^{\prime})
. We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{k-1}\in W^{P}}\delta_{\beta,\alpha_{k-1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{w},{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta},{\mathcal{I}}_{\beta}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(\beta)))}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{h\cdot ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(X(\beta)))}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{2}^{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(ev_{1}^{-1}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}}(u,v)))}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}(u,v)}]
according to Lemma
3.6.3.1
.

∎

Hence for
v=w_{k,p}
, where
k\neq p+1
, the product
h_{i}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{v}++Q_{1}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{l_{1}}(h_{i},v)}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{0,l_{1}}(h_{i},v)}]\right)

\displaystyle+Q_{2}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]-{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{0,l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]\right)

\displaystyle+Q_{1}Q_{2}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{l_{1},l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{l_{2},l_{1}}(h_{i},v)}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{0,l_{1}+l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]\right.

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\left.+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{0,l_{1},l_{2}}(h_{i},v)}]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{0,l_{2},l_{1}}(h_{i},v)}]\right)

3.6.4
Computing the degree of
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
.

Let
1\leq i_{2},j_{2}\leq n
where
i_{2}\neq j_{2}
. We note
v=w_{i_{2},j_{2}}
. We compute here the degree of
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}:={\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}}
. More precisely, we prove the following result.

Proposition 3.6.4.1
.

For any element
v
in
W^{P}
, the product
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}}
only includes terms of degree
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{1}Q_{2}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{2},j_{2}}={\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{v}+Q_{1}P_{l_{1}}(i,v)+Q_{2}P_{l_{2}}(i,v)+Q_{1}Q_{2}P_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(i,v),

where for
{\mathbf{d}}
in
E(X)
,
P_{\mathbf{d}}(i,v)
is an element in
K(X)
.

Our proof occurs in two times. We begin by rewriting the quantum product between two classes
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
in
K(X)
. We then note that most of these terms cancel together.

Rewriting the quantum product.
Denote by
g
the bilinear pairing on
K(X)
induced by the sheaf Euler characteristic ; we write
g_{\alpha,\beta}:=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta})
. We have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha,\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)}Q^{\mathbf{d}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha},{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}

\displaystyle=g_{\alpha,\beta}+\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\}}Q^{\mathbf{d}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha},O_{\beta}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}.

The identity
(g+A)^{-1}=(g({\rm id}+g^{-1}A))^{-1}=\sum(-1)^{k}(g^{-1}A)^{k}g^{-1}
yields the following equality :

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)}Q^{\mathbf{d}}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\}\\
{\mathbf{d}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathbf{d}}_{k}={\mathbf{d}}\end{subarray}}\>\>\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P}}g^{\alpha,\alpha_{1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},O_{\alpha_{2}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}g^{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}\dots g^{\alpha_{2k-2},\alpha_{2k-1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{2k-1}},O_{\alpha_{2k}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}g^{\alpha_{2k},\beta}

\displaystyle=g^{\alpha,\beta}+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}Q^{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P}}g^{\alpha,\alpha_{1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{2}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}\dots\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{k}},{\mathcal{I}}_{v}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}

since
{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha}
is the element in
K(X)
dual to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}
for the pairing
g
.
This implies for any elements
u
and
v
in
W^{P}

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\left(({\mathcal{O}}_{u}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v})\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in W^{P}}\sum_{{\mathbf{d}}\in E(X)}Q^{{\mathbf{d}}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{v},{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}}\left(g^{\alpha,v}+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}Q^{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P}}g^{\alpha,\alpha_{1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{2}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}\dots\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{k}},{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\},\>{\mathbf{d}}_{0}\in E(X)\end{subarray}}Q^{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}+{\mathbf{d}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in W^{P}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{u},{\mathcal{O}}_{v},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{0}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{1}}\dots\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha_{k}},{\mathcal{I}}_{w}\rangle_{{\mathbf{d}}_{k}}

Cancellation between different terms of
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
.

Lemma 3.6.4.1
.

Let
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
i\neq j
.

i)

For any
1\leq s,t\leq n
,
s\neq t
,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{I}}_{s,t}\rangle_{l_{1}}=\delta_{s,n}\delta_{t,j}(1-\delta_{j,n})+\delta_{j,n}\delta_{s,n-1}\delta_{t,n}
.

ii)

For any
1\leq s,t\leq n
,
s\neq t
,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{I}}_{s,t}\rangle_{l_{2}}=\delta_{s,i}\delta_{t,1}(1-\delta_{i,1})+\delta_{i,1}\delta_{t,2}\delta_{s,1}
.

iii)

For any
1\leq i_{2},j_{2}\leq n
,
i_{2}\neq j_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i_{1},j_{1}\leq n,\>i_{1}\neq j_{1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\rangle_{l_{1}}

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle_{l_{2}}

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{j,n})\delta_{i_{2},n}\delta_{j_{2},1}+\delta_{j,n}\delta_{i_{2},n-1}\delta_{j_{2},1}.

iv)

For any
1\leq i_{2},j_{2}\leq n
,
i_{2}\neq j_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i_{1},j_{1}\leq n,\>i_{1}\neq j_{1}}\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\rangle_{l_{2}}

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{i_{1},j_{1}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle_{l_{1}}

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{i,1})\delta_{i_{2},n}\delta_{j_{2},1}+\delta_{i,1}\delta_{i_{2},n}\delta_{j_{2},2}.

Lemma 3.6.4.2
.

.

We denote by

\displaystyle P_{{\mathbf{d}}}

\displaystyle(i,v)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}_{i}\in E(X)\setminus\{0\},{\mathbf{d}}_{0}\in E(X)\\

\displaystyle\in K(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{{\mathbf{d}}}[Q]

the term of
{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{i}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
of degree
{\mathbf{d}}
.
Let
{\mathbf{d}}
be a degree in
E(X)
such that
{\mathbf{d}}\geq 2l_{1}
or

. According to Lemma
3.6.4.2
most of the elements in

cancel. More precisely, for a given

satisfying

cancel together. Suppose

and

. The only terms left are then the ones associated with

,

,

and

. Using Proposition

we obtain

\displaystyle P_{{\mathbf{d}}}(u,v)

\displaystyle=Q^{{\mathbf{d}}}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}}(u,v))}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}-l_{1},l_{1}}(u,v))}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}-l_{2},l_{2}}(u,v))}]\right.

\displaystyle\>\>\left.-[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{d-l_{1}-l_{2},l_{1}+l_{2}}(u,v))}]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{d-l_{1}-l_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}(u,v))}]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{d-l_{1}-l_{2},l_{2},l_{1}}(u,v))}]\right),

which is equal to
0
since according to Lemma

for

and

the projected varieties are equal to
X
. Now suppose

and
d_{1}=0,1
. The only terms left in

are those associated with

and

. We obtain

\displaystyle=Q^{{\mathbf{d}}}\left([{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}}(i,v))}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{ev_{3}(\Gamma_{{\mathbf{d}}-l_{2},l_{2}}(i,v))}]\right),

which is equal to zero since

according to Lemma

. Furthermore by symmetry if

, since

we have

is equal to

.

Hence for any element

in

all terms of degree

and of degree

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h_{1}}\star{\mathcal{O}}_{k,p}
